Operating Systems 2
☆ Rimuru: Unified Memory Management for Modern LLM Serving via Elastic Expert Offloading
Zhiyuan Fang, Dingyang Li, Jianfeng Zhong, Bin Wang, Weixi Zhu, Cunshu Ni, Zicong Hong, Wuhui Chen, Zibin Zheng
Modern LLMs strain device memory through large model weights and attention states, particularly in resource-constrained scenarios. Existing systems separately provision memory for model weights and attention states, imposing fixed fixed boundaries that cannot adapt to the changing memory demands of workloads. Under heavy workloads, insufficient state capacity limits request concurrency and reduces throughput; under light workloads, idle memory in one region cannot be reallocated to another, wasting device memory. Meanwhile, modern LLM architectures introduce heterogeneous tensors with diverse layouts and lifetimes, further complicating memory management. These problems motivate unified memory management for heterogeneous tensors. However, a shared pool alone does not make memory directly reusable across tensors with different allocation granularities and execution layouts. This paper presents Rimuru, a modern LLM serving system that unifies memory management through elastic expert offloading. Rimuru establishes a shared page pool to enables elastic memory sharing across heterogeneous tensors. To accommodate different allocation granularities, Rimuru introduces memory views that allow each tensor type to interpret the shared memory space using its own page size and page table. Furthermore, Rimuru combines huge-page alignment with a multi-level page allocator that splits and merges pages, enabling cross-type capacity reuse. A page-table-aware fused MoE kernel directly accesses non-contiguous expert weights, avoiding copy overhead. Building on these mechanisms, Rimuru employs a workload-aware adjuster that dynamically reallocates pages across tensor types according to runtime demands. Implemented on top of vLLM, Rimuru remains compatible with modern LLMs. Evaluations show that Rimuru achieves up to 19.03x baseline throughput and reduces TTFT by 98.1% on average.
☆ PhOSv1: Efficient Scaling of Model Sleeping on a Single Server for Autoscaling with GPU-Context Deduplication
Tianle Sun, Xingda Wei, Rong Chen
Autoscaling is a key mechanism in model-as-a-service systems, where fast scale-up is essential because the scale-up time directly determines how long a model service remains overloaded, which in turn governs serving quality. The state-of-the-art approach, model sleeping, adopted by mainstream serving engines including vLLM and SGLang, achieves near-zero-cost scale-up by pausing pre-spawned engines on the GPU. However, sleeping is not a free lunch: each sleeping model must keep its GPU context resident, and this cost grows linearly with the number of models, degrading serving performance. Because providers must support many models, they face a dilemma between fast scale-up for all models and high service performance. In this paper, we show that a single server can keep many models asleep without paying this GPU context tax. The key observation is that the contexts of sleeping models are largely duplicated: the driver (CUDA) context can be shared across models, and their GPU kernel binaries overlap heavily due to common operators and libraries. PhOSv1 deduplicates this context by transparently delegating each engine's GPU context allocation to a central daemon through API remoting; therefore, a single server can keep far more models asleep than current engines allow. Our evaluation shows that PhOSv1 matches the scale-up time of vanilla vLLM sleep mode while substantially reducing the GPU memory footprint, improving end-to-end multi-model serving performance.

Hardware Architecture 14
☆ How Far Are We From On-Device VLM Inference? A Measurement Study on Edge NPUs
Huan Yang, Shiqi Jiang, Yu Mao, Chuanyue Xiong, Deyu Zhang
Diverse optimizations spanning model architecture, inference runtime, and hardware acceleration have been proposed for on-device vision-language model (VLM) deployment on edge NPUs. Still, cross-layer coordination of VLM execution on edge NPUs remains largely underexplored. This paper presents an empirical measurement study across six representative VLMs, three real-world edge platforms, and ten multimodal benchmarks. Our evaluation drills down from end-to-end application-level latency and thermal traces down to low-level kernel-scale execution behaviors, challenging several widely held community assumptions. First, softmax dominates attention costs; even lookup-table-based softmax optimizations cannot eliminate pipeline bubbles that severely underutilize NPU matrix units. Second, theoretical speedups from linear attention vanish under real-NPU execution due to prohibitive tensor-rearrangement overhead. Third, no hardware backend universally dominates autoregressive decoding: attention-tensor-shaped parallelism dictates NPU efficiency and shifts the CPU-NPU performance balance. We further find task-specific Sweet Resolutions for VLMs, which deliver meaningful accuracy gains at only modest latency overhead. Complementing these findings, we provide actionable cross-layer recommendations for hardware architects, runtime developers, and model designers.

☆ NORmal: Architecting 3D Ferroelectric NOR Flash as Main Memory -- From Co-Fabricated BEOL Arrays to 200B-Class Multi-Agent LLMs in a Smartphone
Zhuoran Li, Chen Gu, Zhuohang Bian, Haochen Yu, Yibo Zhao, Xuxiao Yang, Zhaori Cong, Jinshan Yue, Qing Luo, Di Geng, Ling Li, Youwei Zhuo
Smartphone agents increasingly work as teams, in which a large planner directs parallel workers across long sessions. Running such a team on the device requires 200B-class models and contexts that exceed phone DRAM many times over, yet existing tiers treat flash as storage: UFS starves per-token weight streaming, and mobile High-Bandwidth Flash (HBF) needs a redesigned SoC and cannot absorb continuous KV writes. We show that a non-volatile memory can join an unmodified LPDDR6 bus as a plain rank only if its access latency fits the DRAM timing window, a condition no NAND tier meets. We present NORmal, to our knowledge the first main-memory architecture for 3D ferroelectric NOR flash. We have fabricated and experimentally characterized back-end-of-line (BEOL) 3D FeNOR arrays that co-integrate dense 3-bit MLC and fast, high-endurance in-place-writable SLC with sub-7.5 ns sensing. NORmal turns these co-fabricated arrays into a byte-addressable, deterministic-timing load/store rank on an unmodified LPDDR6 interface through an under-array DRAM-emulation bridge, staggered bank reads with zero-cycle weight swizzling, and posted SLC writes. Across this rank and DRAM, NORmal places agent state by mutability and lifetime rather than by hotness, serving 200B-class models and persistent planner contexts in place while dynamically partitioning subchannels across agent phases. In serving-level simulation on production agent traces, NORmal is 8.9$\times$--28.0$\times$ faster than UFS offloading, within 0.4% of an unlimited-DRAM ideal, and 2.8$\times$--6.3$\times$ faster than HBF on sessions beyond 256K tokens, with 8.1$\times$--17.8$\times$ less memory energy per token.

☆ LAMP: LLM-Driven Analog Placement through Adaptive Tool Composition
Zecheng Xu, Guanhua Chen, Yumao Wu, Keren Zhu
Analog placement requires circuit-specific trade-offs among area, matching, routability, and electrical performance. Balancing these trade-offs through predefined objectives and fixed placement flows remains difficult. In this paper, we propose an analog placement framework in which a large language model (LLM) acts as a layout engineer through composable placement components. These components support constraint management, placement and legalization, and lightweight evaluation of routability and electrical performance. Using multimodal checkpoints of the initial and evolving design, the LLM selects and combines components to construct and revise its own placement flow. It also adapts the placement hierarchy by reopening modules or partitioning and regrouping devices, allowing local layouts to respond to global design needs. An experience skill provides reusable design knowledge to guide these decisions. Evaluation on two SMIC 180 nm circuits shows that the framework reduces placement area and improves most measured post-layout performance metrics.

☆ Caerus: Prefetching by Thinking Globally and Acting Locally MICRO 2026
Jacky Wong, Jacob Alexandrou, Yinting Huang, Luke Panayi, Lev Mukhanov, Sam Ainsworth, Paul Kelly
Modern processors rely heavily on data prefetching to bridge the growing performance gap between computation and memory accesses. In offset prefetching, prefetches are issued at an address delta relative to the most recently observed access, selecting deltas that have exhibited high coverage in the recent access-stream history. The Best-Offset Prefetcher (BOP) exemplifies this approach by selecting a single uniformly-issued global offset that delivers timely prefetches. However, its lack of context-sensitive accuracy tracking results in significantly increased DRAM traffic and sometimes disappointing performance. Conversely, Berti, the state-of-the-art offset prefetcher, achieves impressive prefetching performance and accuracy. However, its Program-Counter (PC)-local approach to extracting offsets limits its ability to capture global memory patterns, meaning there are many scenarios where simpler global offset prefetchers, such as BOP, outperform it. Observing the limitations of contemporary offset prefetchers, we present Caerus: an offset prefetcher that employs PC-local filtering on multiple non-overlapping globally derived offsets. Caerus extracts multiple offsets from the global memory trace and maximizes their combined coverage by training each offset only on accesses not already covered by previously learned offsets. The accuracy of each extracted offset is tracked per-PC based on whether it can cover future accesses for any PC. This allows Caerus to address complex delta patterns while maintaining high accuracy. In SPECspeed 2017, Caerus gains a geomean speedup of 8.3%, outperforming Berti by 2.0% while achieving low hardware and DRAM-traffic overhead.
comment: Accepted to MICRO 2026
☆ MRUD: Maximally Redundant Unsigned Digit Number Systems for High-Performance Arithmetic Units
Samaneh Rahimi, Ali Azarpeyvand
Signed digit redundant number systems such as Maximally Redundant Signed Digit (MRSD) enable carry-free addition but introduce significant overhead in multiplication due to the presence of both positive and negative bits. While Unsigned Digit Redundant (UDR) systems have shown promise in radix-2, their extension to higher radices remains unexplored. This work presents the first generalized Maximally Redundant Unsigned Digit (MRUD) number systems for high-radix arithmetic, extending UDR representations from radix-2 to radix-$2^h$ while preserving carry-free addition. The proposed framework includes digit encoding, digit extension, arithmetic operations (addition, subtraction, and multiplication), sign inversion, forward/reverse conversion algorithms, overflow/underflow detection, and hardware architectures for both signed and unsigned MRUD representations. Unlike MRSD, which is inherently limited to signed arithmetic, MRUD supports both signed and unsigned representations, similar to conventional binary systems. Evaluations across three multiplier modes---RRR (Redundant inputs, Redundant output), BBR (Binary inputs, Redundant output), and BBB (Binary inputs, Binary output)---as well as Multiply-Accumulate (MAC) units, using the FreePDK 45nm library, demonstrate that MRUD achieves up to 30\% lower delay, 26\% smaller area, and 87\% lower power than MRSD. In BBR mode, MRUD achieves up to 43\% delay reduction over the best conventional architecture for 32$\times$32 operands, while in Multiply-Accumulate (MAC) units, performing reverse conversion only once at the end yields up to 64\% higher throughput than DesignWare and over 150\% higher than Wallace. These results demonstrate that MRUD enables high-radix redundant arithmetic with significantly improved multiplier and MAC performance, making it an efficient alternative to conventional signed digit redundant number systems.
comment: 22 pages, 15 figures. Submitted to Integration, the VLSI Journal (Elsevier). Under review
☆ RV2GC: Reconstructing the Garbling Boundary in a RISC-V Garbled Processor
Zixiao Feng, Xiaolin Li, Chenglu Jin, Wei Yan, Yong Zhang, Hongwei Liu, Yong Liu, Qinfen Hao
Programmable garbled-circuit execution must balance the benefits of cross-program reuse against the cost of repeated work during secure execution. A complete garbled processor provides a fixed circuit for many programs, but its evaluator repeatedly visits processor-wide structures even when only a small part performs the current protected computation. We present RV2GC, which reconstructs the garbling boundary around a fixed library of RISC-V instruction circuits. Public scheduling and operand routing operate outside the circuit topologies; persistent label-encoded state connects independently invoked garbled instruction circuits and instruction-specific garbling-free paths through a common interface. A Conditional-execution Stack and protected commits support secret-dependent branches, bounded loops, and secret-indexed memory access under public scheduling, keeping instruction and memory-address sequences independent of private inputs. Across nine benchmarks, measured ARM2GC-to-RV2GC runtime ratios range from 12.81 to 4,043. In terms of circuit size, the largest listed instruction circuit has 3,386 gates, corresponding to 2.044% and 0.393% of the 256-byte and 4,096-byte Amber processors, respectively. Additional comparisons quantify schedule storage, communication, and the cost of secret-dependent execution.

☆ LLM Agent-Based Design of RISC-V CPUs with FPGA-in-the-Loop Evaluation ISCA
Leo Itoh, Junichiro Kadomoto
Long-run RTL simulation of CPU candidates can take hours, while short diagnostic runs may miss performance degradation that emerges later in execution. This evaluation cost can delay design decisions in LLM-agent-based CPU development. We present a development environment in which LLM agents implement CPU candidates on an FPGA and use on-board performance measurements to guide design decisions. We applied it to the human-supervised development of a single-cycle RV32I core into a three-wide out-of-order RV32IM core. The final design reaches 5.61 CoreMark/MHz at 130 MHz on a Artix-7 FPGA. For the same CPU candidate running 8,000 CoreMark iterations, FPGA evaluation, including implementation, was 9.6x faster than single-threaded RTL simulation. In one case, board measurements revealed long-run performance degradation in a candidate that improved in a short diagnostic run. The result guided candidate selection and led to RTL-based root-cause analysis and a fix.
comment: 5 pages, 6 figures, 2 tables. Submitted to IEEE ISCAS 2027. Code and data: https://github.com/kadomoto-lab/llm-cpu-fpga-loop
☆ APPlace: Co-Optimizing Device Locations and Access Points for Routing-Aware Analog Placement SP
Bingyang Liu, Xiaohan Gao, Souradip Poddar, David Z. Pan
Analog layout automation remains challenging in Analog/Mixed-Signal circuit design, because parasitics and mismatches introduced during layout strongly affect circuit performance. Placement is especially critical among the layout stages, as it constrains the routing solution space and the achievable post-layout performance. Existing analytical placement methods optimize device coordinates under center-based wirelength proxies and leave access point (AP) selection to routing. In analog circuits, however, a routing pin is an extended piece of terminal metal rather than a fixed point, and the best AP on it depends on where the other connected devices are placed, so a coordinate-only placement optimizes for connection points that the router will not use. We present APPlace, a routing-aware analog placement framework that co-optimizes device locations and AP selections during global placement, followed by a legalization stage that preserves the structure established by global placement. Experimental results on four real-world analog benchmarks show that APPlace achieves better overall post-layout performance than representative baselines at comparable layout area. Ablation studies further show that removing AP co-optimization causes up to a 13 dB CMRR loss among the OTA benchmarks and a 23% increase in comparator delay.
comment: Accepted to ASP-DAC 2027. 8 pages, 6 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/bingyang1132/applace
☆ Energy-Efficient Memristor Accelerator for Depression Detection with a CNN-LSTM Hybrid Model
Jiaqi Liu, Yun Yu, Fenglin Xi, Shenjian Zhang, Shenghao Liao, Wenze Zheng, Mingxuan Hu, Yangbin Chen, Xinhong Chen, Chun Zhao
Early screening of depression requires efficient analysis of audio and speech data, while conventional CPU and GPU platforms incur high latency and energy consumption for CNN-LSTM inference. To address this challenge, we propose a unified resistive RAM (ReRAM) processing-in-memory accelerator for a CNN-LSTM attention workload. The architecture integrates convolutional, recurrent, attention, and multi-layer perceptron (MLP) stages within a crossbar-based framework, reducing intermediate data movement between spatial and temporal branches. CNN operations are mapped through im2col-based vectorization, while LSTM gate computations are reformulated using concatenated input-state vectors and mapped onto ReRAM crossbars. We further provide module-level workload breakdown, parameter sensitivity analysis, and matched-workload context against representative ReRAM/PIM accelerators. Experimental results show 32.1 times and 9.3 times latency reduction over CPU and GPU baselines, respectively, with about 160 times and 140 times energy reduction. Simplified weight-perturbation tests characterize ASLF sensitivity in an untrained pipeline with synthetic spectrogram-shaped inputs. Task-level prediction accuracy and robustness remain to be evaluated.
☆ OPERA: OPamp Design by an Expert-Taught Reasoning Agent, from Specifications to Layout ISCA
Vishal Saxena, Soumyajit Mandal
Analog flows from specifications to a signed-off layout rarely run without manual iteration. Recent large language model (LLM) agents size circuits with optimizer loops over the device dimensions and stop at the schematic. We present OPERA (OPamp design by an Expert-taught Reasoning Agent), in which a general-purpose LLM agent designs CMOS opamps from specifications to post-layout sign-off with open-source tools. First, the designer teaches the agent the gm/ID method: map the specifications to transconductances and minimum currents, choose gm/ID per device role from characterized lookup tables, and size from equations, leaving a few knobs to search and an explainable design. Second, the agent learns the gLayout framework and taught layout practice, and writes a reusable layout generator that closes the layout and extraction loop at designer-approved gates. In SKY130, the agent designed a shared constant-gm bias and six opamps (5T, folded-cascode and telescopic OTAs; Miller, folded-cascode and telescopic two-stage) for fun >= 50 MHz, PM >= 60 degrees, SR 25 V/μs into 1 pF and a minimum dc gain of 30 to 60 dB. All six layouts are DRC-clean and LVS-matched, and their extracted netlists meet every target at all 15 PVT corners ( fun >= 53.7 MHz, PM >= 62.1 degrees, SR >= 27.9 V/μs). The benchmark netlists are released at https://github.com/AMPICLab/AMPICBench.

☆ Learning to Formulate: Agentic Hardware-Software Co-Design of AI Infrastructure through Progressive Optimization and Verification
Yi Ding, Li Shang
AI infrastructure, the chips and software that train and serve AI models, is designed in layers. Layers make the system manageable, but they also fix which choices an optimizer can consider. Gains that need the chip and its software to change together are excluded before optimization begins: the formulation leaves them out. Language-model agents make it possible to revisit these boundaries, but an agent's proposal cannot show by itself that a design is correct or optimal. We present Formulaic, a flow that divides the work. The agent writes the formulation, a mathematical solver optimizes within it, and verification and measurement check its assumptions and results. The agent refines the problem step by step, from the computation graph toward the chip. Each step is a template: a family of programs with the conditions under which they are correct. Wall relaxation searches the chip by pricing the hardware limits that bind the software. This paper takes a first step, through separate studies rather than one end-to-end run. On NVIDIA GB10, templates the agent wrote run complete Qwen3-8B layers $1.075\times$ faster than SGLang. In a simulated Ventus GPU study, joint design cuts the worst-scenario cycles of Qwen2.5-0.5B to 0.428 of the reference, against 0.934 for software alone. On a five-step GEMM refinement chain, reusing proofs makes checking $2.51\times$ faster.
☆ Energy-Efficient FPGA Implementation of an End-to-End Cosine-Domain CNN Using High-Level Synthesis
Sana Fatima, Ab Al-Hadi Ab Rahman, Ibrahim Yousef Alshareef, Farhad Khodadadi, Nuzhat Khan
The computational cost of conventional Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) limits their deployment in low-power devices for high-speed edge AI applications. This paper presents an end-to-end cosine-domain CNN (CD-CNN) architecture for energy-efficient FPGA inference using high-level synthesis (HLS). The proposed architecture performs intermediate feature extraction using real-valued DCT-domain representations, avoiding complex-valued arithmetic and intermediate inverse transforms between spectral layers. The design is implemented on a Xilinx Kintex UltraScale+ XCKU5P FPGA and evaluated against a fully spectral Fourier-domain CNN (FD-CNN) under consistent network and implementation constraints. Experiments on the MNIST and 94-class ASCII datasets show that the CD-CNN reduces normalized resource utilization and improves inference throughput on both datasets. Its energy efficiency reaches 112.91 frames/J for MNIST and 101.27 frames/J for ASCII, corresponding to improvements of $1.51\times$ and $2.76\times$, respectively, over the fully spectral FD-CNN architecture. Furthermore, compared to the previous GPU-based fully spectral CD-CNN implementation, the proposed end-to-end FPGA implementation improves energy efficiency by $12.96\times$. These results demonstrate the potential of real-valued spectral processing in the DCT domain for energy-efficient edge AI deployment.

☆ When Does Heterogeneity Pay Off? Understanding GPU-WSE System Complementarity
Ziyu Hu, Zhijing Ye, Jiamin Wang, Zhiqing Zhong, Zhendong Mi, Shaoyi Huang, Weijian Zheng, Rajkumar Kettimuthu, Xiaodong Yu
As AI accelerator architectures become increasingly diverse, integrating different architectures into a unified system is emerging as a new heterogeneous computing paradigm. GPUs and WSEs differ substantially in compute, memory, and communication organization, creating both architectural complementarity and additional overheads, yet how to translate this heterogeneity into practical performance gains remains unclear. To address this gap, we propose a hierarchical benchmarking methodology to characterize GPU--WSE heterogeneous execution and the conditions for exploiting complementarity, together with an analytical performance model that identifies when heterogeneity becomes a bottleneck and when it yields system-level performance gains.
☆ Tactus: Tactile & Pose Sensing Glove for Natural Dexterity Data Collection
Li Guan, Rachael Granberry, Sahar Aseeri, Sahar Rostami, Taylor Niehues, Sai Dhanooshi Marri, Mia Huang, Dingtian Zhang, Ziyi Kou, Jason Pursel, Eric Whitmire, Vatsal Mehta, Ashish Deshpande, Tristan Trutna, Reed Belcher, Emelia Black, Julian Blake, Roger Boldu, Sean Bonaventure, Einar Boyovich, Jordan Brennan, Elizabeth Casillas, David Christensen, Tyler Christensen, Nicholas Colonnese, Stuart Dealey, Gaige DeHaven, Katie Doran, Chris Doughty, Robert Drury, Silvia Espino, Gabriel Geyne, Beth Goodrich, Kyra Gould, Joe Grisso, Susana Guerrero, Andrew Gwozdziowski, Monica Ha, Ehsan Hajiesmaili, Kate Healy, Rose Hope, Michael Hurst, Claire Ikemiya, Daisy Isibor, Rick James, Yashasvi Joginapally, Samuel Johnson, Tyler Jorgensen, Kristy Jost, Ben Kamath, Slava Karulin, Alexander Keener, Woo Kim, Alison Krauskopf, Ankit Kumar, Joshua Lang, Lizzy Li, Cameron Lynch, Daniel Ma, Hector Martinon, Christina Matos, Keagan McCurdy, Lee Mincey, Stuart Nezlek, Tiffany Nguyen, James O'Hara, Jason Osborne, Jacky Pai, Roham Pardakhtim, David Perek, Alexandre Poulin, Nick Raines, Salwa Rasheed, Leah Resneck, Kevin Rhodes, Ergys Ristani, Douglas Roberts, Anik Sarker, Harrison Scarborough, Jonas Schmidtler, Brian Shieh, Benjamin Shuman, Garrett Simard, Forrest Smith, Udara Somarathna, Eliot Sosin, Jonathan Stewart, Kristine Strom, Adam Swett, Hal Talbot-Mantooth, Julia Tanner, Sai Sharan Thirunagari, Curtis Torrey, Simge Uzun, Eileen Wachs, Rylan Wasson, Henry Wei, Jesse White, Beau Woodbyrne, Antonio Woods, Noah Young, Chandler Yount, Joseph Zhang, Sean Keller
Collecting naturalistic human manipulation demonstrations with accurate contact force and hand pose is essential for robot dexterous manipulation policy and foundational world model learning - yet no existing wearable platform provides both calibrated fingertip force sensing and occlusion-resilient hand tracking in a form factor that preserves natural manipulation behavior. We present Tactus, a textile-integrated sensing glove that co-optimizes three design pillars: (1) a thin, conformal 3D-knit form factor (1.5 mm fingertip stackup) designed to minimize interference with natural manipulation, (2) strategically placed capacitive tactile sensors at manipulation-relevant regions informed by empirical contact studies, and (3) IMU-fused pose tracking that maintains accuracy through the hand-object occlusions where manipulation data is most valuable. Tactus includes a documented calibration pipeline for cross-glove consistency and per-session calibration for subject-dependent correction. To enable objective comparison, we introduce standardized benchmarking protocols for tactile force regression and hand pose estimation, and apply them to Tactus and two commercially available tactile gloves - to our knowledge the first quantitative cross-platform evaluation of this kind. Tactus resolves a minimum detectable force of 0.59 N at 95% confidence, 1.6 - 2.7x lower than the commercial baselines, and with 0.87 cross-participant consistency, while our pose tracking sustains 11.0 mm fingertip keypoint relative error during manipulation, when occlusion is heaviest. We show that these combined signals support object property estimation, slip detection, and representative recording of hand-object interaction - yielding demonstration data with the fidelity required for contact-aware manipulation policies and world models.

☆ crush: Extensible Proof Automation for Lean
Mike He, Dexin Zhang, Aarti Gupta
Lean's rich expressiveness supports higher-order functions, inductive datatypes, and user-defined abstractions, making it challenging to translate proof obligations into first-order Satisfiability Modulo Theory (SMT) queries and reconstruct kernel checked proofs. We present crush, an extensible SMT-backed proof automation framework for Lean. To bridge the gap between Lean's higher-order constructs and the solver's first-order logic, crush uses defunctionalization to encode function values and their applications. We formalize this translation for a supported higher-order fragment and prove its soundness. Moreover, crush provides metaprogramming interfaces for extending both Lean-to-SMT translation and the reconstruction of Lean proofs from solver certificates without modifying the translation pipeline. We implement crush as both a tactic and a backend for other third-party automation systems. We show that across four such use cases that target verification and general proof automation, crush discharges more obligations than the competing backends and reconstructs more kernel-checked proofs than the baselines.

☆ Countable Nondeterminism in Weighted Programming with an Application to Mechanized Foundations for Probabilistic Program Verification
Sebastian Körner, Oliver Emil Bøving, Christoph Matheja
Weighted programming is a framework by Batz et al. for describing mathematical models or optimization problems as imperative programs parametrized over semirings and then analyzing them with program verification techniques like weakest precondition calculi. Applications include reasoning about expected outcomes of probabilistic programs and competitive analysis of online algorithms. We extend weighted programming with (1) countable nondeterminism and (2) user-defined additive rewards, where programs are parametrized over modules instead of semirings. The resulting framework enables reasoning about expected resource consumption of probabilistic programs and about optimal scores of combinatorial games. A technical challenge is that wp-calculi for weighted programs with (1) and (2) over arbitrary modules can be unsound, because it is well-known that continuity properties commonly used in program semantics break in the presence of countable nondeterminism. We study conditions under which a wp-style transformer for reasoning about weighted programs is sound with respect to an operational ground truth defined in terms of weighted decision processes (WDPs) - transition systems that generalize Markov decision processes by drawing transition weights and state rewards from a module. Based on those conditions, we show that a range of concrete instances of the weighted programming framework enjoy sound weakest precondition reasoning. We demonstrate the usefulness of our wp-semantics through case studies for different modules. We mechanized our extended weighted programming framework and our main soundness results in the interactive theorem prover Lean. Instantiating these results for probabilistic programming yields, to the best of our knowledge, the first mechanized proof of a fixed point characterization of minimal expected rewards in Markov decision processes with countable nondeterminism.
☆ Automated Verification of Hamiltonian Simulation by Quantum Signal Processing
Min Lai, Yu-Fang Chen, Shih-Han Hung
We present, to the best of our knowledge, the first fully automated verification framework for QSP-based Hamiltonian simulation. Our framework exploits the structure of the simulation algorithm: Hamiltonians are given as weighted sums of Pauli strings, embedded into larger unitaries through block encodings, and transformed into time-evolution operators by quantum signal processing (QSP). Using a symbolic Pauli-sum semantics for ancilla-indexed circuit blocks, our verifier proves that the distinguished all-zero ancilla block is proportional to the target Hamiltonian and infers the subnormalization factor automatically. It then verifies the QSP layer by extracting the implemented polynomial transformation and certifying its approximation error. This structure-aware abstraction is essential for scalability. Composing our verified block encoding and QSP wrapper yields an estimated macro-expanded size of about 1.52 million gates. The verifier certifies the block encoding once and checks the QSP layer through its abstract interface, without expanding the oracle calls. Our implementation verifies block encodings of structured Pauli-sum Hamiltonians with up to 256 system qubits and verifies QSP Hamiltonian-simulation instances by extracting their implemented polynomials and checking the resulting approximation bounds. These results show that fully automated verification can handle common Hamiltonian-simulation circuits at substantial scale when it exploits their algorithmic structure.
☆ DualGuard: Comprehensive and Efficient Memory Safety across Sequential and Speculative Execution
Sixuan Dang, Quan Zhou, Kaiming Huang, Jianan Zhu, Anitha Gollamudi, Trent Jaeger, Danfeng Zhang
Although decades of research have yielded sophisticated mechanisms for enforcing memory safety, speculative execution can subvert these protections through transient memory operations. Conversely, defenses designed specifically for speculative safety, such as Speculative Load Hardening (SLH), cannot enforce memory safety during non-speculative execution. A naive combination of defenses from both domains raises two fundamental challenges: (1) which combination of otherwise fragmented defenses can ensure dual memory safety under speculative and non-speculative executions? (2) whether such combinations introduce redundant protections that incur unnecessary performance overhead? We present DualGuard, a unified framework for dual memory protection under non-speculative and speculative executions. DualGuard systematically analyzes memory behaviors, classifying them into four categories, and further identifies the minimal composition requirements for dual memory safety, which allow us to strategically combine existing mitigations and design new ones to avoid redundant or excessive protections. We implement DualGuard through a combination of static analysis and runtime enforcement within the LLVM infrastructure. Our evaluation demonstrates that DualGuard reduces overhead by 3%-72% compared to naive compositions of fragmented defenses. In some cases, it even incurs lower overhead than approaches that guarantee memory safety solely under either non-speculative or speculative execution.
Data Structures and Algorithms 38
☆ Uniform Capacitated Clustering with Few Extra Centers
Kangke Cheng, Guanlin Mo, Hu Ding
In uniform capacitated $(k,z)$-clustering, we open $k$ centers and assign every client to a center so that no center serves more than $U$ clients, minimizing the sum of the $z$-th powers of the assignment distances; $k$-median and $k$-means are the cases $z=1$ and $z=2$. We study algorithms that may open $k+\lfloor\varepsilon k\rfloor$ centers for a fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ and must respect the capacities exactly. In low dimensions, we give deterministic algorithms that are, except for $k$-median in the plane, the first polynomial-time algorithms with $(1+\varepsilon)k$ centers whose ratio is bounded independently of $\varepsilon$. In Euclidean space of any dimension, with clients of bounded doubling dimension, the ratio is $\mathcal{B}_z-(\mathcal{B}_z-1)\varepsilon+δ$ for every fixed $δ>0$, where $\mathcal{B}_z<3^z$ for $z>1$; this is $3-2\varepsilon+δ$ for $k$-median and $5-4\varepsilon+δ$ for $k$-means. In doubling metrics, the ratio is $3^z-(3^z-1)\varepsilon+δ$ when centers may share a location, and a constant otherwise, such as $\min\{7-4\varepsilon,\,3+2\ln(1/\varepsilon)\}+δ$ for $k$-median. In general metrics, deleting centers greedily gives ratio $3+2\ln(1/\varepsilon)+δ$ for $k$-median when centers may share a location and $7+2\ln(1/\varepsilon)+δ$ otherwise, improving the previous $O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log(1/\varepsilon))$. Our main tool is a rescue lemma. Splitting a near-optimal uncapacitated solution gives at most $2k-1$ blocks of at most $U$ clients, and giving a block a private center saves its share of the extra cost of following an optimal solution. Since a center of the optimal solution can be closed once all its blocks are rescued, $\lfloor\varepsilon k\rfloor$ extra centers save almost an $\varepsilon$-fraction of this extra cost instead of an $\varepsilon/2$-fraction.
☆ On Zeros and Algorithms for Disordered Systems II: The Antiferromagnetic Ising Model up to Kesten-Stigum

This is the entire regime in which the model is "replica symmetric," and is conjectured to be optimal for polynomial-time counting and sampling. To achieve our algorithmic result, we establish the absence of Fisher zeros for the partition function in a constant-width strip around the interval from $0$ up to the Kesten-Stigum threshold, with high probability over the randomness of the input graph. ☆ Approximating Minimum Spanning Trees in Metrics under Semi-Random Corruptions
Aditya Anand, Anupam Gupta, Rajesh Jayaram, Euiwoong Lee, Ali Vakilian
We study the problem of finding minimum spanning trees in semi-random corrupted metrics. In this problem proposed by Bateni, Dharangutte, Jayaram, and Wang (COLT'24), we start with a metric space $\mathcal{M} = (X, d)$. The adversary independently corrupts each pairwise distance with probability $p$. The algorithm only observes these possibly corrupted distances and must output a spanning tree whose expected cost (in the true metric) is multiplicatively close to the minimum spanning tree cost. Prior work showed that losing a factor of $Ω(\log n)$ is necessary, even if the algorithm may adaptively select $O(n/\log n)$ points and probe the true distances between them. However, no upper bounds were known. We study three regimes based on whether the adversary can only increase, decrease, or arbitrarily change the corrupted distances. We show the following: (1) In the increase-only setting, we give a polynomial-time $O(1)$-approximation algorithm for any constant $0 \le p < 1$. (2) In the decrease-only setting, we give an $O(\log n)$-approximation algorithm for any constant $0 \le p < 1$. Additionally, we show an $O(\log^4 n)$-approximation algorithm that runs in quasi-polynomial time $n^{O(\log n)}$, for any constant $p \leq 0.1$. Moreover, we show that a polynomial-time algorithm computing an $n^{1/3-\varepsilon}$-approximation is unlikely to exist. (3) In the general setting, where the adversary can both increase and decrease distances, we show an $O(\log n)$-approximation algorithm for every $0 \leq p < \frac{1}{4}$. Further, we show that no $o(n)$-approximation is possible for $\frac{1}{2} \leq p < 1$. Our algorithms answer the main open question in Bateni et al.'s work; moreover, the $O(\log n)$-approximations in the latter two settings are information-theoretically optimal.

☆ When Imputation Changes the Nearest Neighbor: Exact Certification of Similarity Retrieval for Incomplete Sensor Time Series
Jianru Shen
Sensor time series routinely reach similarity search with missing values, and the retrieved neighbours then depend on how the gaps were filled. We ask which retrieval conclusions hold for every admissible completion of an incomplete query. For a family of window-minimum distances that contains the $L_1$ distance as its separable end we give a pairwise certificate that one candidate is strictly closer than another, a guaranteed candidate set that contains every possible nearest neighbour, and a certificate that a nearest-neighbour label cannot change. The guarantees hold uniformly over the specified box and apply to the true query whenever it lies inside. At the separable end a closed form is known, but beyond it the worst-case gap need not be attained at a corner of the box. We prove a finite reduction to critical values, give an exact interval dynamic programme for the worst-case ranking gap, and a quadratic-time fast layer of valid bounds and feasible witnesses, supported by an exact criterion for when the feature-box relaxation is tight. On three traffic-sensor collections with real gap patterns, linear interpolation changes the nearest neighbour in 15 to 33 percent of the queries. At the full band the feature-box relaxation misses up to 14 percent of the exactly stable boundary pairs, whereas fixed-completion and Monte Carlo checks issue false certificates. The fast layer resolves about 90 percent of all tested boundary pairs without dynamic programming, and on paired network-scale queries the exact method is 82 to 110 times faster than the programme alone. Label certificates transfer to three public benchmarks, and the band width trades forecasting accuracy and certification coverage for lower imputation sensitivity, with dataset-dependent crossovers.

☆ Minimum Label $k$-Cut on Planar Graphs: Complexity and Color Structure
Yuanhao Wang, Wei Wang
Minimum Label $k$-Cut asks for a minimum-size set of colors such that deleting all edges of these colors leaves at least $k$ connected components. We study how the structure of the underlying graph and its color classes affects the complexity of this problem. Regarding fixed-parameter tractability with respect to $k$ on simple planar graphs, we prove that the problem is W[1]-hard, even when the underlying graph is a cactus graph and each color appears at most twice. Furthermore, for cactus graphs in which every color class is connected, we establish a complexity boundary in terms of the maximum monochromatic degree: when this degree is at most two, an optimal solution can be found in $O(Nk^2)$ time, where $N$ is the input size, with no restriction on color frequency or the number of blocks spanned by a color; allowing the maximum monochromatic degree to increase to three already makes the decision version NP-complete. Finally, we prove that the problem remains W[1]-hard parameterized by $k$ on fan graphs, even when every color class is an induced path. These results show that bounding color frequency does not suffice to eliminate parameterized hardness. They also establish a monochromatic-degree boundary for connected colors on cactus graphs and identify complexity barriers to extending this tractable case to outerplanar graphs.
☆ The Tight Competitive Ratio of Greedy for Online Steiner Forest
Iman Gholami, Ilya Hajiaghayi, MohammadTaghi Hajiaghayi, Peyman Jabbarzade
The online Steiner Forest problem is a fundamental network design problem in which terminal pairs arrive one at a time and an algorithm must irrevocably purchase edges connecting each pair. The natural greedy algorithm connects each terminal pair by a shortest path after making all previously purchased edges free. Awerbuch, Azar, and Bartal (SODA 1996) proved that this algorithm is $O(log^2 k)$-competitive and conjectured an $O(log k)$ bound, where $k$ is the number of terminal pairs. Bamas, Drygala, and Maggiori (SODA 2022) obtained an $O(\log \NumPairs\log\log \NumPairs)$ bound for greedy on a restricted class of inputs, leaving the general case open. In this paper, we resolve the competitive ratio of the greedy algorithm for online Steiner Forest, proving that it is tightly $Θ(log(k) log log(k))$. Specifically, we establish an $O(log(k) log log(k))$ upper bound for arbitrary instances and construct a matching $Ω(log(k) log log(k))$ lower bound. Our results refute the $O(log k)$ conjecture of Awerbuch, Azar, and Bartal and establish a tight $Θ(log log k)$ separation between the greedy algorithm and the best possible online algorithm for Steiner Forest.
☆ Nearly-Optimal Time-Sample Trade-off in Consensus Dynamics
Francesco d'Amore, Joel Rybicki, Nobutaka Shimizu, Takeharu Shiraga, Robin Vacus
How does the number of sampled opinions affect the time required to reach consensus in distributed consensus dynamics? We study the synchronous $h$-\textsf{Majority}, in which every node independently samples $h$ nodes uniformly at random with replacement in each round and adopts a most frequent sampled opinion, breaking ties uniformly at random. We show that $h$-\textsf{Majority} achieves the nearly-optimal trade-off between the sample size and the consensus time, among a broad class of consensus dynamics. In particular, we prove the following results. For every sample size $h \ge 3$ and for every initial configuration, we prove that the process reaches consensus with high probability within $\widetilde{O}\left(1+\min\left\{\frac{1}{\min\{h^2p_{\max},1\}},\frac{\sqrt{n}}{h}\right\}\right)$ rounds, where $n$ is the number of nodes and $p_{\max}$ is the largest initial fraction of nodes supporting any single opinion. This is the first bound on the consensus time for $h$-\textsf{Majority} that holds for every $h\ge3$ and every initial configuration. When the initially most popular opinion has a sufficiently large lead over every other opinion, we show that all nodes adopt this opinion with high probability within $O(\log n/\min\{h^2p_{\max},1\})$ rounds. We establish a matching lower bound up to polylogarithmic factors for any fixed update rule that treats all opinions symmetrically and that selects either a sampled opinion or the node's current opinion.
☆ The Value of Experiments in Assortment Optimization
Mohammadreza Ahmadnejadsaein, Amine Bennouna, Omar El Housni
Retailers can learn customer preferences by experimenting with different assortments. However, the cost of each experiment and the operational burden of repeatedly changing product offerings can make extensive experimentation impractical. We study how much a retailer can improve its final assortment decision using customer observations from one (or a very small number of) fixed experimental assortments. We consider the multinomial logit model, where product revenues are known but customer preferences are unknown. In this setting, we jointly design the experimental assortment and the rule for selecting a final assortment from observed customer choices to minimize worst-case regret: the expected revenue loss of the final assortment relative to an optimal assortment under the true preferences, maximized over possible customer preferences. We first characterize exactly the minimax regret and an optimal assortment when no experimentation is possible. We then show, surprisingly, that offering a single experimental assortment to just one customer can already substantially reduce regret, depending on how the products' revenues compare. We derive the exact minimax regret and an optimal experimental policy in this setting. When a single fixed experimental assortment is offered to many customers, we give a geometric characterization of the limiting minimax regret and construct an asymptotically optimal policy. Depending on product revenues, this single experiment can yield no improvement over no experimentation or nearly complete regret reduction. Finally, we extend our analysis to multiple experimental assortments chosen in advance, characterizing the limiting minimax regret, constructing asymptotically optimal policies, and identifying which collections of assortments allow worst-case regret to vanish as the number of customer observations grows.
☆ Exact Competitive Ratios of Index Rules for Scheduling with Obligatory Testing
Felix Buld
We revisit scheduling with obligatory testing, where each job consists of two operations: a test with an initially known time and an operation whose processing time is revealed upon completion of the job's test. We develop an iterative competitive-ratio-preserving instance-reduction method that enables us to compute exact competitive ratios directly on highly structured instances for various index rules. We further leverage this method to obtain near-tight bounds for a simple randomized algorithm on instances with uniform testing times.
☆ Zero-freeness for Random Regular Graphs up to the Replica Symmetry Breaking Threshold
Zejia Chen, Chunyang Wang, Xinyuan Zhang
We study antiferromagnetic spin systems on random regular graphs through the lens of the phase transitions characterized by the complex zeros of the partition function. Our motivating examples include the hardcore model and the antiferromagnetic Potts model (including proper $q$-colorings at $β=0$). For both models on random $d$-regular graphs, we establish, with high probability over the graph, zero-free regions throughout the interior of the replica-symmetric regime. Asymptotically, this regime extends up to tree occupation density $$α< α_{\mathrm{RSB}}(d) = (2\log d - 2\log\log d + 2 - 2\log 2 + o_d(1))/d$$ for the hardcore model and up to degree $$d < d_{\mathrm{RSB}}(q) = (2q-1)\log q - 2\log 2 + o_q(1)$$ for proper $q$-colorings. Our algorithmic guarantees significantly improve upon prior work and extend beyond the parameter ranges reached by previously known search algorithms. To establish our results, we first develop reinforced small subgraph conditioning, which resolves short-cycle fluctuations in the complex plane by lifting classical small subgraph conditioning on random regular graphs to random holomorphic functions. Second, we overcome the standard second-moment barrier caused by the exponential blowup of the bare second moment inside the replica-symmetric phase, by showing that replica symmetry implies a nonpositive Bethe functional and upgrading planted overlap concentration to an exponentially small bound, which enables a typical-graph truncation that controls complex second moments up to the replica symmetry breaking threshold.
☆ Half-Optimal Induced Matchings in Random Graphs: Online and Low-Degree Algorithms
Abhishek Dhawan, Eren C. Kızıldağ, Neeladri Maitra, Cheng Mao, Timothy L. H. Wee
For many optimization problems on random graphs, most prominently the largest independent set problem, natural algorithms reach only half the optimum, and such factor-two gaps are known to be sharp within broad algorithmic classes. We prove such a gap for finding large induced matchings in Erdős-Rényi random graphs. In the worst case, requiring a matching to be induced turns a polynomial-time solvable problem into an NP-hard one; our results reveal an average-case counterpart. In $G(n,p)$ with $C/n\le p\le 1-κ$, the largest induced matching has size $(1+o(1))L$ with high probability, where $L=\log_{1/(1-p)}(np)$. A simple online greedy algorithm finds an induced matching of size $(1/2-ε)L$, while no online algorithm, even with adaptive inspection order and unbounded computation, finds one of size $(1/2+ε)L$ except with probability $\exp(-Ω(L\log(np)))$. In the sparse regime $p=d/n$, low-degree polynomial algorithms exhibit the same sharp threshold. Our guarantees rest on a maximality principle: with high probability, every maximal induced matching is half-optimal. Greedy outputs a maximal induced matching and our low-degree algorithm a near-maximal one. We bypass the delicate coupling arguments classically used to analyze greedy since Karp. For low-degree hardness, we adapt Wein's overlap gap argument, with forbidden structures that track overlaps between endpoint sets rather than edge sets, since a new edge may join two previously used endpoints and thus encounter no fresh randomness. For online hardness, we give a new and significantly simpler argument that avoids the correlated instances of prior OGP-based proofs: at a suitable stopping time, all pairs involving uninspected vertices remain unrevealed, so a direct first-moment bound suffices.

☆ Leveraging Recourse and Size Bounds for OnlineKnapsack with Removable Items
Dimitris Fotakis, Laurent Gourvès, Aris Pagourtzis, Panagiotis Patsilinakos
In the online unweighted knapsack problem, items arrive sequentially and must be accepted or rejected immediately for a bounded-capacity knapsack, aiming to maximize the total size of accepted items. When decisions are irrevocable, no algorithm achieves a non-trivial competitive ratio. An essential relaxation that circumvents this impossibility is to allow previously accepted items to be removed from the knapsack (a.k.a. removal). In this work, we investigate possible further improvements on the competitive ratio with removal by bringing together two other previously proposed model extensions: knowledge of an upper bound on the item sizes (a.k.a. bound awareness) and ability to reinsert previously rejected items(a.k.a.recourse). Our contributions are twofold. First, we establish close upper and lower bounds on the competitive ratio under bound awareness, removal and recourse. We show that significant improvements on the best known competitive ratio are possible when the upper bound on the item sizes is not too restrictive and only few uses of recourse are available. Second, we introduce the notion of adaptive online algorithms which do not require to know any upper bound on the item sizes but still achieve performance guarantees comparable to the ones obtained under the bound awareness model.
☆ Layering and reverse extension for weighted $k$-path vertex cover
Jiajing Wu, An Zhang, Shiming Li, Guohui Lin
We study the weighted $k$-path vertex cover problem for any fixed integer $k \ge 2$, in which a vertex non-negatively weighted simple graph $G = (V, E)$ is given and one wants to find a minimum-weight vertex subset $C \subseteq V$ such that every simple path on $k$ vertices in $G$ contains at least one vertex of $C$. Comput., 1982] and a $(k - \frac 12)$-approximation algorithm of Brüstle et al.~[IWOCA 2021]. When $k = 3$ and $k = 4$, these ratios are $2$ and $3$, respectively, matching the state-of-the-art approximation ratios for the corresponding special cases.

☆ Retrieval Sequencing in a Compact Storage System with Side Access
Yağmur Gül
In a compact storage system with side access, bins are arranged in adjacent vertical stacks. In each retrieval cycle, bins blocking access are lifted to create a temporary tunnel through which a robot extracts one requested bin from the side. The lifted bins are then lowered again, and the energy expenditure of the cycle equals the number of lifted bins. Given a pick list known in advance, we seek a retrieval order that minimizes total energy expenditure. Finally, we quantify the additional energy savings from batching, which allows several requested bins to be retrieved in one cycle.

☆ Counting approximate breakpoint medians of random genomes
Poly H. da Silva, Arash Jamshidpey, David Sankoff
Unsigned linear unichromosomal genomes with genes labeled 1, . . . , n are represented by permutations of these labels, whose adjacencies are the unordered pairs of genes occurring consecutively. The breakpoint distance counts the adjacencies of one genome that are absent from the other, and a breakpoint median minimizes the total breakpoint distance to a given set of genomes. Deciding whether a permutation is a median often requires a minimization over the whole symmetric group, which makes the median set resistant to exact counting. We replace this global condition by a local one: a c-approximate median is a genome having at most c adjacencies absent from all the input genomes. For a fixed genome we introduce its approximate median inverse, the set of ordered k-tuples of genomes ad- mitting it as a c-approximate median, and we construct and count this set explicitly. For k genomes chosen uniformly and independently at random, this gives the probability that a given genome is a c-approximate median and the expected number of such approximate medians. The relaxation is not only technical: approximate medians and near-optimal genomes co- incide up to a shift of the tolerance that remains bounded in probability as the number of genes grows. Finally, we derive an explicit upper bound on the probability that a given genome is an exact median, and on the expected number of exact breakpoint medians.

☆ Accumulating Pruning Constraints: New Exact Polynomial-Space Algorithms for Max 2-SAT
Konstantin Kutzkov
We present deterministic polynomial-space algorithms that solve Max 2-SAT on $n$ variables exactly in time $\Ostar(2^{(1-γ)n})$, for a fixed $γ>0$, on several classes of instances whose clause density may grow with $n$. Our main tool extends the classic DPLL branching algorithm. When the search gives a variable its less promising value, comparing the two values of the variable yields a pruning constraint that is satisfied by some optimal assignment. Accumulating these constraints along each branch prunes all but an exponentially small fraction of the assignments. The classes are formulas with many biased variables, semirandom 2-CNF formulas, and Max-Cut on regular expander graphs and on graphs generated by a stochastic block model. In a semirandom formula, the pairs of variables that may share a clause are arbitrary, and the clauses on these pairs are random. For semirandom formulas and block-model graphs, the algorithms are still exact on every input, and the time bound holds with high probability over the input. To our knowledge, these are the first polynomial-space algorithms with such a running time for nontrivial classes of dense Max 2-SAT instances. Beyond these classes, the pruning constraints can be added to any branching algorithm for Max 2-SAT, and we expect them to be of practical relevance for general formulas, in particular for sparse ones, where each constraint involves only a few variables.

☆ An Exact Parallel Dense Gauss-Seidel Algorithm with Trade-Offs between Parallel Execution Time and Preprocessing Time

☆ Communication-Oblivious Permutation and Sorting
Qi Dong, Yilei Wang, Ke Yi
With the help of secure hardware such as trusted execution environments (TEEs), private data can be uploaded and computed in untrusted cloud clusters, so that an adversary controlling the cloud can only see the data in encrypted form. However, the communication pattern between the processors may still reveal sensitive information about the data, so it is important to develop parallel algorithms in the BSP model whose communication pattern is independent of the input data, a property known as \textit{communication obliviousness}. In this paper, we study such algorithms for three fundamental problems: permutation, sorting, and 0-1 sorting. For the first two problems, we prove a tight (up to lower-order terms) communication complexity of $(2-2/p)n\pm o(n)$. Here, $p$ is the number of processors and $n$ is the input size, assuming $2\le p$ and $p=o(n^{1/3})$. Our lower bound holds regardless of the number of rounds and local memory size, while for balanced permutation and sorting the upper bound is achieved in a constant number of rounds with load at most $n/p+o(n/p)$. For 0-1 sorting, we also derive a tight communication complexity of $C_{\mathrm{cut}}(p) \cdot n \pm o(n)$, where $C_{\mathrm{cut}}(p)$ is the optimal value of a linear program parameterized by $p$. Although there appears to be no closed-form for $C_{\mathrm{cut}}(p)$, we show that it is strictly smaller than $2-2/p$ for all $p\ge 3$, thus separating 0-1 sorting from general sorting in terms of their communication complexity.
☆ From Trees to Binary Trees: Approximation Algorithms and a PTAS
Fabian Weisbrodt, Florian Werner
Kellerhals, Krebs, Nichterlein, and Schmid introduced the optimization problem Tree in Binary Tree (TBT), where, given a tree $T$, the task is to find a binary tree $S^*$ with $V(T)=V(S^*)$ that minimizes $\sum_{uv\in E(T)} \operatorname{dist}_{S^*}(u,v)$. They also introduced the variant Tree in Binary Tree With Steiner Nodes (TBT-SN), where the condition $V(T)=V(S^*)$ is relaxed to $V(T)\subseteq V(S^*)$. We give a simple linear-time $\frac{8}{3}$-approximation algorithm for TBT and a polynomial-time approximation scheme (PTAS) for TBT-SN. Our main building idea in both algorithms is to iteratively consider subtrees of the tree $T$ which contain all vertices below some vertex $v$ and construct a solution where $v$ has degree 1. For the PTAS, we cut $T$ into smaller trees, solve these instances, modify the obtained solutions, and combine them to achieve a solution for the original instance.

☆ Four-Coloring Planar Graphs in Linear Time
Yuta Inoue, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Atsuyuki Miyashita, Bojan Mohar, Carsten Thomassen, Mikkel Thorup
The famous Four Color Theorem (4CT) states that any planar graph can be colored with four colors, but how do we find a 4-coloring? In our previous paper [FOCS'26], we presented a simple combinatorial algorithm to 4-color planar graphs in $O(n\log n)$ time. We improve it here to $O(n)$ time, which is optimal. The algorithm from our previous paper is based on proving that every planar triangulation has a linear number of reducible configurations or obstructing cycles. The bottleneck for the algorithm is that we need $O(\log n)$ Kempe changes to extend a recursive 4-coloring to the reducible configurations. Each Kempe change can involve a constant fraction of the graph and takes linear time. Here we show that all $O(\log n)$ Kempe changes can be identified and implemented in $O(n)$ time. The idea is to spend $O(n)$ time to preprocess the graph so that we can identify and simulate each good Kempe change in $O(n/\log n)$ amortized time. Thus, we implement all $O(\log n)$ Kempe changes in $O(n)$ total time. Based on this, we implement the whole 4-coloring in linear time. We note that creating such a representation for fast identification and simulation of the right Kempe changes is a challenge of data structures, unlike the previous paper that builds on a new graph theoretic understanding of reducible configurations.
☆ Parameterized Approximation of Max k-Weight CSP: A Dichotomy for Boolean Languages
Rudranarayan Kar, Souvik Saha, Abhishek Sahu
We study \textsc{Max $k$-Weight CSP}: maximize the satisfied weight of Boolean constraints with nonnegative rational weights by setting at most $k$ variables to $1$. Each constraint uses at most $d$ distinct variables and a relation from a finite Boolean language $Γ$. This includes \textsc{Partial Vertex Cover}, \textsc{Densest $k$-Subgraph}, bounded-frequency \textsc{Maximum Coverage}, and cardinality-constrained \textsc{Max-SAT}. We completely classify the fixed finite languages admitting an efficient parameterized approximation scheme (EPAS) with parameter $k+d$. Only nonempty relations excluding the all-zero tuple matter. For such a relation, a coordinate is a \emph{unit witness} if the tuple with a single $1$ in that coordinate is accepted, and a \emph{control} otherwise. A relation is \emph{Direct} if setting any selected control to $0$, with all other coordinates unchanged, preserves satisfaction. It is \emph{Rooted} if every satisfying tuple has a selected unit witness such that keeping it, setting all other unit witnesses to $0$, and setting any subset of selected controls to $0$ preserves satisfaction. If all such relations are Direct, or all are Rooted, we give a deterministic $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation for every rational $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ in time $2^{O(d+k\log(1+dk/\varepsilon))}\ell_{\mathrm{in}}^{O(1)}$, where $\ell_{\mathrm{in}}$ is the total input length. The algorithms are uniform in $d$ and give an EPAS parameterized by $k+d$ whenever relation membership is polynomial-time decidable. If neither condition holds, no EPAS exists unless $\mathsf{FPT}=\mathsf{W[1]}$, even for unweighted instances where no two constraints involve the same set of variables.
☆ Constant-Factor Approximation for Non-Splitting Coflow Scheduling in Heterogeneous Parallel Networks via Iterative Relaxation
Chi-Yeh Chen
Coflows capture the collective communication requirements of data-parallel applications, and scheduling them well is essential to the performance of modern data centers. We study non-splitting coflow scheduling in a heterogeneous parallel network whose $m$ network cores may be Electronic Packet Switches (EPS), not-all-stop Optical Circuit Switches (OCS), or all-stop OCS, where every flow must be transmitted entirely through a single core. Previous approximation algorithms for this setting have guarantees that grow linearly with the number of cores $m$ or with the maximum flow degree $τ$ of a port, and therefore become weak as data centers deploy more cores. The source of this dependence is the rounding step: rounding each flow to the core holding its largest fractional value can inflate the load of a port by a factor of $m$. We replace this step with an iterative relaxation rounding scheme that handles the input-port and output-port capacity constraints simultaneously. For minimizing the makespan, this yields approximation ratios of $\min\{τ, 3\}$, $2\min\{τ, 3\}$, and $4\min\{τ, 3\}$ for pure EPS, pure not-all-stop OCS, and pure all-stop OCS networks, respectively. For minimizing the total weighted coflow completion time, we obtain approximation ratios of $8\min\{τ, 4\}$, $16\min\{τ, 4\}$, and $32\min\{τ, 4\}$ for the same three architectures. In a hybrid network, the guarantee is governed by the weakest architecture present, so the overall ratios are at most $4\min\{τ,3\}\le 12$ and $32\min\{τ,4\}\le 128$, respectively. Unlike all previous guarantees for non-splitting coflow scheduling in heterogeneous parallel networks, these bounds are absolute constants that do not grow with the number of cores $m$ or with the flow degree $τ$. We also show that the integrality gap of the underlying linear program is at least $2$.
☆ Substring Complexity in BWT-Runs Bounded Space
Johannes Fischer
Given a string $T[1..n]$, its substring complexity is defined as $δ=\max_k d_k/k$, where $d_k$ is the number of its distinct substrings of length $k$ in $T$. We show how to compute it in $O(n+r\log r)$ time, using only $O(r)$ words of space, where $r$ denotes the number of runs in the Burrows-Wheeler transform.
☆ Tight Asymptotic Bounds on the Size of Order Graphs
Ruben Becker, Adrián Gómez-Brandón, Gonzalo Navarro, Nicola Prezza
Order graphs were introduced by Arroyuelo et al.\ (TODS 2024) as a generalization of the ring index, to support worst-case optimal joins on relations of arity $d$ while storing as few columns as possible: the index stores one column per edge, so its size is proportional to the number of edges of an order graph. It was known that the minimum number of edges lies between $Ω(2^d\sqrt d)$ and $O(2^d d)$, and the lower bound had been claimed to be $42$ for $d=6$. We first exhibit an order graph with only $40$ edges for $d=6$, and show that the proof of that lower bound holds only for unions of cycles, whereas order graphs can be smaller than the smallest set of cycles. We give a corrected statement and proof that holds for every order graph: there must be at least $\max_m\binom dm\lceil m/2\rceil$ edges. This bound is $40$ for $d=6$, so our $40$-edge graph is optimal. We then settle the asymptotic question: the minimum size is $Θ(2^d\sqrt d)$. This is obtained via an explicit construction, based on symmetric chain decompositions of the Boolean lattice, with at most $(d-1)\binom{d}{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}+2^d$ edges. The upper bound is within a factor $4+O(d^{-1/2})$ of the lower bound, and both numerically and asymptotically better than trie switching.
☆ Building On Learning-Augmented Online Sorting
Wyatt Olson
Online sorting is a problem that has gained attention recently, and is a natural translation of sorting into an online setting. The problem at hand is to place elements of an online sequence immediately and irrevocably with the goal of producing an array that is as close to sorted as possible upon inserting all elements. Recent work by Bercea et al. [ESA 2026] introduced the incorporation of learned predictions into the online sorting domain. They show lower bounds for the consistency and robustness of any algorithm, including randomization, then provide a deterministic algorithm that is $O(n/r)$-consistent and $O(r)$-robust, which are tight bounds. However, this algorithm requires prior knowledge of the optimal offline cost of the full input sequence before all elements have been revealed. We resolve this by providing an algorithm with matching bounds that does not require knowledge of the optimal cost, then extend these results to the more general problem of online metric TSP, in line with the prior work.

♻ ☆ A quantum central path algorithm for linear optimization
Brandon Augustino, Jiaqi Leng, Jialu Bao, Giacomo Nannicini, Tamás Terlaky, Xiaodi Wu
We propose a novel quantum algorithm for solving linear optimization problems by quantum-mechanical simulation of the central path. While interior point methods follow the central path with an iterative algorithm that works with successive linearizations of the perturbed KKT conditions, we perform a simulation working directly with the nonlinear complementarity equations. Using a two-stage quantum eigenpath traversal schedule, this approach yields an algorithm for solving linear optimization problems involving $m$ constraints and $n$ variables to $\varepsilon$-optimality using $\widetilde{O}\!\left((m+n)^{3/2}\,\varepsilon^{-1}+(m+n)^{1/2}\,\varepsilon^{-3/2}\right)$ queries to an oracle that evaluates a potential function. In the standard gate model (i.e., without access to quantum RAM), our algorithm can obtain $\varepsilon$-precise solutions to LO problems using at most $$\widetilde{O}\!\left(\left[\frac{(m+n)^{3/2}}{\varepsilon} + \frac{(m+n)^{1/2}}{\varepsilon^{3/2}}\right] \cdot \mathrm{nnz}(A)\right)$$ elementary gates, where $\mathrm{nnz}(A)$ is the total number of non-zero elements found in the constraint matrix.

♻ ☆ Sampling Sphere Packings with Continuum Glauber Dynamics
Aiya Kuchukova, Santosh S. Vempala, Daniel J. Zhang
Continuum Glauber dynamics is a spatial birth-death process whose stationary distribution is a Gibbs distribution. We establish a spectral gap for Continuum Glauber dynamics applied to Gibbs point processes with repulsive pair potentials, a well-known special case of which is the hard sphere model. For arbitrary-range repulsive pair potentials, we show that a continuous version of Spectral Independence suffices to establish a spectral gap. This extends the regime of activity for which Continuum Glauber dynamics is known to mix, yielding a simple efficient sampling algorithm for arbitrary-range pair potentials that matches the known efficient sampling regime for finite-range pair potentials currently based on specialized algorithms. As a consequence, we also improve the threshold up to which packings of fixed size/density can be efficiently sampled from a bounded domain, the first improvement since Kannan, Mahoney and Montenegro (2003). To prove these results, we develop continuous analogs of Spectral Independence and negative fields localization. We show that a stronger variant of zero-freeness implies Spectral Independence, which in turn allows us to run the localization scheme to boost the spectral gap of Continuum Glauber dynamics from smaller activity to larger activity. While this follows the high-level blueprint of Chen and Eldan (2022) for the discrete setting, we have to address several novel difficulties due to the continuous setting. Notably, we avoid discretization in the algorithm and the analysis and work directly in the continuous setting.

♻ ☆ Express Language Modeling
Albert Gong, Annabelle Michael Carrell, Raaz Dwivedi, Lester Mackey
We introduce a new tool, Express, for converting a non-causal attention approximation into a causal approximation with matching approximation guarantees. When combined with the state-of-the-art Thinformer approximation, Express improves upon the best known causal attention guarantees, delivering $\log^{3/2}(n)/s$ approximation error with only $O(s)$ memory and $O(s^2 \log^2(n))$ compression overhead for a sequence of length $n$. ♻ ☆ A Deterministic Near-Linear Time Approximation Algorithm for Restricted Shortest Paths in Directed Graphs
Vikrant Ashvinkumar
In the restricted shortest paths problem, we are given an $n$-vertex $m$-edge directed graph $G$ whose edges are assigned two non-negative weights (or resources) called lengths and costs, a source vertex $s$, and a cost threshold $C$. The goal is to find, for each target vertex $t$, the length of the shortest $(s,t)$-path whose total cost is at most $C$. Ashvinkumar, Bernstein, and Karczmarz (SODA 2025) gave the first randomized bicriteria $(1+\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)$-approximate algorithm on directed graphs running in sub-$mn$ time, answering a problem posed by Bernstein (SODA 2012). We substantially improve this on two fronts, supplying a deterministic bicriteria $(1+\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)$-approximate algorithm running in near-linear time. Our approach straightforwardly generalizes to more than two resources. For $k$ resources, a deterministic $(1+\varepsilon_1,\ldots,1+\varepsilon_k)$-approximate algorithm running in near-linear time is possible, with $\varepsilon_1=\ldots=\varepsilon_k=\varepsilon$.

Lizhi Du

♻ ☆ Parameterized Algorithms for Computing MAD Trees
Tom-Lukas Breitkopf, Vincent Froese, Anton Herrmann, André Nichterlein, Camille Richer
We consider the well-studied problem of finding a spanning tree with minimum average distance between vertex pairs (called a MAD tree). This is a classic network design problem which is known to be NP-hard. While approximation algorithms and polynomial-time algorithms for some graph classes are known, the parameterized complexity of the problem has not been investigated so far. We start a parameterized complexity analysis with the goal of determining the border of algorithmic tractability for the MAD tree problem. To this end, we provide a linear-time algorithm for graphs of constant modular width and a polynomial-time algorithm for graphs of bounded treewidth; the degree of the polynomial depends on the treewidth. That is, the problem is in FPT with respect to modular width and in XP with respect to treewidth. Moreover, we show that it is in FPT when parameterized by vertex integrity, by maximum leaf number, or by an above-guarantee parameter. We complement these algorithms with NP-hardness on split graphs.

♻ ☆ Self-Referential $K$-SAT and the Finite Analogue of Gödel's Incompleteness Theorem
Wen Fang, Xianxian Li, Jun Liu, Jie Luo, Yongxin Tong, Ke Xu
We study self-reference and solution independence in Boolean $K$-SAT as a finite analogue of Gödel incompleteness. Independence is defined through factorial moments of the solution count. Constant-width random formulas fail this criterion due to correlations among overlapping assignments. At a covering threshold, logarithmic-width random CNFs have a Poisson solution count, with positive probabilities of unsatisfiability and unique satisfiability. Replacing a redundant clause by one falsified by the unique solution yields an unsatisfiable companion preserving the unsigned incidence graph. For every permitted local view of a good uniquely satisfiable formula, an unsatisfiable companion preserves that view. Irreducibility defeats deterministic and bounded-error evaluators using $N^c$ clause queries, for fixed $00$, transcripts of $N^{1-δ}$ queries leave $N-o(N)$ bits of witness entropy. On typical support graphs, expansion yields linear Resolution width, linear PC/PCR and SOS degree, and exponential proof size. Bounds hold for semantic Cutting Planes under a partition condition and for Positivstellensatz with bounded product width, uniformly over unsatisfiable support-preserving signings. Structural evidence and the limits of acceleration strategies motivate the self-referential $K$-SAT exhaustive-search hypothesis (SRK-ESH): no uniform deterministic algorithm deciding the promise family achieves a fixed proportional saving in the exponent. This dual-evidence criterion combines intrinsic hardness mechanisms with algorithmic evidence. We interpret this hypothesis as a quantitative projection of the Gödel-style local--global gap. The information obstruction constrains quantum local observation and statistical learning from bounded local information.
