♻ ☆ Model checking with temporal graphs and their derivative
Binh-Minh Bui-Xuan, Florent Krasnopol, Bruno Monasson, Nathalie Sznajder
Temporal graphs are graphs where the presence or properties of their vertices and edges change over time. When time is discrete, a temporal graph can be defined as a sequence of static graphs over a discrete time span, called lifetime, or as a single graph where each edge is associated with a specific set of time instants where the edge is alive. For static graphs, Courcelle's Theorem asserts that any graph problem expressible in monadic second-order logic can be solved in linear time on graphs of bounded tree-width. We propose the first adaptation of Courcelle's Theorem for monadic second-order logic on temporal graphs that does not explicitly rely on a parameter proportional to the lifetime, or defined as the maximum number of time-edges incident with any vertex which in the worst case is higher than the lifetime. We then introduce the notion of derivative over a sliding time window of a chosen size, and define the tree-width and twin-width of the temporal graph's derivative. We exemplify its usefulness with meta-theorems with respect to a temporal variant of first-order logic. The resulting logic expresses a wide range of temporal graph problems including a version of temporal cliques, an important notion when querying time series databases for community structures.
♻ ☆ Multidimensional Online Resource Allocation for Large Language Model Services under Nonstationary Demand
Hao Wang, Zihao Li, Zhenzhen Yan
We study multidimensional online generalized assignment under nonstationary arrivals, motivated by resource allocation for large language model agent services. Providers may commit model and tool allowances to agent tasks upon admission, reserving budgets for their internal execution loops. Each request is rejected or accepted with an irrevocable assignment to one compatible server, earning reward and consuming a vector of its nonreplenishing budgets. Arrivals are independent with known, potentially time-varying type distributions. Scalar reductions can discard feasible combinations of requests with complementary resource requirements. To maximize expected reward, we develop Vector-Aware Best-Fit, which combines linear-programming-guided candidate routing with calibrated acceptance while retaining vector feasibility. Relative to the expected clairvoyant offline optimum, its guarantee improves on scalar reduction in the multidimensional case and has the best possible inverse-dimensional order. With an appropriate acceptance parameter, the proposed policy attains the optimal competitive ratio on all-heavy instances, where every feasible assignment consumes more than half of at least one resource budget. We further develop two heuristics that retain its performance guarantee. Computational results show that the heuristics perform well against benchmarks in most tested settings, especially when capacity is scarce relative to demand.
♻ ☆ Online Stochastic Allocation with Increasing Returns
Shuo Sun, Yunduan Lin
We study online allocation with increasing returns, where an uncertain number of customers arrive sequentially and can be irrevocably assigned to any product with remaining capacity. Each assignment earns a base reward plus a bonus that is nondecreasing in the number of previous assignments to the same product. Prior work has focused primarily on fixed or diminishing returns. Under increasing returns, the central challenge is that concentrating assignments can unlock large future rewards, while a different product may be preferable if arrivals stop early. We show that classical approaches, including greedy and natural LP-based rounding algorithms, can perform arbitrarily poorly, even with homogeneous bonuses. We evaluate performance through competitive ratios relative to the hindsight offline optimum. For homogeneous bonuses, we establish a tight competitive ratio of $1/2$. For arbitrary heterogeneous bonuses, however, no online algorithm can guarantee a competitive ratio greater than $1/m$, where $m$ is the number of products. By imposing discrete concavity on the bonus functions, we limit the concentration of rewards in later assignments and restore constant guarantees. Under this structure, we develop a re-solving framework that repeatedly selects a planning horizon, constructs a feasible allocation plan from the current state, and executes its assignments in a specific order. For arbitrary arrival distributions, we design distribution-oblivious deterministic and randomized algorithms with competitive ratios of $0.2537$ and $0.3864$, respectively. For known increasing-failure-rate (IFR) arrival distributions, we exploit distributional information to obtain a deterministic $1/2$-competitive algorithm.

☆ HairDeck: Animation-Ready Stylized Hair Cards from a Single Image
Yuze He, Yixiang Wei, Wang Zhao, Chuhao Chen, Yanning Zhou, Yong-Jin Liu
The hair of stylized characters in games and avatars is built from hair cards: artist-placed ribbons whose geometry is the visible hair, animated by spring bones. Existing image-based methods produce realistic hair or a single fused mesh, while CHARM, the only generator of stylized hair cards, decodes a hairstyle autoregressively as a long sequence of control points. Observing that a card hairstyle is an unordered set of artist-defined primitives, we propose HairDeck, which generates it as one: each card is a single token, and a set diffusion transformer denoises all cards together. From a single image, HairDeck generates the cards and the spring-bone chains that drive them, yielding an animatable asset that exports to ready-to-use downstream formats. On CHARM's own test set, it lowers Chamfer distance by 44% and card-orientation error by more than half while running 33 times faster, and from single images it outperforms CHARM, image-to-3D pipelines and strand-based methods. ☆ Greenscapes: Optimized Vegetation Configurations in Cities
Patryk Bartkowiak, Marcin Marlewski, Paweł Wójcik, Jae Joong Lee, Dominik L. Michels, Bedrich Benes, Sören Pirk, Wojciech Pałubicki

To define the objective function for the optimization, we evaluate the generated 3D scenes with efficient surrogate models of shade and path-based acoustic exposure. ☆ Mastering Multi-State Dynamics: Continuum Damage and Viscoplastic MPM for RL-Based Dexterous Manipulation
Yusen Feng, Shangbo Luan
Robotic manipulation of multi-state materials needs a simulator that handles large deformation, topology change, and two-way contact between tools and materials while staying fast enough to drive online reinforcement learning. We present MG-CD-MPM, a tool-centered localized extension of CD-MPM. The implicit Ginzburg-Landau phase-field damage system is solved on a local patch by preconditioned conjugate gradients using a geometric-multigrid V-cycle as the preconditioner. ☆ Trajeform: Embedding Crowd Data
Marilena Lemonari, Panayiotis Charalambous, Yiorgos Chrysanthou
Crowd simulation aims to generate agent behaviours that are rich, natural, and appropriate to their surrounding environment. Recent advances in Machine Learning have enabled such behavioural patterns to be learned directly from trajectory data, allowing simulation models to capture increasingly complex crowd dynamics. However, the capabilities of these systems remain strongly dependent on the type and diversity of the data used during training. When evaluating how well crowd simulators generalise to real-world datasets, most existing metrics focus primarily on trajectory-level differences rather than behaviour-level similarity. In this paper, we present Trajeform, a framework that trains a reconstruction VAE (Variational Autoencoder) on real trajectory data to learn a meaningful continuous embedding that preserves behavioural characteristics of real crowds. The trained encoder can then be applied to synthetic trajectories generated by simulators and used to compare the resulting embedding distributions. Additionally, the learned embedding can be used to analyse supplementary data, such as trajectories corresponding to a simulator's principal behaviours or captured data from controlled environments (e.g., cycling), providing insight into their ability to span the real behavioural embedding space. The framework compares data from three simulators (CCP, modCCP, and RVO) against ground-truth datasets and is evaluated quantitatively and qualitatively using latent-space analysis, distributional metrics, and trajectory generation through sampling from the aggregated posterior distribution. The results indicate that the learned latent space captures meaningful trajectory dependencies found in the real data, making the framework useful both as an additional measure of synthetic data believability and as a tool for informing the training of future trajectory models.

☆ CAD-WAM: Anticipating Geometry for Text-to-CAD Generation
Zhengyang Li, Weijie Zheng, Yuxin Cui, Sijie Wang, Wenjie Feng, Qiyu Kang
Generating editable computer-aided design (CAD) models from text requires selecting operations that produce the intended changes to the geometry constructed so far. An incorrect operation can compromise subsequent steps, making reliable multi-step generation challenging. Existing methods based on large language models (LLMs) primarily learn to predict commands, without explicit supervision of the geometric states those commands should produce. Inspired by world-action models, we introduce CAD-WAM, which couples geometry-conditioned action generation with next-state prediction. We define complete modeling operations as actions in a CAD domain-specific language (DSL) and encode surface point clouds of intermediate solids into compact latent states using a pretrained variational autoencoder. An LLM backbone generates the next action from the target description and current geometric state, while an auxiliary head on the same backbone predicts the next state to provide geometric supervision during training. We construct step-wise training trajectories from parametric CAD models in the DeepCAD dataset, pairing each trajectory with an expert-level textual description. At inference, each generated action is executed by a CAD kernel, and the resulting solid is re-encoded to inform the next decision. On the DeepCAD test set, CAD-WAM outperforms the task-specific baselines in geometric accuracy and output validity, with larger gains on models requiring more operations, and produces more accurate geometry than general-purpose LLMs prompted with the same DSL. Code is available at https://github.com/Lzy-1037/CAD-WAM.
♻ ☆ NRF-GS: Neural Residual Fields for Expressive and Compact Gaussian Splatting NeurIPS 2026
Pratik Singh Bisht, Andreas Kolb
We revisit the role of appearance modeling in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) and show that limited expressiveness in view-dependent reflectance is a key driver of representation redundancy. In standard 3DGS, low-order spherical harmonics (SH) are used, restricting the splats' ability to model high-frequency directional effects, which is typically compensated by increasing the number of splats. We propose \emph{NRF-GS: Neural Residual Fields for Gaussian Splatting}, a hybrid representation that replaces per-splat SH-bases with a shared neural residual field. Each Gaussian encodes a compact set of appearance features and a lambertian base color, while a lightweight \emph{global scene-level MLP} predicts view-dependent residuals conditioned on viewing direction, distance, and per-splat features. This formulation enhances directional reflectance modeling by combining diffuse per-splat reflectance representations with a shared global function for high-frequency details, enabling both higher expressiveness and parameter sharing across splats. Our key insight is that by accurately capturing high-frequency directional reflectance, especially in specular regions, the GS-representation becomes more expressive, reducing the need for geometrically redundant splats. As a result, NRF-GS achieves comparable or better rendering quality while reducing the number of Gaussians by up to 50\%, and produces visibly improved specular and high-frequency details.

♻ ☆ MEGMAG: Micro-Expression Generation via MAGnification
Sliman Jammal, Andrei Sharf
Facial micro-expressions are subtle, short-lived facial movements that reveal genuine human emotions. Despite the large advances in facial expression synthesis and animation, realistic micro-expressions remain challenging due to scarce annotated data and the extremely subtle nature of their motion. Existing dedicated micro-expression methods therefore often struggle with generation quality, robustness, and generalization. We present MEGMAG, a transferable framework for micro-expression generation via magnification that enables pretrained facial animation models to generate realistic micro-expressions without modifying or fine-tuning their backbones. The key idea is to transform subtle micro-expression dynamics into the domain of regular facial expressions, where pretrained animation models can effectively capture and transfer facial motion. A learnable Magnification Module amplifies subtle micro-expression dynamics, enabling a frozen facial animation backbone to transfer the amplified motion to a source identity without altering its original architecture or parameters. A DeMagnification Module then attenuates the generated motion to recover realistic micro-expression characteristics. This design allows MEGMAG to leverage diverse off-the-shelf facial animation models while remaining architecture-agnostic and fully transferable across backbones.
♻ ☆ SemantiScene-Edit: Expert-Synthesized Supervision and Dual-Path Source Conditioning for Instruction-Based Video Editing
Xinyu Chen, Song Wu, Dingbao Shao, Jiahang Li, Jiang Lin, Yuhang Liu, Duo Li, Jiaxin Hu, Shengrong Gu, Liang Li, Qian Wang, Junlan Feng, Ying Tai, Jian Yang, Zili Yi

☆ Tracekit Box: Running AI Agents in Micro-VMs Where Every Way Out Is Decided and Signed
Bravish Ghosh
AI agents now install packages, call model APIs with their operators' keys and change code in repositories that deploy to production. The network, credentials and write access they need are also how they can leak data, steal a key or change a system nobody approved. Sandboxes contain an agent but leave little evidence of what crossed their boundary; tracing records what happened but contains nothing. We present Tracekit Box, an open-source system (MIT, v0) that does both. Each job runs in its own Firecracker micro-VM with no network card. The only ways out are doors on the host, served over vsock: commands, outbound connections, secrets, model calls and the workspace patch. At every door a Tracekit signer the agent cannot reach decides what passes and signs it into one run per box. Names are resolved on the host; private and cloud-metadata addresses are always refused; secrets never enter the VM, which sees placeholders the host proxy swaps for real values only in one header for bound hosts; and changes come back as a patch applied file by file after the signer decides each file. Box fails closed, and each job ends as a .tkb bundle anyone can verify offline. All 13 end-to-end checks pass on a real KVM runner (the test that runs them takes 28.5 s, including image pull and boot); 15 fuzz targets on the trust boundary produced nine findings, all fixed; and the demo run's 102 signed records verify. Box does not yet run Firecracker's jailer, needs a co-located signer, and records processes inside the VM only where they cross a door.

☆ FlatCake: A Flat KVCache Storage Architecture for LLM Serving
Xin Huang, Teng Ma, Zhuoran Li, Yibo Zhao, Zhaoshi Li, Qianchao Zhu, Wei Yang, Chenxi Wang, Tao Xie, Youwei Zhuo
KV cache reuse accelerates LLM serving, but existing systems manage host DRAM and NVMe SSD as a tiered hierarchy where DRAM acts as an intermediate cache. Production LLM workloads break both premises of this hierarchy. First, bimodal reuse defeats DRAM temporal filtering, since workloads are dominated either by one-shot blocks with long reuse distances or by multi-gigabyte agentic sessions that exceed DRAM capacity, putting SSD on the critical read path. Second, DRAM staging and eviction-ordered layout bottleneck SSD restoration. Holding host staging buffers for in-flight prefixes causes contention under load that aborts prefetches, while incremental eviction scatters a session's blocks across the SSD and prevents large sequential reads. We present FlatCake, a flat KV storage architecture that exposes host DRAM and NVMe SSD as peer media in a unified 64-bit address space. Exploiting the fact that KV blocks are write-once, immutable, and reused at session scope, FlatCake places each block directly onto its target medium in session-contiguous, layer-major order at creation time and never migrates it. FlatCake streams coalesced SSD reads directly into a bounded GPU window via GPUDirect Storage and decouples DRAM and SSD request queues to prevent head-of-line blocking. On a production Claude Code trace with DeepSeek-V4-Flash, FlatCake sustains a 94% cache hit rate as load increases (versus 64% for Mooncake), eliminates Mooncake's $6.5\times$--$6.7\times$ SSD read amplification, and reduces peak-load mean TTFT by $1.6\times$ while improving total token throughput by $1.6\times$. Across 8K--128K contexts on Llama-3.1-70B, FlatCake further reduces mean TTFT by up to $2.4\times$ over Mooncake and $1.8\times$ over Mooncake-GDS.

☆ LLM-PowerTrace: A Dataset of Time-Resolved GPU Power Traces for AI Compute
Jiansheng Wang, Jaskeerat Singh, Haochen Xu, Xinyi Li, Mingyuan Yan, Shaohui Liu, Yuzhuo Li, Yize Chen, Wenqi Cui
AI compute is becoming large and fast-changing electrical loads. Its impacts on data center infrastructure and power grids depend not only on total energy consumption, but also on how their power draw moves over sub-second to minute timescales, including peaks, ramps, and synchronized fluctuations across accelerators. Existing energy benchmarks for large language models (LLMs) report aggregate quantities such as joules per token and rarely release the underlying time-series measurements, leaving these power dynamics poorly characterized and difficult to model. We introduce LLM-PowerTrace, an open dataset of time-resolved, per-GPU power traces for LLM fine-tuning and inference, sampled every 100\,ms on varying NVIDIA platforms paired with complete workload and system metadata. For fine-tuning, LLM-PowerTrace profiles open model families spanning a wide range of model sizes and fine-tuning methods, together with controlled sweeps over batch size, sequence length, precision, parallelism, and checkpointing. For inference, it covers variations in concurrency, GPU frequency, KV-cache and weight quantization, prompt and output lengths, and request-arrival patterns. LLM-PowerTrace profiles a set of open model families across a wide range of model sizes, and controlled sweeps over batch size, sequence length, precision, parallelism, and checkpointing for fine-tuning, and it sweeps concurrency, GPU frequency, KV cache and weight quantization, prompt and output lengths, and request arrival patterns for inference. Built upon power traces, we define power-grid-facing metrics as well as AI-compute power\&energy scaling law for extrapolating beyond the measured settings. Together, LLM-PowerTrace provides a dataset foundation for modeling, forecasting, and managing the fast-changing electrical behavior of AI compute.

☆ GearUp: DAG-aware Acceleration for Serverless Inference Serving via Slack-guided Pre-loading
Qi Qi, Yifan Sui, Hanfei Yu, Hong Zhang, Hao Wang
Serverless platforms increasingly host machine learning (ML) inference workflows, modeled as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) of interdependent functions. Beyond container startup, each ML node must load libraries, model weights, and CUDA kernels before it can run. These costs accumulate along dependent DAG paths and place initialization on the end-to-end critical path. Existing systems optimize one half of this problem. DAG-agnostic pre-loading prepares nodes the request may never reach, wasting host and GPU memory. DAG-aware serving routes and scales whole functions, leaving the loading stages inside a node untouched. This paper presents GearUp, a serverless DAG inference serving system that removes ML-node loading from the end-to-end critical path at low resource cost. GearUp profiles the loading stages of each node and schedules them inside the slack time the DAG exposes, hiding initialization behind upstream execution. It reuses initialized instances within and across requests to avoid redundant cold starts. A probabilistic, traffic-adaptive keep-alive policy sets how long idle instances are retained, balancing memory cost against cold-start risk under bursty load. On the AMBER Alert and FINRA-ML workflows with NVIDIA L40S GPUs, GearUp reduces median end-to-end latency by 64.5% and 75.9% against SMIless, for median speedups of 3.1x and 10.0x over cold execution. Integrated GPU and host memory allocation fall by up to 83.9% and 94.6% against eager pre-loading in InstaInfer. GearUp achieves 4.2x strong scaling on four workers.

☆ IMPACT: Constraint-Aware Search for Imperfectly Factorized Accelerator Mappings SP
Konstantinos Stavrakakis, Ilgaz Er, Theofilos Spyrou, Dimitrios Stavrakakis, Stephan Wong, Georgi Gaydadjiev, Said Hamdioui
The efficiency of Deep Neural Network (DNN) accelerators depends on how each tensor operation is mapped onto the hardware. A mapping specifies how the computation is tiled across the memory hierarchy, ordered, and parallelized across the processing elements. These choices form a combinatorially large search space, where efficient mappings are hard to find. Most violate hardware constraints, and most remaining mappings underutilize the hardware. Existing mappers either search only part of this space to keep the search tractable, excluding the most efficient mappings, or sample the full space randomly, so most mappings they evaluate are infeasible. We present IMPACT, a mapper that searches the full space. IMPACT pairs a genetic algorithm with a constraint-aware repair operator that converts each infeasible mapping into a feasible one instead of discarding it. On a flexible accelerator, IMPACT outperforms state-of-the-art mappers in EDP, latency, and energy on every DeepBench workload. On a compute-in-memory (CIM) accelerator, whose constraints leave few mappings feasible, it reduces EDP by 66%, latency by 47%, and energy by 18% over the strongest prior mapper across five networks, and rivals hand-crafted expert mappings. Across flexible and highly constrained accelerators, IMPACT finds efficient mappings automatically. IMPACT will be released as open source.
comment: 7 pages, 6 figures. Accepted for publication in ASP-DAC 2027
☆ Hardwired Distributed Processing Units at the Sensor: Truly Parallel Vision Pre-Processing that Frees the Host CPU
Raj Mali
Conventional vision pipelines route every pixel to a host processor, which must then wait for a complete frame before it can act and devote part of each frame period to routine pre-processing. We examine an alternative organisation in which this work is distributed across hardwired distributed processing units (HDPUs): small, fixed datapaths attached directly to the sensor, each committed to a single always-on task, operating truly in parallel on the same pixel stream and delivering compact results to the host. From one library of 3x3 operators sharing a common two-clock latency, we construct three HDPUs (Canny edges, Harris corners, and a line tracker that issues a steering decision) and run them side by side on a single camera stream in a low-cost Artix-7 FPGA, at a cost of 2,568 LUTs and an estimated 0.318 W. Every stage of every unit is bit-exact with its software model. In a full-system simulation with a datasheet camera model, each decision leaves its HDPU within 11.03 camera lines of the pixel that produced it, 22-67x sooner than the earliest moment at which a frame-based program on the host could respond; the HDPU latency varies by no more than 17 ns, whereas the host's own run times reached 12.5x their median. Offloading the three tasks returns 18-30% of a core to the host in every frame period at 640x480, and 63% of the frame period, with all threads in use, at 1080p. The cost of the units grows with frame width only through their line buffers, and in a closed-loop model of a line-following vehicle the HDPU raises the highest speed at which the line is held from 4.75 to 10.00 m/s.

☆ Streaming Speech Recognition on a Microcontroller: a 65.7M-Parameter Transducer in 2 and 4 Bits on the ESP32-P4
Billal Boumedine
We run Kroko, a 65.7M-parameter streaming Zipformer2 transducer for English, on the ESP32-P4, a microcontroller with two 400 MHz RISC-V cores, a 128-bit SIMD extension (PIE) and 32 MB of PSRAM, but no neural accelerator. Without any training, the encoder's 290 matrices are stored with 2 or 4 bits per weight in Hadamard-rotated groups: the width of each matrix comes from its measured sensitivity, and each matrix is first corrected toward the original model's output, then rounded with GPTQ and a search over the group norms. The released 71 MB of int8 weights become a 26.65 MB file. Activations are int16 between the matrix products and int8 into them. A C engine with hand-written PIE kernels processes each 1.28 s chunk of audio in 0.90 s on both cores, beam search included, and its PC build computes the same bits as the chip, so accuracy is measured on whole test sets. LibriSpeech WER is 4.34% on test-clean and 10.26% on test-other, against 3.94% and 9.17% for the released model; on FLEURS and Common Voice the gap grows to 2.3 and 1.1 points. A GPU simulator shows that the integer arithmetic costs nothing and that the whole gap comes from the weights, and an ablation on all of test-clean shows that GPTQ does most of the work. We also report the port's speed history, from 51.3 s to 0.62 s of encoder time per chunk, each design step measured again in one build, an attempt to reach 16 MB that stopped at 19.63 MB, and what did not work. We did not measure energy. The inference engine, the firmware and the 2/4-bit weights are available at https://github.com/BoumedineBillal/esp32-pie-asr.

☆ GI-RTL-Fuzz: Grammar-Informed RTL Generation with LLMs for End-to-End Differential Simulator Fuzzing
Ziyue Zheng, Zhi Qu, Yangdi Lyu
Register-Transfer Level (RTL) simulators are fundamental to modern IC design workflows, yet like any complex software, they frequently contain critical bugs that compromise simulation accuracy. To systematically stress-test these tools, we introduce GI-RTL-Fuzz, a grammar-informed fuzzing framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate high-quality RTL test cases. Our key innovation is guiding the LLM's generation process using Backus-Naur Form (BNF) grammar coverage information, enabling the exploration of complex and rarely-tested language features. ☆ The Code Behind the Layout: AI Agents for Analog Layout Generators
Minxing Chu, Anhang Li, Martins Atilla, Ruichen Qi, Mehdi Saligane
Analog layout remains largely manual and hard to automate. Generator frameworks such as gLayout express layouts as programs, producing layouts from parameterized, design-rule-aware generators; however, writing such generators still takes expert effort. Large language models (LLMs) could now write generator code, and frontier models can even draw layout geometry from design intent. This raises a practical question: if a model can draw the layout itself, what does a generator framework still contribute, and is applying these models to a generator framework better than having them draw a layout from scratch? Through controlled experiments on gLayout in SKY130, we find that the main obstacle to drawing a layout directly is its time and token cost, and that the main advantage of a model building on a generator framework is reusability. Its verified primitives keep each model edit small enough to check, and its parameters allow a single edit to be evaluated across an entire family of layouts. Accordingly, a model is most effective when it develops the layout from the generator source of the family that the design intent belongs to, and tests each edit on the family members most likely to fail, so that one verified change improves every layout the generator produces. The results of these family tests can then serve as supervision for a local model, which learns edits that hold across the family at a fraction of the inference cost.
☆ A Case Study of a Multi-Agent Kernel-Optimization Loop That Learns a New AI Accelerator MICRO 2026
Byonggon Chun
Frontier large language models write correct CUDA kernels for most single-operator benchmark tasks, yet their accuracy collapses on accelerators whose architecture and programming language they have rarely seen. We present a case study of an LLM agent loop built for such accelerators. Our example is the FuriosaAI RNGD NPU, programmed in a recent, sparsely documented Rust DSL, where the loop optimized three Gemma-4-12B decode kernels over 12 days on a shared, scarce device. The loop builds its own target knowledge: it writes and checks a summary of the DSL documentation, measures the chip with microbenchmarks, records compiler-derived legality rules, and revisits this knowledge when the toolchain changes, rechecking rules and measurements where it can. A role-based search over per-kernel Design Trees explores whole-kernel designs and accumulates small accepted changes in the best version of each kernel. Because device time is scarce and readings are noisy, cheap checks screen new designs before any device run, and late in the run a change was accepted only after repeated comparisons with the best kernel on the device. The final kernels reach a 9.293x geomean speedup over the official baseline, each within 1.25x of the cycles needed just to move its weights. Our prompts, profiler and acceptance test are available at https://github.com/bgchun-fs/multi-agent-kernel-gen-loop.

☆ Rosemary: Efficient and Adaptable In-Storage Acceleration for Fully Homomorphic Encryption
Xuan Wang, Tianqi Zhang, Gabrielle De Micheli, Yujin Nam, Keming Fan, Augusto Vega, Minxuan Zhou, Tajana Rosing
Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) enables computation on encrypted data and is promising for privacy-preserving computing. However, FHE deployment remains constrained due to massive computational, memory, and data-movement overheads. Existing acceleration strategies often overlook the storage I/O bottleneck caused by the enormous size of FHE data in large-scale applications, as most prior works focus on small benchmarks that fit in main memory. Our studies show that state-of-the-art FHE systems spend 71\% to 85\% of their execution time moving data between storage and compute units rather than performing useful computation. To address the I/O bottleneck, we present Rosemary, a heterogeneous FHE acceleration system that incorporates a novel in-storage processing (ISP) architecture with compiler-level and algorithmic optimizations. Our key innovations include 1) an adaptable hardware-aware FHE engine inside a NAND flash-based solid-state drive (SSD) for efficient and cost-effective in-storage FHE processing, 2) a Throughput-Data-Aware Program Allocator (TDAP) that partitions FHE workloads into heterogeneous programs, and 3) a Compiler-Level Ciphertext-Aware Optimizer (CLCAO) that exploits ISP-friendly FHE kernels for low-cost ISP execution. Across a diverse set of FHE workloads, including encrypted deep learning inference and cloud database queries, Rosemary achieves geometric-mean speedups of $185.7\times$ over state-of-the-art CPU/GPU implementations and $3.7\times$ over prior FHE accelerators, while improving energy efficiency by up to $3.9\times$ and Energy-Delay-Area-Product (EDAP) by up to $16.5\times$.
☆ FPGAFly: A 166.7k-Neuron Fruit-Fly Connectome on One FPGA via Deferred Spike Expansion and Shared-Codeword States
YE Ziyang
Synapse-resolution fruit-fly (Drosophila) connectomes enable neuron-by-neuron simulation of entire insect nervous systems, yet whole-brain models still require computationally demanding software or multiple neuromorphic chips, and single FPGAs support only small or statistical networks. Scaling to the full connectome, per-synapse spike expansion scales queue traffic with fan-out up to 11.2k, while 41-bit neuron states require 6.83 Mbit, exceeding the 6.27 Mbit on-chip block static RAM (BSRAM) of the target device. FPGAFly runs the 166.7k-neuron, 25.6M-connection leaky integrate-and-fire male central nervous system (CNS) connectome on one FPGA. Order-preserving deferred expansion queues one 64-bit descriptor per spike in source-ID order, cutting external memory traffic by 54.5%-57.9% and peak queue data by 95.6%-96.5% with bit-identical results. A 24-bit encoding shares codewords across normal, refractory, and quantized out-of-range states, reducing BSRAM usage from 438 to 270 with 1245 extra LUTs and zero changes across 10,000 MNIST predictions. At 80 MHz on a Gowin GW5AST-LV138, each 100 ms window finishes in 86.83 ms on average and within 93.05 ms across 2000 tests, realizing the first complete Drosophila CNS connectome simulation on one FPGA.
☆ SPARSEBLEED: Bypassing Attribute-Inference Defenses via Sparsity Optimizations
Darsh Asher, Farshad Dizani, Joshua Kalyanapu, Ro Cammarota, Aydin Aysu, Samira Mirbagher Ajorpaz
Deep neural networks (DNNs) are widely deployed in safety- and privacy-critical domains such as healthcare and finance, where models are trained on confidential data. This paper presents the first hardware-level attribute-stealing attack that bypasses state-of-the-art privacy defenses, revealing that accelerator optimizations intended for performance can directly undermine data confidentiality. SparseBleed requires no access to gradients, logits, model weights, ground-truth labels, power traces, DVFS manipulation, shared caches, or other channels. Instead, we show that sparsity-driven execution paths encode stable timing signatures that act as a surrogate for model confidence, enabling high-fidelity attribute inference from a single query. SparseBleed generalizes across heterogeneous backends and hardware engines, including Intel AVX2/AVX-512/AMX, NVIDIA Ampere Tensor Cores with 2:4 sparsity, and nine well-known sparse and dense libraries such as Intel oneDNN, oneMKL SparseBLAS, and NVIDIA cuSPARSE. We evaluate SparseBleed to infer sensitive input attributes and true output labels. On Texas-100X, SparseBleed infers sensitive input attributes such as Sex with 66.39% accuracy and recovers the true output label, Surgical Procedure, with 99.8% accuracy. Finally, we evaluate mitigations. Constant-time execution by disabling sparsity optimizations is expensive (e.g., up to $2\times$ higher latency on NVIDIA Sparse Tensor Cores and $\approx 25\%$ higher power in AMX microbenchmarks). By contrast, a padding defense that equalizes responses to the worst-case time masks timing leakage with only 7.24% average performance overhead across nine libraries on Texas-100X/Adult and near-zero extra energy.
♻ ☆ ESBMC-PLC: Formal Verification of IEC 61131-3 Ladder Diagram Programs Using SMT-Based Model Checking
Pierre Dantas, Lucas Cordeiro, Waldir Junior
Ladder diagram (LD) is the relay-circuit language of IEC 61131-3 in which many PLC safety interlocks are written, and model checkers built on SMT solvers do not read it. A verdict reached through a translation is only as good as that translation. We present ESBMC-PLC, which verifies LD programs with the ESBMC model checker, and argue that its translation is faithful. It encodes the scan cycle as a single loop with nondeterministic inputs, resolves graphical PLCopen XML networks into ordered rungs, models timers in milliseconds, and rejects programs outside an explicit subset. We prove, under eight stated hypotheses, that one iteration of the emitted loop agrees with one scan of a semantics for the subset, so scan-count verdicts on programs without user function blocks hold on that semantics. For timing-mode verdicts, a refinement theorem for MATIEC's timers gives evidence, not proof. One SMT query per program also compares the emitted loop with the C that MATIEC generates. On the 241 labeled LD variants of a public benchmark suite, ESBMC-PLC returns no wrong verdict against the current labels and proves 127 variants safe; ESBMC on MATIEC's C for the same programs proves 2. PLCverif with NuSMV, which shares neither our frontend nor ESBMC, agrees with ESBMC-PLC on all 207 of the 208 variants within the theorem's scope that it can run. On 413 LD programs from public GitHub repositories, none of the 104 that the per-program query can check differs from MATIEC. Every k-induction proof closes at k = 2. ESBMC's inductive step proves only properties that one scan preserves, so properties that depend on a longer history stay unproved.

♻ ☆ Continuous-time nonlinear closed-loop in-memory computing for high-accuracy massive MIMO detection
Piergiulio Mannocci, Giacomo Pedretti, Giacomo Larelli, Fabian Böhm, Thomas Van Vaerenbergh
Analog in-memory computing (IMC) has emerged as a promising approach for accelerating matrix operations by exploiting the intrinsic physics of memory arrays. To date, however, most IMC architectures have focused on linear algebra workloads in which computation is encoded in the equilibrium state of a physical system. Extending these principles to nonlinear optimization remains challenging and typically relies on iterative algorithms composed of repeated linear operations. Here, we introduce a continuous-time nonlinear closed-loop IMC architecture for box-constrained zero-forcing (BCZF) decoding in massive multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) systems. The proposed architecture embeds the decoding problem directly within the dynamics of a nonlinear feedback network of memory arrays and supply-limited operational amplifiers, allowing solutions to emerge through continuous-time physical optimization. We derive a compact analytical model of the circuit and show that its trajectories minimize an equivalent energy function. Experimental emulation using a fabricated IMC chip confirms the predicted dynamics under realistic hardware nonidealities for up to 16x16 MIMO systems. To overcome the finite precision of analog hardware, we extend mixed-precision iterative refinement from linear algebra to nonlinear continuous-time optimization, enabling reliable detection of high-order modulation formats including 1024-QAM. Benchmark projections indicate operation from ultra-low-energy approximate decoding to high-accuracy massive MIMO detection. Together, these results extend closed-loop IMC from equilibrium-based linear algebra to continuous-time nonlinear optimization and establish a pathway toward efficient physical accelerators for high-accuracy wireless communications.
♻ ☆ Proba: A High-Performance, Low-Traffic Probabilistic Spatial Memory Streaming Prefetcher
Yinting Huang, Jacky Wong, Sam Ainsworth
Spatial Memory Streaming (SMS) prefetchers exploit recurring spatial access patterns across memory regions to predict future accesses. Despite their effectiveness, existing SMS-based designs suffer from coarse control over aggressiveness and limited adaptability to dynamic program behavior and historical patterns. In this work, we present Proba, a feedback-directed SMS-based prefetcher that integrates probabilistic pattern updates, marginal accuracy decision-making, and pattern accumulation over several trigger features to achieve fine-grained accuracy-coverage trade-offs. When accuracy deteriorates, Proba moderates its aggressiveness to prioritize accurate prefetches; conversely, when accuracy rises, it expands its coverage. Proba achieves higher speedups than any other SMS-based prefetcher while being vastly more efficient in DRAM traffic. This culminates in a large performance gap between Proba and other techniques in multicore and DRAM-constrained scenarios without harming single-threaded throughput.

♻ ☆ Ego-OSCAR: Egocentric Open source Stereo CAptuRe System
Senthil Palanisamy, Satpal Singh Rathore, Pratyush Kumar Patnaik, Shubhanshu Khatana, Gunjan Paul, Abhishek Anand
Egocentric vision has scaled its corpora far faster than its instruments. Calibrated stereo rigidly coupled to a synchronized inertial stream is not new; research-grade headsets deliver it. What does not exist is an open version: those platforms are closed products a third party cannot build or extend, and the consumer cameras used elsewhere are monocular and rolling-shutter. The instrument is the least reproducible part of an otherwise open pipeline. We present our system, an open-hardware head-mounted capture platform, at a complete bill of materials under USD 200 in commodity parts and 3D prints. It pairs a hardware-synchronized global-shutter stereo camera with a 6-axis IMU, an embedded Linux SBC for on-device encoding, and a real-time microcontroller that bridges the camera and inertial clock domains to a residual of 700 microseconds and watchdogs the recording against silent failure. We release the full stack alongside the device: CAD, firmware, recording and time-synchronization tooling, and our corpus, roughly 550 hours of stereo video over 1,462 sessions and 25 contributors, every device shipping its own stereo calibration. The corpus ships with model-predicted annotation layers rather than raw video alone, including per-frame 3D hand reconstructions spanning essentially the whole timeline; these are automatic predictions, not human-verified ground truth, and we report them as such. We characterize the instrument along sensor fidelity, data utility, and six months of distributed field deployment. Our system is not the highest fidelity egocentric rig; it is the cheapest one a reader of this paper can reproduce, and the only corpus here that others can extend rather than only consume.
♻ ☆ Arapai: An Offline-First LLM Architecture for Adaptive Learning in Low-Connectivity Environments
Joseph Walusimbi, Ann Move Oguti, Joshua Benjamin Ssentongo, Keith Ainebyona
Artificial intelligence and large language models (LLMs) are transforming educational technology by enabling conversational tutoring, personalised explanations, and inquiry-driven learning. This paper presents Arapai, an offline-first large language model architecture designed for AI-assisted learning in low-connectivity settings. ♻ ☆ RETENTION: Resource-Efficient Tree-Based Ensemble Model Acceleration with Content-Addressable Memory
Yi-Chun Liao, Chieh-Lin Tsai, Yuan-Hao Chang, Camélia Slimani, Jalil Boukhobza, Tei-Wei Kuo
Although deep learning has demonstrated remarkable capability in learning from unstructured data, modern tree-based ensemble models remain superior in extracting relevant information and learning from structured datasets. While several efforts have been made to accelerate tree-based models, the inherent characteristics of the models pose significant challenges for conventional accelerators. Recent research leveraging content-addressable memory (CAM) offers a promising solution for accelerating tree-based models, yet existing designs suffer from excessive memory consumption and low utilization. This work addresses these challenges by introducing RETENTION, an end-to-end framework that significantly reduces CAM capacity requirement for tree-based model inference. We propose an iterative pruning algorithm with a novel pruning criterion tailored for bagging-based models (e.g., Random Forest), which minimizes model complexity while ensuring controlled accuracy degradation. These results demonstrate that RETENTION is highly effective in minimizing CAM resource demand, providing a resource-efficient direction for tree-based model acceleration.

☆ Beyond atol and rtol: Sound Test Oracles for Low-Precision GPU Kernels
Xinyi Shi
Low-precision GPU kernels are commonly tested against a reference using hand-picked absolute and relative tolerances. Legitimate numerical error varies with the input, format and precision plan, so a fixed tolerance trades false alarms for missed faults. We present numoracle, which derives per-element acceptance regions from precision-annotated reference programs. Its calculus tracks shared rounding errors, provenance-ordered products, block-scaled quantizers and calibrated math-library errors. A rounding-interval check detects faulty stores that symmetric bounds can admit. Soundness is conditional on explicit rounding, hardware, domain and implementation assumptions; a fixed-input mode removes the zero-mean rounding assumption. We evaluate 13 Triton operators from Liger-Kernel, Unsloth and vLLM on two GPU generations. The main campaigns produce no false alarm on 33,960 correct-kernel and behavior-preserving-variant runs per GPU. Under input shift, the GPU campaigns record no false alarm while tuned tolerances flag up to 30% of correct runs; final-version reference checks identify inputs without complete finite-range certificates. On the RTX PRO 6000, numoracle kills 116 of 124 effective mutants, versus 59-83 for four fixed baselines. Among 400 tuned tolerance configurations, requiring at most 1% false alarms in every evaluated Liger input group permits at most 31 of 53 main-mutant kills, versus numoracle's 50. Historical replays expose accuracy faults in eight change pairs without rejecting their matched corrected controls; a ninth change concerns rounding parity.

☆ Write Once, Instrument Anywhere: Portable, Sandboxed Tooling with WebAssembly Components
Elizabeth Gilbert, Chris Fallin, Heather Miller, Ben L. Titzer
Monitoring and debugging deployed software is crucial for scalability, performance, and robustness. Cross-cutting concerns are pervasive across software ecosystems, yet engineers must address them with a fragmented toolbox. While high-level frameworks exist to offer application-level types and semantics, they often lock a solution into specific languages, runtimes, or hardware architectures. Portability often forces engineers to choose lower-level instrumentation mechanisms. For example, in-language frameworks can trace a request across processes, but multi-language contexts require instrumentation at the boundary, for example using eBPF. While these frameworks provide powerful capabilities, they often result in 1) a tradeoff between application-level types or low-level bytes and packets, 2) non-composable domain-specific tools, 3) feature fragmentation across frameworks, and 4) tools that often run with unrestricted access to the program and its environment. We address these problems with Splicer, a WebAssembly component interposition framework that 1) provides semantically rich application-level types, 2) enables pluggable tooling with powerful capabilities, 3) reduces ecosystem fragmentation through porting across languages and hardware, and 4) confines each tool to a sandbox with explicitly granted permissions. We evaluate Splicer on a suite of benchmarks demonstrating the pluggability of non-trivial tooling written in multiple source languages and their performance.
☆ A Case Study of a Multi-Agent Kernel-Optimization Loop That Learns a New AI Accelerator MICRO 2026
Byonggon Chun

The loop builds its own target knowledge: it writes and checks a summary of the DSL documentation, measures the chip with microbenchmarks, records compiler-derived legality rules, and revisits this knowledge when the toolchain changes, rechecking rules and measurements where it can. A role-based search over per-kernel Design Trees explores whole-kernel designs and accumulates small accepted changes in the best version of each kernel. Because device time is scarce and readings are noisy, cheap checks screen new designs before any device run, and late in the run a change was accepted only after repeated comparisons with the best kernel on the device. The final kernels reach a 9.293x geomean speedup over the official baseline, each within 1.25x of the cycles needed just to move its weights. Our prompts, profiler and acceptance test are available at https://github.com/bgchun-fs/multi-agent-kernel-gen-loop.

☆ Local Important Separators with Applications to Unbreakable Decomposition and Multicut
Aditya Anand, Euiwoong Lee, Yaowei Long, Thatchaphol Saranurak
We present the first local FPT algorithm for finding important separators: Given an undirected graph $G$ with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges, vertices $s,t$ and parameters $k$ and $ν$, we can enumerate all $s$-$t$ important separators of size at most $k$ and volume at most $ν$ in time $\tilde{O}(ν2^{O(k)})$. Known approaches spend either $Ω(m)$ time (not local) or $ν^{Ω(k)}$ time (not FPT). Using this local algorithm, we show the following applications. (1) The first is an improved construction of unbreakable decomposition, a powerful generalization of tree decompositions. For any $k$, we can compute a $(5k,k)$-unbreakable decomposition with adhesion $4k$ in $\tilde{O}(k^{O(k)}m)$ time. This strictly subsumes the result of Anand, Lee, Li, Long, and Saranurak (SODA 2025) and is faster than the recent $O(k^{O(k^2)}m)$-time algorithm by Korhonen (STOC 2025) when $k = ω\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\log \log n}{\log \log \log n}}\Bigr)$. Our algorithm is also simpler than that of Korhonen (STOC 2025). (2) We give a near-linear time algorithm for single-sink important separators. For any sink $t$, we can list all $v$-$t$ important separators of size at most $k$ across all $v \in V(G)$ in time $\tilde{O}(2^{O(k)}m)$. Known approaches trivially compute $v$-$t$ important separators for each $v$, taking $Ω(4^k k mn)$ time. (3) Using the above result on single-sink important separators, we speed up undirected shadow removal and give the first almost-linear time FPT algorithm for Edge Multicut.

☆ Planned Restlessness: Delayed Meta Bandits with Budgets
Sudipto Guha
We study episodic finite-horizon Bayesian bandits, or meta-bandits, with independent arms. Each episode draws fresh arm qualities from unknown recurring priors. Observations arrive after known delays, and plays obey hard per-episode caps and total budgets. A play earns reward and informs both the current quality and the recurring prior: the learner must explore, use, and generalize. We formulate a common planning optimization over learning trajectories. Its LP relaxation values experiments through current reward, resource consumption and continuation: the value of future decisions from the learning state produced. Dual prices separate the arms into local programs and certify every admissible policy, including hierarchical Thompson sampling. Execution enforces the hard constraints while preserving the promised learning transitions or accounting for their loss. The framework also represents risk-sensitive objectives and prices purchased observations and buyout options. Delayed reports create planned restlessness: an arm's information changes while it is not played, through reports of its own earlier plays arriving on a known calendar. For stationary priors, finite reward alphabets, bounded report lag and nonbinding budgets, posterior sampling over the recurring priors bounds the price of not knowing them by $O(\sqrt{N})$ over $N$ episodes, with other parameters fixed. For one undelayed $T$-step episode with arms capped at $T/X$ plays each, we obtain the LP-relative approximation factor $1+(2πX)^{-1/2}+O(X^{-1})$, improving previous factors, with a matching lower bound for the leading term.
☆ Dimension Reduction and Coresets for Min-Sum $k$-Clustering
Asaf Dallal, Robert Krauthgamer
The min-sum $k$-clustering problem asks to partition an input of $n$ points into $k$ clusters so as to minimize the total pairwise distance within clusters. It is a natural alternative to center-based objectives when the data is not well captured by a small set of representative points. We study for this problem two standard sketching methods, dimension reduction and coresets, which represent the input more compactly while preserving the min-sum cost of every $k$-partition up to $1\pm\varepsilon$ factor. Our first contribution addresses dimension reduction in Euclidean space. The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) Lemma readily implies that a random linear map to target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ preserves, with high probability, the min-sum cost of all $k$-partitions. We show that target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(ddim\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log k+\log\log n))$ suffices for Euclidean inputs of doubling dimension at most $ddim$, and moreover this bound is nearly optimal. We also show that target dimension $t=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log k+\logΔ))$ suffices for Euclidean inputs of aspect ratio at most $Δ$. Our second contribution is to design the first $\varepsilon$-coreset for metric min-sum $k$-clustering. It is actually a movement-based coreset, has size $(1/\varepsilon)^{O(ddim)} k\log n$ for inputs of doubling dimension at most $ddim$, and can be constructed in randomized near-linear-time. We further use these coresets to design streaming algorithms for the problem.
☆ Fully Dynamic Unconstrained Submodular Maximization
Mohammad Hajiaghayi
We study unconstrained maximization of a fixed nonnegative submodular function when the ground set changes by insertions and deletions. A uniformly random subset gives a $1/4$-approximation with constantly many value-oracle queries per update. We obtain improvements over this bound for arbitrary oblivious update sequences, without knowing future deletions. For value estimation, we give a $(\sqrt2-1-\varepsilon)$-approximation using $O_{\varepsilon,δ}(\log^2(N+1))$ expected amortized oracle queries, with failure probability at most $δ$ at each fixed time. Here $N$ bounds the number of active elements. The algorithm maintains Poisson samples of the positive mass of random chain lower bounds. We prove that the factor $\sqrt2-1$ is tight for this certificate, even when polynomially many independent orders are used. For every fixed integer $K\ge2$, we maintain a feasible $(1/4+Ω(K^{-2}))$-approximate set using $\widetilde O_K(N^{1/K})$ amortized queries. Thus every fixed exponent $γ>0$ permits a fixed improvement over $1/4$ with $\widetilde O_γ(N^γ)$ update queries. In the square-root regime, a stronger algorithm attains $ρ_{\rm sq}-\varepsilon$, where $ρ_{\rm sq}>0.3177$. The maintained sets are feasible after every update, and their approximation guarantees hold in expectation. All bounds count oracle queries; the value estimator may query previously seen inactive elements, whereas the set algorithms use only currently active elements.
☆ Fast Perfect $L_2$ Sampling PODS 2027
David P. Woodruff, Samson Zhou
Perfect $L_2$ sampling is a powerful primitive for turnstile data streams, allowing algorithms to extract an index with probability proportional to its squared frequency, with applications to dynamic join size estimation, query optimization, heavy-hitters, sampling from self-joins, and real-time monitoring of evolving datasets. The setting $p=2$ is also the largest $p$ for which $L_p$ sampling can be achieved in polylogarithmic space. However, existing space-optimal perfect samplers rely on explicit polynomial duplication, requiring inefficient polynomial update times per stream arrival. Resolving a open question left by Swartworth, Woodruff, and Zhou [FOCS 2025], we present a perfect $L_2$ sampler that achieves an expected $\text{polylog}(n)$ update time while maintaining an optimal $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\log^3 n)$ bits of space.

☆ Complexity of Differentially Private Selection via Federated APIs NeurIPS 2026
Hilal Asi, Vitaly Feldman, Jelani Nelson, Huy L. Nguyen, Kunal Talwar, Samson Zhou
Differential privacy (DP) has become the standard for privacy-preserving data analysis, yet its practical implementation in federated or distributed settings often faces a significant utility gap compared to the central model. While existing frameworks, including local, shuffle, and secure aggregation models, offer vetted primitives, they can incur high error for important tasks such as selection. Our first contribution is to show that this is inherent in the aggregation model: any private selection algorithm relying on the standard noisy aggregation primitive requires $Ω(\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)$ samples. To address this limitation, we propose adding a new primitive that implements a version of the Sparse Vector Technique (SVT) and argue that it has simple implementations under various trust models. We demonstrate its power by developing an $\varepsilon$-differentially private algorithm for the selection problem. For selection in dimension $d$, our algorithm achieves an additive error of $O\left(\frac{\log(dn)}{\varepsilon}\right)$, matching that of the central model when $n$ is polynomially bounded in $d$. Furthermore, we show that this can be done using a near-optimal expected number $O(d\log^*d)$ of SVT queries. Together with existing lower bounds for the shuffle model, this result establishes an exponential separation between the SVT model and shuffle or aggregation-based models. We demonstrate the model's broad applicability to diverse tasks such as $F_p$ loss minimization, heavy-hitter identification, and sparse mean estimation.

☆ Dynamic $(Δ+1)$ Vertex Coloring with Adaptive Adversaries: Entropy Compression to the Rescue
Sepehr Assadi, Helia Yazdanyar
We present a randomized algorithm for maintaining a $(Δ+1)$ vertex coloring of a fully dynamic graph against an adaptive adversary with $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})$ amortized update time. Our algorithm improves upon the recent randomized algorithms of Behnezhad, Rajaraman, and Wasim (SODA 2025) with $\tilde{O}(n^{8/9})$ and Flin and Halldórsson (ICALP 2025) with $\tilde{O}(n^{2/3})$ amortized update time. Our result (nearly) matches a fundamental limit of $O(\sqrt{n})$ update time identified by Flin and Halldórsson and settles their main open question. The previous algorithms' main improvement came from efficient coloring of the dense parts of the graph. Our approach complements these prior work by handling the sparse parts more efficiently, using ideas inspired by the recently developed dynamic entropy compression method. A key new aspect of our work is to tailor this method specifically to dynamic algorithms---as opposed to original local search algorithms---and achieve update times that can be sublinear in the number of involved constraints per update.

☆ Fully Dynamic All-Pairs Min Cuts on Planar Graphs via Planar Pattern Emulators
Arnold Filtser, Gramoz Goranci, Ali Momeni, Neel Patel, Maximilian Probst Gutenberg
We study the fully-dynamic all-pairs minimum cut problem on planar graphs. Given an $n$-vertex undirected planar graph $G=(V,E)$ undergoing edge insertions and deletions, the goal is to efficiently process these updates while supporting $s$-$t$ minimum cut queries for any pair of vertices $s$ and $t$. We give a $(1+\varepsilon)$-approximate dynamic algorithm that supports edge updates and minimum cut queries in $n^{o(1)}$ time, for any $1/\text{poly}(\log n) < \varepsilon < 1$. For general graphs, previous results either are limited to partially dynamic settings or achieve only logarithmic-factor approximations. The main conceptual and technical contribution is to show that a suitable adaptation of the planar emulator of Chang, Krauthgamer, and Tan~[STOC'22] satisfies the stronger pattern emulator introduced by Chen and Tan~[ICALP'25]. This leads almost immediately to a nearly linear-time construction of pattern emulators, which, when combined with the recent fully dynamic $(1+\varepsilon)$-approximate all-pairs shortest paths algorithm of Filtser, Goranci, Patel, and Probst Gutenberg~[FOCS'24], yields our result.
☆ ExplainCC: Explaining Correlated Clusters by Node Labels CIKM 2026
Nadeesha Shyami Kumari Herath Mudiyanselage, Florian Adriaens, Nikolaj Tatti
An increasingly important requirement for clustering algorithms is that decisions and outcomes need to be clearly understandable for human users. We address this requirement for the Correlation Clustering (CC) problem, in case the objects to be clustered are accompanied by labels. In CC one seeks to partition a set of objects into clusters based on pairwise similarity and dissimilarity information. Our proposed model only considers clusters that can be explained by a label tree, which is essentially a decision tree that partitions the objects according to labels. The goal is to find an explainable partition of the data that minimizes the CC objective function. We formalize this problem from a theoretical viewpoint, and prove several hardness of approximation results, also when constraining the size of the tree. Our proposed greedy method finds locally optimal splits, and works recursively in a top-down approach. To efficiently compute the best split, we carefully maintain certain counters. An experimental evaluation demonstrates its effectiveness. The proposed algorithm processes graphs with over million edges in a few minutes, can find the ground truth in synthetic data, and is able to produce interpretable clusters in real world networks.

☆ Order Maintenance in Worst-Case Constant Time, Verified
Denis Firsov
Order maintenance asks for a data structure that keeps a sequence of elements under insertions and deletions and tells which of two elements comes first. We give an algorithm, assembled from published components, that performs each operation in worst-case constant time, and verify it in Lean 4. The algorithm is a program for a random-access machine deeply embedded in Lean, whose interpreter counts every instruction. We prove that the program answers every query as a simple reference specification does, that each operation takes at most a fixed number of steps, independent of the number of elements and of the history, and that after $t$ operations no value it has computed has more than $O(\log t)$ bits, so it runs unchanged on a machine with words of that size. A verified translation that preserves step counts exactly connects the high-level proofs to the machine program, so the interpreter is the only cost model to trust. An unverified native implementation shows the algorithm's practical cost.
☆ Estimating the number of nodes and paths in a DAG by following a random path
Günter Rote
We investigate a simple method to compute an unbiased estimate of the number of nodes of a directed acyclic graph (DAG) by following a random directed path. This method is due to Leonard Pitt from 1987, and it generalizes a method of D. E. Knuth from the 1960s for estimating the size of a rooted tree. A slight variation makes it possible to estimate the number of source-sink paths. We extend the algorithm to use many "agents" that walk cooperatively through the graph. This extension unifies and generalizes several methods that have been proposed for approximately counting the nodes of a tree.

☆ Optimal Encodings for Next/Previous Larger/Smaller Value Queries: A Unified Approach
Seungbum Jo, Geunho Kim, Kunihiko Sadakane, Srinivasa Rao Satti
Given a 1D array over a total order and a query position in the array, a next larger value query returns the nearest position to the right whose value is larger than the value at the query position. Next smaller value queries and previous larger/smaller value queries are defined analogously. These queries are closely related to the $k$-th range minimum/maximum problem, which asks for the position of the $k$-th leftmost minimum/maximum value when the query range contains multiple minimum/maximum values. In this paper, we consider the encoding problem for answering any arbitrary subfamily of these four queries, where the queries must be answered without accessing the input array. We give a unified approach for obtaining space lower bounds for such encodings. We also obtain data structures for the query subfamilies that match the lower bounds up to lower-order additive terms, while supporting the queries in constant time. Our results generalize and improve several previous results on this problem [Gawrychowski and Nicholson, ICALP-2015; Jo and Satti, TCS-2016; Tsur, IPL-2019; Jo and Kim, Algorithmica-2025]. In addition, these results give tight bounds on the encoding complexity of all subfamilies of these queries for (1) permutations, (2) arrays without consecutive equal elements, and (3) general arrays.
☆ Averaged Annealed Markov Chains Achieve the Algorithmic Threshold for Tensor PCA, Part II: Langevin Dynamics
Aniket Das, Tselil Schramm
We consider the tensor principal components analysis (tensor PCA) problem on the sphere, in which we are asked to recover an unknown vector in $\mathcal{S}_N=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^N:\lVert x\rVert^2=N\}$ from a noisy observation of its $k$-th tensor power, for a fixed $k\ge3$. Tensor PCA exhibits a well-studied information-computation gap: global algorithms such as spectral methods and sum-of-squares work above an algorithmic threshold, below which we know matching lower bounds (against these and some other restricted models of computation). In contrast, local algorithms such as Markov chains were only known to succeed from a random initialization at a polynomially larger signal-to-noise ratio, and were believed to be inferior to global algorithms for tensor PCA. Continuing the program initiated in Part I [arXiv:2610.16705], we show that spherical Langevin dynamics, when supplemented with annealing and ergodic averaging, achieves polynomial-time strong recovery above the algorithmic threshold from a uniformly random initialization. Together with Part I, our results demonstrate that local Markov chains can succeed all the way down to the algorithmic threshold from an uninformative cold start.

☆ Fast Sparse $α$-Navigable Graph Construction with Improved Resource Augmentation
Alex Conway, Laxman Dhulipala, Martin Farach-Colton, Ben Landrum, Christopher Musco, Yarin Shechter, Torsten Suel, Richard Wen
Graph-based nearest neighbor search methods, which run greedy search on a directed graph over the data points, have surged in popularity in recent years. A common goal is to construct a search graph with the $α$-navigability property, which ensures that greedy search returns provably good approximate nearest neighbors and converges in few iterations. Increasing $α\geq 1$ improves accuracy and convergence rate, but requires more edges, which increases both space and search time. This tradeoff has motivated the study of algorithms for constructing a sparsest $α$-navigable graph for a given set of points. For any point set and any distance function, we show how to compute an $α$-navigable graph with at most $O(\log n)$ times as many edges as the sparsest $(1+\varepsilon)α$-navigable graph, in $O(n^ω\log(Δ)/\varepsilon)$ time, where $ω< 2.372$ is the matrix multiplication exponent and $Δ$ is the aspect ratio of the input. This improves on Khanna, Padaki, and Waingarten (SODA 2026), who achieve the same runtime but are only competitive with the sparsest $2(1+\varepsilon)α$-navigable graph. Since sparsity often increases rapidly with $α$ (typically $α\leq 1.1$), their bound can be significantly weaker. Previously, the best algorithm competing with the sparsest $(1+\varepsilon)α$-navigable graph required $\widetilde{O}(n^{2.5})$ time. Our techniques also extend to constructing sparse $τ$-monotonic graphs, another popular target in nearest neighbor search. Finally, we give a fine-grained complexity analysis suggesting that improving the $O(n^ω)$ running time is unlikely, or would require a very different approach. It is based on hardness results for problems related to verifying whether a graph is navigable, a topic of independent interest.

☆ Separations between $\#\mathrm{BIS}$ and $\#\mathrm{IS}$
Ijay Narang
We give complexity-theoretic separations between $\#\mathrm{BIS}$ and $\#\mathrm{IS}$. Given a graph $G$, and a fugacity $λ>0$, the hard-core model is given by $Z_G(λ)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal I(G)}λ^{|I|}$, where $\mathcal I(G)$ denotes the independent sets of $G$. For $F\ge1$, an $F$-factor approximation to $Z_G(λ)$ is an estimate $\widehat Z$ satisfying $F^{-1}Z_G(λ)\le\widehat Z\le FZ_G(λ)$. Let $\mathcal G_Δ$ denote the class of $Δ$-regular graphs and $\mathcal G_{Δ,Δ}$ the class of $Δ$-regular bipartite graphs. We give two separation results. (1) There exist an integer $Δ\ge3$, a sufficiently large fugacity $λ$, and a constant $c>0$ so that the following hold: (a) For every $δ\in(0,1)$, deterministic $(1+δ)$-factor approximation on $\mathcal G_{Δ,Δ}$ is possible in time $\exp(cn)\operatorname{poly}(n,1/δ)$. (b) Under the Gap-Exponential Time Hypothesis, there exists no deterministic algorithm running in time $\exp(2cn)\operatorname{poly}(n)$ on $\mathcal G_Δ$ that gives an $\frac{11}{10}$-factor approximation. (2) For every fixed $Δ\ge3$ and $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, for all sufficiently large fugacities $λ$, the following hold: (a) There exists an algorithm which outputs a deterministic $(1+\varepsilon)^n$-factor approximation to $Z_G(λ)$ on $\mathcal G_{Δ,Δ}$ in polynomial time. (b) Under the assumption $\mathrm P\ne\mathrm{NP}$, there exists no deterministic polynomial-time algorithm on $\mathcal G_Δ$ that gives a $(1+\varepsilon)^n$-factor approximation. Both our algorithmic results follow from a new spanning-tree encoding of independent sets that enables an efficient representation of large independent sets.
☆ On Worst-Case Guarantees for Graph-Based Nearest-Neighbor Search
Majid Daliri, Mohammadreza Daneshvaramoli, Purna Dutta, Edo Liberty, Cameron Musco, Christopher Musco, Torsten Suel
Graph-based nearest neighbor search algorithms like HNSW, DiskANN, NSG, and others have surged in popularity in recent years due to their excellent empirical performance in comparison to space-partitioning methods like IVF data structures and Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH). However, in contrast to LSH and related methods, obtaining strong worst-case theoretical guarantees for graph-based search has proven challenging. In this paper, we prove a strong lower bound that helps explain the lack of progress towards such guarantees. We show that no standard graph-based method (any that runs a variant of greedy search on a flat or hierarchical graph) can achieve sublinear query time for approximate nearest neighbor search in Euclidean space, even for an arbitrarily large approximation factor. On the other hand, we prove that sublinear query time is possible if we allow for Steiner points in the search graph. We obtain bounds comparable to LSH, showing e.g., that graph-based search with Steiner points can solve $c$-approximate nearest neighbor search in Euclidean space with query time $\tilde{O}(n^{1/(c-1)})$. We complement this result with initial experiments exploring how Steiner points can be incorporated into practical graph-based methods.
☆ Output-Sensitive Enumeration of Maximal Closed Substrings in Linear Space
Johannes Fischer, Dominik Köppl
We revisit the output-sensitive enumeration of maximal closed substrings (MCSs). Given a string of length $n$ over an integer alphabet, let $k$ denote its number of MCS occurrences. We combine a right-to-left sweep on the string with range-successor queries to obtain all MCSs with two deterministic word-RAM bounds: for any fixed $\varepsilon>0$, we can report all MCSs in $O(n\sqrt{\log n}+k\log^\varepsilon n)$ time using $O(n)$ words of space, or in $O(n\sqrt{\log n}+k\log\log n)$ time using $O(n\log\log n)$ words. Both bounds include preprocessing and report occurrences by their endpoints. In particular, these algorithms improve the known $O(n\log n)$ worst-case time bound for sufficiently small output.
♻ ☆ Group Isomorphism and the Polylogarithmic-Time Hierarchy: Depth-2$\frac{1}{2}$ Circuits and Lower Bounds
Joshua A. Grochow, Gülce Kardeş, Michael Levet
In this paper, we investigate the low-depth circuit complexity of Group Isomorphism in the multiplication (Cayley) table model. We prove the first circuit lower bounds for Group Isomorphism: namely, we show that every family of depth-$2$ Boolean circuits deciding Group Isomorphism requires quasipolynomial-size. (We also extend this to a limited class of depth-3 circuits, namely $\textsf{CNF}$-of-$\textsf{XOR}$s.) We complement this with upper bounds of uniform depth-$2\frac{1}{2}$ circuits of quasipolynomial-size: depth 3 circuits with poly-logarithmic bottom fan-in. A sequence of previous results from 1970-2025 progressively reduced the circuit depth from polynomial to $3\frac{1}{2}$; all of these results relied on the generator-enumerator strategy and, in fact, applied more generally to quasigroups. In contrast, our depth-$2\frac{1}{2}$ construction follows a fundamentally different strategy that exploits structure more specific to groups. We guess a composition series for each group, together with generators for its terms and the isomorphism types of its composition factors. We then inductively verify that the corresponding extensions at each level of the two composition series are compatible. A central part in this approach brings to bear the extensive work on the Short Presentation Conjecture, in tandem with the algorithmic theory of group extensions and cohomology.

♻ ☆ A Logic-based Algorithmic Meta-Theorem for Treedepth: Single Exponential FPT Time and Polynomial Space
Benjamin Bergougnoux, Vera Chekan, Giannos Stamoulis
For a graph $G$, the parameter treedepth measures the minimum depth among all forests $F$, called elimination forests, such that $G$ is a subgraph of the ancestor-descendant closure of $F$. We introduce a logic, called neighborhood operator logic with acyclicity, connectivity and clique constraints ($\mathsf{NEO}_2[\mathsf{FRec}]+\mathsf{ACK}$ for short), that captures all NP-hard problems like Independent Set or Hamiltonian Cycle that are known to be tractable in time $2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}$ and space $n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}$ on $n$-vertex graphs provided with elimination forests of depth $k$. We provide a model checking algorithm for $\mathsf{NEO}_2[\mathsf{FRec}]+\mathsf{ACK}$ with such complexity that unifies and extends these results. For $\mathsf{NEO}_2[\mathsf{FRec}]+\mathsf{k}$, the fragment of the above logic that does not use acyclicity and connectivity constraints, we get a strengthening of this result, where the space complexity is reduced to $\mathcal{O}(k\log(n))$. With a similar mechanism as the distance neighborhood logic introduced in [Bergougnoux, Dreier and Jaffke, SODA 2023], the logic $\mathsf{NEO}_2[\mathsf{FRec}]+\mathsf{ACK}$ is an extension of the fully-existential $\mathsf{MSO}_2$ with predicates for (1) querying generalizations of the neighborhoods of vertex sets, (2) verifying the connectivity and acyclicity of vertex and edge sets, and (3) verifying that a vertex set induces a clique. Our results provide $2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}$ time and $n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}$ space algorithms for problems for which the existence of such algorithms was previously unknown. In particular, $\mathsf{NEO}_2[\mathsf{FRec}]$ captures CNF-SAT via the incidence graphs associated to CNF formulas, and it also captures several modulo counting problems like Odd Dominating Set.

♻ ☆ Quantum lower bounds for convex optimization and real matrix-vector query problems
Andrew M. Childs
We (the author and the AI systems that did the heavy lifting) show that the quantum query complexity of minimizing a convex function over a convex subset of $\mathbb{R}^n$ with evaluation and membership queries is $\tildeΩ(n)$, nearly matching the best known upper bound. In particular, we show this even for quadratic minimization, which is equivalent to inverting an $n \times n$ real matrix using matrix-vector queries. We also show linear or nearly linear lower bounds on the quantum query complexity of computing the trace, the sign of the determinant, and the magnitude of the determinant of a real matrix in the matrix-vector query model. We use a novel quantum lower bound technique, the determinantal witness method, based on identifying a witness whose Fourier transform vanishes on low-rank matrices and that correlates well with the function being computed.

♻ ☆ Deterministic Streaming Lower Bounds for Approximate Maximum Clique and Maximum Independent Set
Adithya Diddapur
We study the canonical \textsf{Maximum Clique} and \textsf{Maximum Independent Set} problems in the one-pass edge-arrival graph streaming setting. Here, the edges of some input graph $G = (V,E)$ are presented one at a time (possibly including deletions), before an algorithm needs to produce either a large clique or independent set at the end of the stream, with the focus being on space complexity. We are interested in finding $β$-approximate solutions, for any $β\geq 1$. Previous work gave an algorithm using $\tilde{O}\left(n^2/β^2\right)$ bits of space, together with a corresponding $\tildeΩ\left(n^2/β^2\right)$ two-party communication lower bound [Halldórsson et al., ICALP'12], seeming to resolve the problem. However, their algorithm crucially relies on randomness, and the best known deterministic algorithm remains a folklore derandomisation using $O\left(n^2/β\right)$ bits of space, leaving a (deterministic) gap of size $\tilde{O}(β)$. We make progress towards this gap with a strong lower bound for a restricted range of $β$: whenever $β\leq \sqrt{n}/36\log n$, any deterministic algorithm for either problem must use $Ω\left(\frac{n^2}{β\cdot\log n}\right)$ bits of space. Our proof is via a two-party one-way communication lower bound, and highlights the power of randomness when approaching either of these problems.

♻ ☆ Adaptive Allele-Haplotype Incidence Indexing for Pangenome Queries: Representation, Reconstruction, and Benchmarking
Roberto Garrone
Population-scale analysis of phased genetic variants requires both allele-carrier retrieval and ordered haplotype context. We examine H1, an adaptive allele-haplotype incidence-row index, and H2, a canonical edge-path representation derived from a fixed, nonoverlapping variant catalog.
