♻ ☆ Logarithmic Separation between LZ77 and LZ-End and Grammar Compression
Philip Bille, Inge Li Gørtz, Rikke Jessen, Jingxun Li, Simon Rumle Tarnow, Renfei Zhou
We show that there is a logarithmic separation between LZ77 and LZ-End, and between LZ77 and grammar compression.
♻ ☆ The Polynomial-Time Low-Degree Conjecture is False
Songtao Mao
The low-degree method and its associated lower bounds are widely used to guide algorithm design and to provide evidence of computational hardness in average-case inference, high-dimensional statistics, random optimization, and related problems. This led to the low-degree conjecture, which predicts that when the low-degree advantage between a planted distribution and a uniform null distribution remains bounded, no efficient distinguisher can succeed after independent noise, provided that the planted distribution has permutation symmetry. Several works have produced counterexamples to variants of this conjecture or to versions for algorithms with higher time complexity, but the conjecture remained open in its standard binary, polynomial-time formulation. We disprove the polynomial-time low-degree conjecture by giving a family of examples. For every fixed integer $r\geq3$, we construct a permutation-invariant distribution $\mathbb{P}_n$ on simple graphs, with $\mathbb{Q}_n=G(n,1/2)$, such that every marginal of $\mathbb{P}_n$ on at most $D_n=Θ((\log n)^{r-1})$ edges is uniform. Therefore, the low-degree advantage is zero through degree $D_n$. Nevertheless, after every edge is independently resampled at a fixed positive rate, a deterministic rank test strongly distinguishes the resulting distribution from $\mathbb{Q}_n$ in linear time in the graph input size. The construction chooses a subspace of a Reed--Muller code whose nonzero polynomials have small absolute bias, selects points whose evaluation vectors have no short linear dependencies, and evaluates a random alternating bilinear form on pairs of these vectors. Our result shows that low-degree indistinguishability, a uniform null distribution, permutation invariance, and independent resampling do not by themselves imply polynomial-time hardness, and suggests that a valid general conjecture must impose an additional condition.

♻ ☆ On the Role of Normalization in Binary Iterative Hard Thresholding for 1-bit Compressed Sensing
Arya Mazumdar, Prateeti Mukherjee
Binary Iterative Hard Thresholding (BIHT) is a simple, yet effective, greedy method for recovering a sparse vector from one-bit sign measurements. In its original form, BIHT performs a ``gradient-descent'' step, followed by hard thresholding. A convergence analysis of this algorithm was left open in the introductory work of Jacques, Laska, Boufounos, and Baraniuk [IEEE Trans. Inform. Theory (2013)] and has remained unresolved for over a decade, with subsequent sharp analyses studying a normalized variant instead, that additionally projects every iterate onto the unit sphere. This paper resolves that gap and characterizes when per-iterate normalization is algorithmically necessary. In the noiseless setting, we prove a universal, sample-optimal convergence theorem for the original BIHT algorithm. Specifically, with $\widetilde O(s/ε)$ measurements, a deterministic, computable finite-time iterate has directional error at most $ε$, simultaneously for every $s$-sparse unit vector. This matches the optimal sample dependence achieved by normalized BIHT in prior work. Under sign corruptions, we prove a sharp separation. If at most a $τ$ fraction of signs are flipped adversarially, then BIHT, without per-iterate normalization, still reaches the robust error floor at an early iterate with a matching $\widetilde O(s/ε)$ sample complexity rate as its normalized variant. This recovery, however, is not stable. We prove a scalar lower bound showing that any nontrivial corruption pattern, even one that involves only one flipped sign together with one clean sign, forces the iterates to oscillate indefinitely. Consequently, no general last-iterate convergence theorem can hold for BIHT under sign corruptions, while its normalized surrogate provably escapes this instance.
comment: SODA 2027, to appear
♻ ☆ Density Decomposition on Hypergraphs
Xiaoyu Leng, Hongchao Qin, Rong-Hua Li, Guoren Wang
Hypergraphs capture higher-order interactions involving multiple entities. Existing decomposition models, such as $k$-core and nbr-$k$-core, rely on degree or neighborhood constraints and may overlook fine-grained density variations. We propose a $(k,δ)$-dense subhypergraph model based on capped hyperedge contributions. For each parameter pair, the unique dense set is the inclusionwise smallest maximizer of a penalized capped hyperedge contribution. These dense sets form a nested hierarchy characterized by egalitarian $δ$-orientations and hypergraph reachability. We develop three algorithms for dense subhypergraph search and two for complete density decomposition. Both decomposition algorithms use integral flow balancing: Flow-Decomp extracts density levels through strongly connected component condensation, while Reverse-Decomp uses direct reverse propagation. Once an egalitarian orientation is obtained, both recover all density levels in linear time. We further derive sandwich bounds that restrict computation to equivalent residual subproblems and develop incremental algorithms for maintaining the hierarchy under hyperedge insertions and deletions. Experiments on nine real-world hypergraphs, including datasets with over 15 million vertices, compare our methods with four established decomposition models. The results show that Reverse-Decomp improves decomposition efficiency, incremental maintenance outperforms recomputation in the tested settings, and the proposed model captures density structures overlooked by degree-based decompositions.

♻ ☆ Sublinear Algorithms for Estimating Single-Linkage Clustering Costs
Pan Peng, Christian Sohler, Yi Xu
Single-linkage clustering (SLC) is a fundamental method for hierarchical data analysis. In the distance setting, a $k$-clustering produced by SLC can be obtained by computing a minimum spanning tree (MST) and deleting its $k-1$ heaviest edges. This naturally induces a cost profile for the SLC hierarchy: for each $k\in[n]$, we define $\mathrm{cost}_k$ to be the weight of the resulting $k$-component spanning forest, equivalently, the minimum total weight of any spanning forest with exactly $k$ connected components. The corresponding \emph{SLC cost profile} is $(\mathrm{cost}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{cost}_n)$, and the scalar quantity $\mathrm{cost}(G)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{cost}_k$ is the area under this profile. We study the problem of approximating these quantities in sublinear time. We assume that the input is a weighted graph $G$ of average degree $d$ with edge weights in $\{1,\dots,W\}$, accessed through adjacency-list queries; missing edges are treated as having infinite distance. Our main result is a sampling-based algorithm that outputs a succinct sketch of the entire SLC cost profile in the distance setting. The algorithm runs in $\widetilde{O}(d\sqrt{W}/\varepsilon^3)$ time and returns a sketch from which one can derive estimates $(\widehat{\mathrm{cost}}_1,\ldots,\widehat{\mathrm{cost}}_n)$ satisfying $\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{cost}}_k-\mathrm{cost}_k\bigr| \le \varepsilon\,\mathrm{cost}(G)$. Thus, we obtain an $\ell_1$ approximation to the full profile whose error is at most an $\varepsilon$-fraction of the area under the true profile. In particular, this yields a $(1\pm\varepsilon)$-approximation to $\mathrm{cost}(G)$ within the same running time. We also prove a nearly matching lower bound of $Ω(d\sqrt{W}/\varepsilon^2)$ queries for estimating $\mathrm{cost}(G)$.

☆ Self-Consistency Geometry Injection for Multi-view Consistent Image Generation ACCV 2026
Yupeng Xiao, Yankai Wu, Jianjie Luo, Jiajie Lin, Yi Yu, Zhenguo Yang
Recent diffusion models have achieved impressive progress in single image to multi-view generation by synthesizing consistent novel views efficiently. Existing methods usually explicitly or implicitly couple geometry inference and texture synthesis, making generation vulnerable to inaccurate geometry prediction. Although several geometry-guided methods have explored decoupling, they usually treat predicted geometry as a fixed condition and do not explicitly model whether the geometry is reliable. We therefore propose Reliability-Guided Multi-View Generation (RGMV), which comprises a parameter-free Self-Consistency Geometry Gate (SCG) and a Reliability-aware Geometry Injection (RGI) module. Specifically, a geometry proposal generator produces multiple geometry predictions for the same reference image and target viewpoints under different stochastic sampling configurations. SCG measures their spatial agreement to derive a reliability map, which RGI uses to modulate the injection strength of geometry-conditioned residuals in the RGB synthesis model. Moreover, RGMV can adapt to different geometry proposal generators. As a result, RGMV preserves stronger geometric guidance in stable regions while attenuating unreliable geometry cues before they propagate into multi-view RGB generation. Extensive experiments show that reliability-aware geometry injection improves the geometric quality of multi-view generation. Code: https://github.com/WISLab-GDUT/RGMV.

☆ Neural Explicit Shape Representation
Jun-Hao Wang, Changan Lu, Baoquan Chen, Peng-Shuai Wang
Existing neural 3D representations separately address continuous surface modeling, efficient image synthesis, or object-specific compression. They do not jointly provide the explicit surface queries required by ray tracing and a structured output space that can be predicted across objects. We introduce NeuOctree, a neural explicit representation that directly predicts whether and where a ray intersects a surface, together with its normal and albedo. NeuOctree stores shared features at the corners of sparse octree voxels and interpolates them into a continuous local field. A lightweight decoder with recurrent intersection-guided sampling refines each query near the predicted surface, enabling direct surface rendering without mesh extraction or texture baking and accommodating open, non-watertight geometry. The same octree structure supports both compact per-object optimization and a shared octree CNN that constructs renderable assets from colored point clouds without per-object fitting. Across seven meshes and four storage budgets, NeuOctree achieves the lowest positional error in 24 of 28 comparisons while retaining competitive normal accuracy, and substantially improves joint geometry--appearance reconstruction over N-BVH and VQAD. The feedforward model constructs a representation in 0.05 seconds and transfers from Objaverse to ShapeNet without retraining. We further demonstrate ray-traced rendering and image-conditioned generation, showing that NeuOctree provides a common output space for neural inference and direct surface rendering. Code is available at https://github.com/octree-nn/neural-octree.
☆ 3DCodeEdit: Benchmarking Instruction-Guided 3D Program Editing
Binjie Wang, Xinran Zhao, Shoubin Yu, Xiang Li, Silong Yong, Chenyang Yang, Tianyu Chen, Ruofei Du, He He, Chen Zhao, Tongshuang Wu
Language models increasingly create 3D content through code, with practical workflows often relying on editing existing programs rather than regenerating them, for speed and precise control. Such edits require understanding what a program builds and foreseeing how a change reshapes it, yet prior benchmarks mostly evaluate generation or simple edits against a reference. In this paper, we introduce 3DCodeEdit, a benchmark of 2,592 instruction-guided editing tasks over 108 Blender programs that construct objects entirely in code. Its edits range from parameter to structural changes, style redesign, and scene composition, and are judged from renders against human-reviewed checklists. Across 17 models, the average success rate is only 37%, and even the strongest models complete fewer than 60% of structural and style edits. As models become more capable, their failures shift to geometric foresight, where the code runs but builds the wrong shape. We therefore study whether multi-turn feedback can compensate for this missing foresight. Feedback from a separate reviewer model that describes how the renders differ from the request helps most, more than maximum reasoning effort or an agent harness, and can let a 27B model surpass the single-shot rate of a 2.8T-parameter model.
☆ HyperIntent: Tracking Intent Shifts for Efficient Reasoning on Dynamic Textual Graphs
Yilin Yao, Bin Lu, Xinhui Li, Meng Jin, Xinbing Wang
Dynamic web interactions continuously evolve as new edges arrive, offering large language models (LLMs) great potential for reasoning over edge-level temporal semantics. However, continuously arriving edges are often repetitive, obscuring the key structures that truly reflect temporal changes and limiting the efficiency of LLMs. A key insight is that beneath the dense stream of edge arrivals lies a sparse evolution of temporal intent, enabling LLMs to be responsively triggered only when such intent shifts, while otherwise inheriting the previous state. To this end, we propose a hypergraph-based framework named HyperIntent, which enhances dynamic graph reasoning by parsing differentiated intents, quantifying their temporal discrepancies, and performing shift-triggered semantic updating. Experimental results on destination node retrieval and future link prediction demonstrate that HyperIntent consistently achieves the best overall performance, with average relative Hits@1 gains of 34.0% and 25.4% over vanilla TGAT and DyGFormer, respectively. Meanwhile, HyperIntent reduces LLM token consumption by 54.4%-95.9% across six datasets while achieving leading predictive performance. In-depth studies further confirm the necessity of shift triggering for identifying structural and semantic temporal changes. The code will be made publicly available upon completion of the review process.
♻ ☆ MeshSuite: A Unified Benchmark for Triangle- and Mesh-Based Neural Rendering
Kaixuan Zhang, Minxian Li, Mingwu Ren, Xiatian Zhu
Triangle- and mesh-based neural rendering seeks to bridge neural representations and graphics engines through triangle primitives compatible with standard rasterization. Yet existing methods were developed and evaluated under inconsistent settings, with few direct comparisons and little evaluation in graphics engines. We introduce MeshSuite, the first benchmark for systematic evaluation of engine deployment and Non-Engine Render in triangle- and mesh-based neural rendering. MeshSuite evaluates two deployment settings: Standard Deployment, using an opaque mesh pipeline with vertex colors and hardware Z-buffering; and Dedicated Deployment, using method-specific engine implementations to preserve appearance and compositing features. For mesh splatting, we also audit exported surfaces for topological and geometric integrity as downstream graphics assets. Our results show that engine deployment reduces image quality in both settings; among evaluated methods, MeshSplatting degrades least under Standard Deployment. Dedicated Deployment preserves most fidelity at roughly 5-33x the runtime of Standard Deployment across two deployment datasets. For mesh splatting, explicit connectivity and shared indexing remain limited: shared vertex indexing alone guarantees neither manifoldness nor global connectivity. These findings show that rasterizability is only part of graphics readiness and underscore the need to assess engine deployment. Project page: https://x-up-lab.github.io/research/meshsuite.
♻ ☆ Evaluation of Gauss-Legendre curves
Filip Chudy, Paweł Woźny
We present new representations of Gauss--Legendre polynomials and their derivatives in the shifted power basis and in bases related to symmetric orthogonal Jacobi polynomials. Using these representations and certain recurrence relations, we propose $O(n^2+dn)$ methods for evaluating a Gauss--Legendre curve of degree $n$ in $\mathbb E^d$. We also propose algorithms for multipoint evaluation with computational complexity $O(Mdn+dn^2)$, where $M$ is the number of evaluation points. Numerical tests show that the approach using the shifted power basis is very fast, but it works well only for small $n$ (approximately up to 20), whereas the method employing Jacobi polynomials gives good numerical results even for large $n$ (around 100).
♻ ☆ Fast subdivision of Bézier curves
Paweł Woźny, Filip Chudy
It is well-known that a $d$-dimensional polynomial Bézier curve of degree $n$ can be subdivided into two segments using the famous de Casteljau algorithm in $O(dn^2)$ time. We show that it is possible to do this in $O(dn\log{n})$ time using the fast Fourier transform and its inverse. The direct application of the method is numerically unstable for larger values of $n$. Therefore, we also introduce a modified version based on parameter scaling, which retains the same asymptotic computational complexity. This scaling approach can improve numerical accuracy in some cases (for $n\lesssim 50$), as demonstrated by our experiments. Moreover, the new method has a nice property: if a Bézier curve is extended by an additional control point, the subdivision can be updated in $O(d)$ time. The method can be adapted to subdivide rational Bézier curves and rectangular Bézier surfaces, as well as to compute the derivatives of Bézier curves. Although motivated by applications in CAGD, the proposed methods may also be useful in other areas involving polynomial reparameterization.
♻ ☆ X2HDR: HDR Image Generation in a Perceptually Uniform Space NeurIPS 2026
Ronghuan Wu, Wanchao Su, Kede Ma, Jing Liao, Rafał K. Mantiuk
High-dynamic-range (HDR) formats and displays are becoming increasingly prevalent, yet state-of-the-art image generators (e.g., Stable Diffusion and FLUX) typically remain limited to low-dynamic-range (LDR) output due to the lack of large-scale HDR training data. In this work, we show that existing pretrained diffusion models can be easily adapted to HDR generation without retraining from scratch. A key challenge is that HDR images are natively represented in linear RGB, whose intensity and color statistics differ substantially from those of sRGB-encoded LDR images. This gap, however, can be effectively bridged by converting HDR inputs into perceptually uniform encodings (e.g., using PU21 or PQ). Empirically, we find that LDR-pretrained variational autoencoders (VAEs) reconstruct PU21-encoded HDR inputs with fidelity comparable to LDR data, whereas linear RGB inputs cause severe degradations. Motivated by this finding, we describe an efficient adaptation strategy that freezes the VAE and finetunes only the denoiser via low-rank adaptation in a perceptually uniform space. This results in a unified computational method that supports both text-to-HDR synthesis and single-image RAW-to-HDR reconstruction. Experiments demonstrate that our perceptually encoded adaptation consistently improves perceptual fidelity, text-image alignment, and effective dynamic range, relative to previous techniques.

♻ ☆ Turbo4DGen: Ultra-Fast Acceleration for 4D Generation ICML 2026
Yuanbin Man, Ying Huang, Zhile Ren, Miao Yin
4D generation, or dynamic 3D content generation, integrates spatial, temporal, and view dimensions to model realistic dynamic scenes, playing a foundational role in advancing world models and physical AI. However, maintaining long-chain consistency across both frames and viewpoints through the unique spatio-camera-motion (SCM) attention mechanism introduces substantial computational and memory overhead, often leading to out-of-memory (OOM) failures and prohibitive generation times. To address these challenges, we propose Turbo4DGen, an ultra-fast acceleration framework for diffusion-based multi-view 4D content generation. Turbo4DGen introduces a spatiotemporal cache mechanism that persistently reuses intermediate attention across denoising steps, combined with dynamically semantic-aware attention pruning and an adaptive SCM chain bypass scheduler, to drastically reduce redundant SCM attention computation. Our experimental results show that Turbo4DGen achieves an average 9.7$\times$ speedup without quality degradation on the ObjaverseDy and Consistent4D datasets. To the best of our knowledge, Turbo4DGen is the first dedicated acceleration framework for 4D generation.

☆ Agar: What-If Analysis for Microservice Applications
Saarth Deshpande, Neeraja J. Yadwadkar, Venkat Arun
Microservice operators must reason about how applications would behave under changes that have not yet occurred: different workloads, resource allocations, or SLO targets. Evaluating every alternative experimentally is expensive, but predicting such counterfactuals is difficult because microservices contain many interacting components. End-to-end models must learn behavior over a combinatorial space of conditions, but composing per-component predictors causes prediction errors to accumulate. We address this challenge by changing what each component model must predict. Rather than predicting exact values, we learn simple, interpretable bounds that contain every observed behavior while remaining tight enough to support useful end-to-end conclusions. We build AGAR, a counterfactual performance analysis tool that composes these models to answer end-to-end SLO-feasibility questions. This allows us to formulate autoscaling as a counterfactual query: what is the smallest resource allocation under which an SLO is met? Our evaluation demonstrates that across three microservice applications, AGAR's bounds are actionable: its derived autoscaler reduces CPU allocation by up to 36.8% over state-of-the-art horizontal autoscalers while satisfying application SLOs. Further, AGAR is able to predict operational cost given workload traces, provide guidance on which service's startup time to optimize and localize performance bugs within each application.

☆ USB Differentiated Service for Networked Cyber-Physical Systems
Anton Njavro, Rafiuddin Syed, Shriram Raja, Zhiyuan Ruan, Richard West
Cyber-physical systems are increasingly complex, requiring high throughput, low-latency data processing across multiple cores and compute devices. Additional functional safety challenges dictate the need for high system availability in the presence of failures. Together, these requirements are addressed using a network of compute nodes across which data processing, control and functional safety tasks are distributed. A key challenge is how to address the service requirements of independent data flows, spanning a set of hosts and I/O devices. In this paper, we show how to develop connectivity between hosts using a custom xDCI gadget driver that uses USB interrupters to differentiate the processing of separate data flows. Our solution is the first to handle both isochronous and bulk communication at USB 3.0 transfer rates, with predictable bus latencies down to the granularity of a 125 microsecond microframe, which is sufficient for many CPS control operations. We present an end-to-end solution that couples the predictable execution of user-level tasks with interrupt processing and data communication, so that multiple virtual connections are able to share a common bus. This work is not only applicable to host networking but also device I/O, which makes it arguably desirable for CPS scenarios requiring short-range communication, such as in vehicle control area networks or robotics.

☆ SLICE-CiM: Selective Layer-wise Inference Control Engine with Confidence-Based Early Exit for SRAM based Digital Compute-in-Memory
Pratham Sharma, Mohd Faisal Khan, Santosh Kumar Vishvakarma
Digital SRAM compute-in-memory (CIM) macros enable energy-efficient CNN inference by eliminating off-chip weight transfers; however, peripheral control logic introduces three inefficiencies: bit- slice over-activation at reduced precision (L1), unnecessary accumula- tion depth (L2), and idle precision-transition stalls (L3). Anthony Sánchez, Rafael Ruiz, José A. Belloch, Jesús O. Lacruz, Joerg Widmer

☆ Kernel Performance Engineering under Physical Non-Idealities
Supun Kuruppu, Pranav Dangi, Wei Li, Zhenyu Bai, Tulika Mitra
Physical non-idealities in integrated circuit (IC) design and fabrication are concealed by conventional hardware abstractions, yet they can limit kernel performance and obscure optimization opportunities. Using distributed shared memory (DSMEM) on NVIDIA H100 GPUs as a case study, we show how floorsweeping restricts hardware resources available for clustered execution, potentially offsetting the benefits of reduced data movement enabled by DSMEM. Guided by these insights, we develop a wave-aware clustering strategy that accounts for these resource constraints to improve kernel performance. Evaluations using a global-load-and-broadcast microbenchmark, GEMM, and the Hopper version of FlashAttention 4 demonstrate the performance implications of these constraints, with wave-aware clustering improving FlashAttention 4 throughput by up to 5.30% over its baseline. We further show that, under thermal design power (TDP) limits, reduced data movement enables higher Streaming Multiprocessor (SM) frequencies through Dynamic Voltage and Frequency Scaling (DVFS), explaining otherwise unexpected throughput improvements. Extending this analysis beyond GPUs, we show that power constraints also limit throughput scaling on Tenstorrent's Wormhole accelerator, highlighting the broader importance of accounting for physical non-idealities when analyzing and optimizing kernel performance.
☆ AnalogScout: Simulation-Efficient PVT-Aware Analog Sizing with LLM Agents ISCA
Yichen Long, Keren Zhu
Analog device sizing must satisfy all specifications across process, voltage, and temperature (PVT) corners to ensure tape-out success, but simulating every candidate at all corners dominates the sizing time and slows design iteration. Conventional surrogate-based optimizers search the sizing space directly, while recent large language model (LLM) agents add circuit knowledge to designs but still delegate the sizes to global optimizers. Both learn the circuit response from repeated multi-corner SPICE simulations and optimize on top of it, which becomes expensive under a limited simulation budget. Providing an agent with reusable verified designs and sizing sub-blocks analytically could save most of this budget. It also attains lower power than Bayesian optimization on all three OTAs and than two re-implemented LLM-driven flows on the two OTAs where they were tested.

☆ Hardware-Matched Spiking Neural Networks for Real-Time Purkinje Cell Spike Classification
Arooba Farhan, Afsah Syed, Muhammad Fahd Ibrahim Bhatti, Muhammad Areeb Khalid Kidwai, Muhammad Ahsan Salar Khan, Vincenzo Romano, Muhammad Ali Siddiqi

☆ p-DCIM: A 28nm Probabilistic SRAM DCIM based Ising Machine for Near-Memory Context Selection in LLM-RAG Pipeline
Madhav Vadlamani, Rahul Singh, Yuyao Kong, Haomei Liu, Zheng Zhang, Shimeng Yu
We present a fabricated SRAM DCIM Ising machine that performs set-level context selection for large language model (LLM) with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). In RAG, the evidence-selection stage is increasingly delegated to repeated language-model reranking, which adds substantial energy to the inference pipeline. We reformulate evidence selection as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem and solve it on a dual-mode digital compute-in-memory (DCIM) macro: the same multiply-and-accumulate datapath computes the embedding-similarity scores that populate the QUBO coefficients and evaluates the per-spin Hamiltonian differences that drive the anneal. The annealing temperature is set by a digital plane-count control along with the SRAM pseudo-read random bit flips, giving graded cooling entirely from on-chip digital configuration. The macro is taped-out in a 28 nm process [0.16 mm2, 0.41 mW] and is proposed to be integrated as part of the logic base-die in hybrid high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and high-bandwidth flash (HBF) stacks in a GPU package, where only the selected passage identifiers return to the host. Replacing the language-model selection pass with the near-memory Ising solver yields a macro normalized selection-energy estimate more than six orders of magnitude lower than the LLM-based baseline and reduces the evaluated total energy per query by 67%, while maintaining comparable token-level F1 on the evaluated examples.
☆ Comparative Analysis of Large Load Interconnection Requirements Across U.S. Transmission Entities
Rafi Zahedi, Rahul Anilkumar, Amin Zamani
The rapid growth of large loads, especially data centers driven by artificial intelligence, is creating unprecedented demands on the North American electric grid. Data centers now account for nearly 80% of large load interconnection requests in the Western Interconnection, and U.S. electricity consumption from data centers is projected to reach 325-580 TWh by 2028. Despite this urgency, technical interconnection requirements for large loads have not yet matured and still contain several gaps. This paper presents a comparative analysis of large load interconnection requirements across eleven U.S. entities, including AEP, Ameren, CAISO, Dominion, Duke Energy, ERCOT, LIPA, MISO, PJM, Southern Company, and SPP, under seven technical categories: large load definition, load data and modeling, fault ride-through, load curtailment and shedding, dispatchability, power quality, and reactive power control. The analysis reveals significant variation in both the scope and specificity of requirements. LIPA addresses all seven technical categories at least partially, while Dominion and Duke Energy provide detailed coverage across nearly all categories. The analysis also finds that data center applicants can be viewed as potential grid resources, rather than only as consumers. The findings provide a structured baseline from which utilities, regulators, ISOs/RTOs, and developers can identify gaps, benchmark against peers, and accelerate the development of interconnection standards.
☆ Silent Blindness: Fault Vulnerability of Hardware Malware Detectors
Alireza Abolhasani Zeraatkar, Parnian Shabani Kamran, Parsa Mirfasihi, Saleh Khalaj Monfared, Hussain Al-Asaad
Hardware malware detectors (HMDs) classify hardware performance-counter measurements to identify malicious execution. Faults in their decision logic or parameters can weaken detection and allow malware to be classified as benign. We investigate this vulnerability through structural fault injection across a design space comprising 18 classifiers, four counter budgets, and AdaBoost and Bagging ensembles. The simulation evaluates 453,174 decision stuck-at and selected parameter bit-flip events on a 7,400-instance. Primary comparisons use 128 completed configurations. These completed-subset results are distinguished from the simulation summaries covering 140 configurations with enumerable fault sites. Fault outcomes are classified by their security consequences, including evasion, false-alarm increases, and metric silence. A fault is critical when malware recall decreases by at least five percentage points. Tree-based models exhibit a lower mean critical-fault fraction than function-based models, 5.3% versus 14.3%; exhaustive-only analysis preserves this ordering. Moreover, 72.3% of evaluated tree faults are metric-silent, changing both recall and false-positive rate by less than 0.1 percentage points without necessarily preserving individual predictions. Among the 27 completed matched comparisons, Bagging reduces criticality in 26, whereas AdaBoost increases it in two. Finally, an exact minimax selection method identifies protection candidates under additive unit costs and ideal correction. For a representative J48 detector, protecting 16 of 115 structural units reduces predicted maximum recall loss from 60.1 to 3.3 percentage points. These results characterize structural-model sensitivity and potential protection benefits; faulted RTL correspondence and physical hardening overhead remain unvalidated.
☆ A State Augmented Approach to Paralle Modeling in High-Level Synthesis
Nicholas Kovacs, Hao Zheng
High-level synthesis (HLS) can schedule only the concurrency made legal by its input description. For feedback-coupled designs, a sequential behavioral model can obscure the architectural state boundaries and hazard resolution needed for efficient overlap. We develop an explicit architectural modeling methodology based on synchronous sequential processes that start in lockstep and synchronize completion before repeating. Persistent state, communication, and feedback control are represented in C++, while HLS schedules and binds operations within that structure. A five-stage RV32I processor illustrates three consequential modeling decisions: which version of a value a consumer reads, when it consumes that value, and how architectural memory actions map to port operations. On a Kintex UltraScale+ FPGA, the CPI-optimized configuration achieves 1.221 cycles per instruction at 220 MHz, compared with 4.000 for an algorithmic HLS baseline and 1.575 at 242 MHz for an Ibex reference. An area-optimized configuration reduces LUTs by 65\% and registers by 47\%, with 9.7\% more benchmark cycles and a 250 MHz achieved clock. These results demonstrate a separation between designer-controlled architecture and tool-controlled scheduling.
♻ ☆ Combinatorial Lock for Trojan-Resilient AI Engines via Hash-Assisted Spectral Filtering
Soumita Chatterjee, Sudip Ghosh, Tamal Ghosh, Bhargab B. Bhattacharya, Hafizur Rahaman
Multi-trigger backdoor attacks, in which heterogeneous trigger mechanisms (e.g., patch, blend, warping, and the latent-domain Spy-Watermark) coexist within a single poisoned model, cannot be addressed by existing shortcut-based defenses that assume a single, static attack mechanism. Current multi-trigger remedies (BU+FT, BU+ANP, and BU+ABL) rely on backdoor unlearning, which incurs high training costs and loses effectiveness at low poisoning ratios. This paper proposes Combinatorial Lock, a training-free, reference-calibrated defense that reduces multi-trigger identification to a combinatorial problem over binary activation signatures. This formulation is the key novelty: it yields a multi-trigger defense that is training-free, trigger-oblivious, and directly implementable in hardware, since its decision rules can be evaluated with integer and logic operations. The framework combines anomaly-gated spectral scrubbing, which removes the malicious subspace only from inputs identified as anomalous, with a hash-compressed combinatorial signature verified by an ensemble of detection experts, including a compound-attack-aware expert for multi-trigger inputs. A joint threshold-shrinkage search identifies an operating point ($τ$ at the 97.1st percentile, $γ= 0.78$) that reduces the attack success rate (ASR) to as low as 0.1% while preserving 97.8% clean accuracy. Evaluation on six models (ResNet18, VGG16, MobileNet, AlexNet, ViT-Small, ViT-Base) shows strong generalization: near-100% ASR drops to single digits across poisoning rates of 1-10% and 1-9 simultaneous triggers, with only gradual degradation in clean accuracy. ASIC and FPGA implementations confirm practicality, adding a consistent 19-20% latency overhead across all evaluated architectures and establishing Combinatorial Lock as a hardware-native approach to multi-trigger backdoor mitigation.

♻ ☆ DiffRouter: A Differentiable Routing Framework for UltraScale FPGAs
Xiaohan Gao, Zhili Xiong, Yusu Wang, David Z. Pan
Routing has become a major bottleneck in large-scale FPGA design flows, where traditional routers rely on sequential net-by-net processing and rip-up-reroute heuristics. This inherent sequentialism limits parallelism, leads to suboptimal utilization of routing resources, and significantly increases runtime on modern FPGAs. This paper introduces DiffRouter, the first differentiable, gradient-based FPGA routing framework with GPU acceleration. By lifting routing into a continuous optimization space, DiffRouter enables highly concurrent routing and improved global optimality before projecting solutions back onto the discrete fabric. The framework consists of three key stages: (1) Resource-pruned preprocessing, which extracts compact routing variables per net to reduce memory footprint and problem complexity; (2) Differentiable global routing via gradient descent, which formulates routing as a Lagrangian-relaxed optimization that minimizes wirelength while iteratively enforcing connectivity and congestion constraints as relaxed terms; and (3) Postprocessing, which maps the continuous wire distributions to a valid UltraScale routing solution and completes solutions by detailed routing. Experiments on the FPGA 2024 Routing Contest benchmarks show that DiffRouter outperforms state-of-the-art parallel routers, achieving over 5x speedup over RWRoute and 2x over Vivado, and running 18% faster than the state-of-the-art academic FPGA router Potter while delivering competitive critical path wirelength.
♻ ☆ Arapai: An Offline-First LLM Architecture for Adaptive Learning in Low-Connectivity Environments
Joseph Walusimbi, Ann Move Oguti, Joshua Benjamin Ssentongo, Keith Ainebyona
Artificial intelligence and large language models (LLMs) are transforming educational technology by enabling conversational tutoring, personalised explanations, and inquiry-driven learning. However, most AI-based learning systems rely on continuous internet connectivity and cloud-based computation, limiting their use in bandwidth-constrained environments. This paper presents Arapai, an offline-first large language model architecture designed for AI-assisted learning in low-connectivity settings. The system performs all inference locally using quantized language models and incorporates hardware-aware model selection to enable deployment on low-specification, CPU-only devices. By removing dependence on cloud infrastructure, the system provides curriculum-aligned explanations and structured academic support through natural-language interaction. To support learners at different educational stages, the system includes adaptive response levels that generate explanations at varying levels of complexity: Simple English, Lower Secondary, Upper Secondary, and Technical. The system was evaluated with 120 students and 9 instructors from secondary and tertiary institutions under limited-connectivity conditions. Results indicate stable operation on legacy hardware, acceptable response times of 1-3 seconds for typical queries, and positive user perceptions of its effectiveness in supporting self-directed learning.

♻ ☆ A Type System for Communication-Safe Hardware Design
Aditya Ranjan Jha, Jason Zhijingcheng Yu, Umang Mathur, Prateek Saxena
Hardware components communicate through clock-cycle-sensitive protocols. Hardware Description Languages (HDLs), however, provide limited support for expressing these protocols or for checking that independently developed components agree on them. When components disagree, the composed design can perform unsynchronized exchanges or stop making progress. We call these failures communication-safety violations. Communication safety is thus a global property, determined by interactions between components. This makes it difficult to check modularly. This paper presents Pact, a typed extension of Anvil for modular verification of communication safety in synchronous hardware. Pact types describe message-passing, control-flow, and clock-cycle behaviour. Each component is checked against its type. Compatible types guarantee that the components compose into a communication-safe system. Pact also supports incremental design. It can check a subsystem before the rest of the system exists. The check produces a summary type that captures the communication behaviour left for future compositions. To reduce annotation burden, Pact infers types by abstracting payload values while preserving communication, timing, control, and fairness. These inferred types are optimal for the checker: whenever some checker-accepted types can prove compatibility, the inferred types can prove it as well. Type inference and type checking are decidable, and compatibility is decided by a sound and complete algorithm. We implement Pact by extending the Anvil compiler and evaluate it along five dimensions: detecting communication errors in incremental design, expressiveness on ten clock-cycle-accurate translations of open-source hardware IPs, compatibility-checking time, generated hardware overhead, and annotation burden. Pact expresses realistic protocols and verifies all ten benchmarks in reasonable time.

♻ ☆ EdgeCIM: A Hardware-Software Co-Design for CIM-Based Acceleration of Small Language Models
Jinane Bazzi, Mariam Rakka, Fadi Kurdahi, Mohammed E. Fouda, Ahmed Eltawil
The growing demand for deploying Small Language Models (SLMs) on edge devices, including laptops, smartphones, automotive and robotic computing platforms, and high-performance embedded systems, has exposed fundamental inefficiencies in existing accelerators. While GPUs handle prefill workloads efficiently, the autoregressive decoding phase is dominated by GEMV operations that are inherently memory-bound, resulting in poor utilization and prohibitive energy costs at the edge. In this work, we present EdgeCIM, a hardware-software co-design framework for accelerating autoregressive decoding of SLMs on edge devices in latency-critical applications. At its core is a CIM macro, implemented in 65 nm, coupled with a tile-based mapping strategy that balances pipeline stages, maximizing parallelism while alleviating DRAM bandwidth bottlenecks. Our simulator enables design space exploration of SLMs up to 4B parameters, identifying hardware configurations that optimize latency and energy according to a configurable objective function. On LLaMA3.2 3B (INT4), EdgeCIM achieves 1.76x and 3.21x higher throughput than NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin and Orin Nano Super operating in MAXN mode, respectively, and 7.07x higher throughput than Qualcomm's Snapdragon-X Elite. Extensive benchmarks on TinyLLaMA 1.1B, LLaMA3.2 (1B, 3B), Phi 3.5 mini 3.8B, Qwen2.5 (0.5B, 1.5B, 3B), SmolLM2 1.7B, SmolLM3 3B, and Qwen3 (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) reveal that our accelerator, at INT4 precision, achieves on average 336.4 tokens/s and 173 tokens/J. These results establish EdgeCIM as a compelling solution towards real-time, energy-efficient, latency-critical SLM inference at the edge.

Programming Languages 6
☆ Preserving Separation Logic Specifications through Compilation
Carmine Abate, Kleio Liotati, František Farka, Sebastian Ertel
Program specifications in separation logic describe ownership of resources and serve as logical interfaces for local reasoning. Compiler correctness, expressed as semantics preservation, does not by itself supply such an interface for compiled code, because it does not explicitly relate source and target resources and assertions. We investigate WP-preserving compilation: when a compiler translates a source program, the weakest preconditions that the program satisfies in a separation logic carry over to the compiled program. Our proof technique consists of a resource-aware simulation over interaction trees: it relates the resources required by corresponding source and target events, and tracks ownership through target operations, such as register accesses, that have no source counterpart. We apply the approach to a compiler from a structured imperative language to an assembly-like language, first for closed programs and then extended with modules and function calls. In the extended setting, each module can be verified in the source logic and compiled separately; its specifications carry over to the compiled module and are preserved when compiled modules are linked. All results are mechanized in Rocq.

☆ Abstract Lipschitz Continuity
Marco Campion, Isabella Mastroeni, Michele Pasqua, Caterina Urban
We introduce Abstract Lipschitz Continuity (ALC), an extensional property of functions, in particular of program semantics, requiring that bounded differences between properties of two inputs yield proportionally bounded differences between properties of the corresponding outputs. ALC explicitly discerns between two complementary forms of approximation: property-based (or qualitative) approximations, which retain a property of the data and are formalized by upper closure operators as in abstract interpretation, and quantitative approximations, which measure the closeness of data and are formalized by pre-metrics, a weakening of metrics that covers the distances arising in program analysis. Keeping the two forms of approximation explicit has two main benefits. First, it allows us to precisely relate ALC to established extensional properties: ALC is a sufficient condition for partial completeness in abstract interpretation, for partial abstract non-interference in language-based security and, with a null Lipschitz constant, for abstract robustness in machine learning. Second, it supports reasoning about ALC of program semantics by induction on the syntax of programs: we present a sound deductive system, parametric in the property-based approximations and in the pre-metric of interest, that reduces a proof of ALC for a program to proofs for its basic commands and to some explicit side conditions, which isolate what makes ALC compositional across sequential composition, non-deterministic choice and iteration.
☆ Polymorphic First-Class Environments
Jinhao Tan, Jam Kabeer Ali Khan, Bruno C. d. S. Oliveira
First-class environments expose runtime environments as ordinary values and support a substitution-free, environment-based operational semantics. They also support applications such as first-class modules, dynamic composition of environment fragments, and sandboxing via local environments. Existing environment-based calculi, however, are mostly studied in a simply typed setting and stop short of System F and polymorphism. We present $F_E$, a polymorphic calculus with first-class environments that conservatively extends System F with a small-step environment semantics. $F_E$ eliminates all forms of substitution in System F. Term and type abstractions evaluate to closures, boxes run code under local environments, and box types expose the local typing assumptions of both. Manifest types, together with a two-sided type equivalence, play a dual role: they model type-level instantiation, and enable transparent type components in environments and modules. We prove type soundness, normalization, decidability, and standard properties of type equivalence, and conservativity over System F. The metatheory of $F_E$ is based on instantiation lemmas rather than substitution lemmas. This foundation supports polymorphic first-class modules with dynamic composition and sandboxing. We study $F_E$ in two forms. Our main presentation is an explicit, type-passing calculus that follows conventional System F. We also develop an implicitly-typed variant aligned with the semantics of polymorphic functional languages. All metatheory is mechanized in the Rocq theorem prover.
☆ Proof Repair and (Co)algebra Equivalences
E. G Plante, Eric Paul, Cosmo Viola, G. A. Kavvos, Talia Ringer
Maintaining formal proofs in response to breaking changes in their dependencies is a major challenge of proof engineering. Proof repair is a kind of proof automation that lessens the burden of this challenge by automatically adapting proofs in response to those changes. While proof repair is well understood through an algorithmic lens, it has, until now, been poorly understood through a theoretical lens. We address this gap by giving the first foundational treatment of proof repair, focusing in particular on type-theoretic proof repair in response to changes in datatypes. Our key insight is that the core component of a proof repair algorithm---a ``configuration''---is exactly an algebra isomorphism. We formalize and prove this insight, answering open questions from prior work. We then take this one step further and show that, by taking the dual of this representation, we obtain proof repair over coinductive types. All of our results are formalized in Agda.

☆ ProCerDCL: Query-Efficient Black-Box Probabilistic Verification of Deep Learning Compiler Optimization
Zexin Li, Weibo Zhang, Linyi Li, Arvind Easwaran, Simin Chen
We present \tool, a query-efficient framework for black-box probabilistic verification. Given a fixed, sampleable input distribution and a binary property oracle, \tool assesses the property's violation probability $p$ against a threshold $θ$. Under independent sampling and stateless oracle behavior, acceptance asserts $p\leθ$, rejection asserts $p>θ+η$ for a user-specified separation margin, and an inconclusive verdict indicates insufficient evidence for either conclusion. The probability of each type of erroneous verdict is bounded by its specified error budget. To support this three-valued contract efficiently, \tool uses a single sequential test, eliminating the interval family required by the baseline. The test accounts for repeated sampling checks through either corrected exact binomial confidence intervals or an anytime-valid confidence sequence. We apply \tool to probabilistic local model semantic consistency, where the property oracle detects decision-level inconsistencies between reference and compiled executions under a specified perturbation distribution. Our evaluation covers 24 tasks across TorchInductor and TensorRT and examines sampling efficiency, conclusiveness, and diagnostic behavior. For sampling efficiency, we use paired one-model clean-label radius searches: the baseline requires 3.6-4.2 times as many unique samples under matched numerical parameters while providing a weaker decision contract. For model semantic consistency, checks at $θ=10^{-3}$ yield 817 acceptances, 19 inconclusive verdicts, and 4 rejections across 840 input--backend instances from 21 tasks. Case studies of two documented compiler faults further demonstrate what these local verdicts can diagnose.

Data Structures and Algorithms 27
☆ Approximate Counting for Mixed $p$-Spin Glasses in Weak External Fields via Cluster Expansion
Qiang Wu
We give algorithms for approximating the partition function $Z_N$ of the Sherrington--Kirkpatrick (SK) model and finite mixed Ising $p$-spin models with $p\ge 3$. The algorithms apply at a fixed margin below the second-moment threshold $β_{\mathrm f}$ with homogeneous weak external fields $|h|\leqρ_0N^{-1/4}$, for fixed $ρ_0>0$. For any accuracy $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ and failure parameter $δ\in(0,1)$, they approximate $\log Z_N$ to additive error $\varepsilon$. The number of exact operations is polynomial jointly in $N$, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, and $δ^{-1}$. The disorder variables have i.i.d symmetric distribution with unit variance. The failure probability is at most $δ$ plus an explicit disorder tail bound. This tail bound tends to zero under a finite fourth moment condition. Our approach builds on the cluster expansions of Aizenman--Lebowitz--Ruelle~\cite{ALR87} and Dey--Wu~\cites{DW23,DW26}. For higher-order mixtures, we retain hypergraphs using few vertices and evaluate their total weight deterministically through local spin averages and inclusion--exclusion. For SK, we impose cutoffs on the number of vertices of degree at least three and on the total number of edges. We estimate the retained sum using randomized color coding~\cite{AYZ95}. A lower tail control based on reverse hypercontractivity~\cite{MOS13} converts the mean-square approximation of the normalized partition function into logarithmic accuracy.

☆ Online Interval Selection with Binary Predictions: Optimal Consistency and a Continuous Robustness Tradeoff
Zhong Han
We study online interval selection with binary predictions of membership in a final optimal solution. Requests arrive in arbitrary order; accepted requests may be revoked, but rejected or revoked requests cannot be recovered. With rewards proportional to interval length, the best consistency under finite strict robustness is $d=(3+\sqrt5)/2$ when both guarantees hold almost surely on each fixed input, including for randomized algorithms. A continuous lower bound forces consistency strictly above $d$ at every robustness budget below $4\varphi+3\approx9.472$, whereas our deterministic algorithm attains $d$ with exact robustness $\varphi^5\approx11.090$, where $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$. Each held interval inherits a ledger recording positive prediction mass lost through irrevocable decisions. A tunable replacement cost gives a continuous family with exact consistency throughout and exact robustness on two parameter intervals, including $(C,R)=(25/9,10)$ and $(4,7)$. Randomization permits expected consistency approaching one with finite expected robustness, whereas almost-sure robustness forces expected consistency strictly above two at every finite budget.

☆ A Linear Kernel for Planar Semitotal Domination
Paloma T. de Lima, Lukas Retschmeier
A set of vertices $S$ in a graph $G$ is a semitotal dominating set if $S$ is a dominating set and every vertex in $S$ has a witness, that is, another vertex in $S$ within distance at most two. The Semitotal Domination problem asks whether $G$ admits such a set of size at most $k$. We study this problem parameterized by the solution size. We provide a linear kernel of size $358k$ on planar graphs, a result that adds to several existing linear kernels for other domination variants on planar graphs. Additionally, we show Semitotal Domination is $W[2]$-hard on split graphs, on bipartite graphs and on $t$-claw-free graphs, it is $W[1]$-hard on circle graphs.
☆ Tight Bounds for Randomized Generalized $k$-Server on Uniform Metrics
Junho Hwang
In the generalized $k$-server problem, each of $k$ servers moves in its own metric space, and a request $(r_1,\dots,r_k)$ is served once some server $i$ stands at the point $r_i$ of its space. We determine the randomized competitive ratio against an oblivious adversary when every space is a uniform metric. With unit distances the ratio is $Θ(k\log k)$: we give a $(k+1)\ln(2k)$-competitive algorithm and show that for large $k$ every algorithm has ratio at least $\frac{1}{20}k\ln k$, improving the bounds $O(k^2\log k)$ and $Ω(k)$ of Bienkowski, Jeż, and Schmidt. When server $i$ pays $w_i$ per move, the ratio is $\exp(Θ(k))$: we give a $20^k$-competitive algorithm, which also applies to weighted $k$-server on uniform metrics. This improves the $\exp(O(k^2))$ bound of Bijoy, Mondal, and Chiplunkar and matches the $Ω(2^k)$ lower bound of Ayyadevara and Chiplunkar up to the base of the exponent. Both algorithms follow normalized weights exactly, through couplings whose expected movement is controlled by the decrease of the logarithm of the total weight. The lower bound hides a random permutation among the solutions of random permutation constraints, which split into small, well-separated components, and then forbids one position to one server at a time.

☆ Polynomial-Time Solvability of the Mixed Evacuation Problem
Naoyuki Kamiyama
The mixed evacuation problem is a variant of the dynamic transshipment problem where there exist two modes of transportation and we allocate supplies to each mode of transportation in such a way that we can send all the supplies from sources to sinks within a given time limit. It is known that this problem can be solved in polynomial time when the number of sources and sinks is small. In this paper, we prove that the mixed evacuation problem can be solved in polynomial time with the ellipsoid method.
☆ Linear-Time FPT Rectilinear Planarity Testing of Series-Parallel Graphs
Giacomo Ortali
A planar graph is rectilinear planar if it admits a planar orthogonal drawing without bends. For series-parallel graphs in the variable embedding setting the best known testing algorithm takes $O(n^2)$ time. We show that rectilinear planarity of a series-parallel graph with maximum degree four can be tested in $2^{O(k)}\cdot n$ time, where $k$ is the number of degree-4 vertices; the planar embedding is not fixed. In particular, the running time is subquadratic when $k=o(\log n)$. Each node of the SPQ$^*$-tree stores the set of its admissible spiralities; we show that this set is a union of $2^{O(k)}$ arithmetic progressions of step two. ☆ Differentially Private Preconditioned Power Iteration Method for Principal Component Analysis that Adapts to the Coherence
Duy V. Le, Ta Duy Nguyen, Alina Ene, Huy L. Nguyen
In Principal Component Analysis (PCA), we are given as input a matrix $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ and an integer $k\leq d$, and the goal is to find the top $k$ right-singular vectors of $A$. We consider the setting where each row of $A$ is the private data of an individual, and we design an $(ε,δ)$-differentially private algorithm whose utility guarantee improves with the coherence of the input matrix. Prior works were either restricted to the case $k=1$ or ensured significantly weaker privacy protections. ☆ Uniformity Testing for the Monomer-Dimer Model
Joy Qiping Yang, Yuichi Yoshida
We study uniformity testing for the monomer-dimer model on a known graph $G=(V,E)$. Given samples from an unknown distribution $D_w(M)\propto \prod_{e\in M} w_e$ over matchings $M$ of $G$, we ask whether $D_w$ is the uniform distribution over all matchings of $G$ or is $\varepsilon$-far from it in total variation distance. Our main result is that, for every maximum-degree bound $Δ$, uniformity can be tested with $\widetilde O_Δ(\sqrt m/\varepsilon^2)$ samples, where $m=|E|$. We complement this with an information-theoretic lower bound for general graphs: for every pair of parameters $n\le m\le n^2$, there are graphs with $Θ(n)$ vertices and $Θ(m)$ edges on which every tester requires $Ω(\sqrt m/\varepsilon^2)$ samples. Thus, for bounded-degree graph families with $m=Θ(n)$, testing uniformity over the space of matchings has $\widetilde Θ(\sqrt n/\varepsilon^2)$ sample complexity, in contrast with the $Θ(n/\varepsilon^2)$ samples needed to estimate $D_w$ in total variation distance.

☆ An Unconditional Space-Approximation Lower Bound for Semi-Streaming Matching
Yang P. Liu, Kheeran Naidu, Hoai-An Nguyen, Janani Sundaresan
We prove the first unconditional lower bound tradeoff between the number of passes and the approximation factor of maximum matchings in the semi-streaming model. Specifically, we show that any algorithm that outputs an $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation to maximum matching requires $Ω(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ passes for constant $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$. Prior to our result, such tradeoffs were proven only conditioned on a certain combinatorial assumption about the density of Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs. The state-of-the-art unconditional lower bound rules out any approximation factor better than $(8/9)$ in two passes but leaves open the possibility of a $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation for any $\varepsilon > 0$ even in three passes. We provide two different proofs of our result: the first proof uses the recent hierarchical-embedding framework of Assadi, Konrad, Naidu, and Sundaresan (STOC 2024) along with combinatorial constructions using Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs. The second extends the two-level Ruzsa-Szemerédi hierarchy of Konrad and Naidu (SODA 2024) beyond two levels and proves the corresponding communication lower bound directly by induction, rather than through a reduction to Index.
☆ A Simple Optimistic B-Skiplist Algorithm
Jianting Guo, Yicong Luo, Helen Xu
We present the first optimistic concurrent B-skiplist, built on optimistic lock coupling (OLC). OLC replaces the multi-cache-line RW lock with a compact, single-cache-line lock: readers validate a version number without writing to shared memory, avoiding cache invalidations and retrying only on a detected conflict. Although OLC was thought poorly suited to skiplists, where a single per-element lock may span structurally unrelated levels, the B-skiplist's blocked layout places each level (and associated lock) in its own node. We exploit the B-skiplist's upfront knowledge of promotion height with a hybrid top-down scheme that traverses upper levels optimistically and locks only from the promotion level down, so most inserts never dirty a cache line. On 128 threads, the proposed optimistic B-skiplist achieves $1.2-10\times$ higher throughput over state-of-the-art concurrent skiplist implementations. Furthermore, the optimistic B-skiplist achieves $0.8-4.3\times$ the throughput compared to high-performance optimistic B+-tree implementations based on RW locks and OLC. Finally, the optimistic B-skiplist achieves up to $13\times$ lower latency than concurrent skiplists and tree-based indices, and is within $1.6\times$ of the latency of the OLC-based B+-tree.
☆ Generating pivot Gray codes for spanning arborescences of complete digraphs and split digraphs in constant amortized time
Bowie Liu, Dennis Wong
We present the first known efficient algorithm to generate pivot Gray codes for spanning arborescences of complete digraphs, listing all spanning arborescences such that consecutive arborescences differ by reassigning the outgoing edge of exactly one vertex. Our recursive algorithm generates each spanning arborescence in constant amortized time using $O(n^2)$ space. We then extend our algorithm to generate the first known pivot Gray codes for spanning arborescences of split digraphs, achieving constant amortized time per arborescence using $ O(n^2) $ space. Finally, we further generalize our approach to proper interval digraphs. When the arborescences are rooted at the leftmost interval, our algorithm generates the pivot Gray code in constant amortized time per arborescence using $ {O}(n^2) $ space.
☆ Local-to-Global Theorems Based on Cones with Applications to Graph Coloring
Anqi Li, Shayan Oveis Gharan
Local-to-Global theorems are a cornerstone of many of recent developments in theoretical computer science from coding theory and PCPs all the way to analyzing mixing time of Markov chains. Building on trickledown theorems on simplicial complexes, several techniques have been established to obtain tighter local-to-global theorems to analyze the spectral gap of the Glauber dynamics, namely matrix trickledown, ${\mathcal C}$-Lorentzian polynomials and more recently Bochner type inequalities. ♻ ☆ Approximate Envy-Freeness and Pareto Optimality Beyond Additivity
Harish Chandramouleeswaran, Prajakta Nimbhorkar
We study the compatibility of envy-freeness up to one good (EF1) and Pareto optimality (PO) beyond additive valuations. For two agents with monotone submodular valuations, we prove that a $2/3$-EF1+PO allocation always exists, while for every $α>3/4$, an $α$-EF1+PO allocation may fail to exist even for unweighted coverage valuations. A six-good EF1+PO counterexample resolves the longstanding exact existence question negatively. Exact EF1+PO does exist when one agent has a monotone submodular valuation and the remaining agents have identical additive valuations. For two agents, we also establish tight EF$k$+PO item thresholds of $4k+1$, $2k+1$, and $3k+1$ for two submodular agents, two subadditive agents, and one subadditive and one additive agent, respectively. Finally, deciding whether an EF$k$+PO allocation exists is NP-hard for every fixed integer $k\ge1$, even for three agents with unweighted coverage valuations, one of whom has an additive valuation.

♻ ☆ A Tight Space Lower Bound for Pivot-Based Distance Oracles
Avi Kadria, Liam Roditty
An approximate distance oracle (ADO) is a compact data structure that answers distance queries within a multiplicative \emph{stretch} $k$. The classical oracle of Thorup and Zwick~[STOC'01, JACM'05] has stretch $k=2\ell-1$ and uses $O(\ell n^{1+1/\ell})$ space, for every integer $\ell\ge1$, and this trade-off is optimal for dense graphs under the Erdős girth conjecture. However, for sparse graphs ($m=O(n)$), their lower bound yields nothing beyond $Ω(m)$ space. A pivot-based $1$-hop ADO gives every vertex $\tilde{O}(1)$ pivots and returns as an estimate the length of a walk of the form $u\to p_u \to p_v \to v$, where $p_u$ is a pivot of $u$, $p_v$ is a pivot of $v$, and $p_u\to p_v$ is a stored distance. We observe that all the state-of-the-art ADOs with $\tilde{O}(1)$ query time are in fact pivot-based $1$-hop ADOs. Our main result is that, under the Erdős girth conjecture for graphs with constant degree, there are sparse graphs on which every \emph{pivot-based} $1$-hop ADO with stretch $k$ requires $\widetildeΩ(m^{1+2/(k+1)})$ space, for every \emph{real} $k\ge1$. This matches the best known upper bounds with $\tilde{O}(1)$ query time, up to polylogarithmic factors, at every stretch where they are known. We also extend our lower bound to pivot-based ADOs with any constant number $β$ of hops, showing that they require $\widetildeΩ(m^{1+2/(β(k+1))})$ space. In addition, we obtain a tight lower bound for two-hop hopsets. Under the Erdős girth conjecture, for every integer $\ell\ge3$, $(2\ell-3,2)$-hopsets on sparse graphs require size $Ω(m^{1+1/\ell})$, which matches a construction based on the oracles of [Kadria and Roditty, ISAAC'25]. Using the recent explicit high-girth graphs of Double Agent~[doubleAI'26], we also obtain unconditional versions of our ADO lower bounds, with the exponent above $1$ multiplied by $4/5$.
♻ ☆ An Unconditional Lower Bound for the Active-Set Method in Almost-Linear Programming
Eleon Bach, Yann Disser, Sophie Huiberts, Nils Mosis
We prove that, for every fixed $ε\in(0,1]$, the active-set method requires exponentially many iterations in the worst case to maximize a convex objective of the form $(x_1)^{1+ε}+\vec c^{\top}\vec x$ subject to linear constraints, regardless of the pivot rule used. Our lower bound therefore holds for objectives arbitrarily close to the linear case $ε=0$, where the active-set method coincides with the simplex method and a lower bound for all pivot rules would constitute a major breakthrough. The special case $ε=1$ yields a lower bound in convex quadratic maximization. This improves on the best previously known lower bound [IPCO 2025] requiring polynomial objectives of degree $ω(\log d)$ in dimension $d$ and firmly resolves the open question of whether a constant degree suffices. Our result is based on a novel extended formulation, recursively constructed using deformed products. Its key feature is that it projects onto a polygonal approximation of a parabola while preserving all of its exponentially many vertices. We define a convex objective with a single non-linear variable that forces the active-set method to follow the parabolic boundary of this projection, without allowing any shortcuts along chords corresponding to edges of its full-dimensional preimage.
♻ ☆ A Better Analysis For PPSZ For 3-SAT
Tao Jiang, Shaowei Cai
The PPSZ algorithm of Paturi, Pudlák, Saks, and Zane solves 3-SAT by processing the variables in a uniformly random order, setting a variable whenever a bounded number of clauses implies its value, and guessing it otherwise. Its running time is determined by how many variables are forced in this process, and the exact exponent is a central question in the study of exponential-time satisfiability algorithms. Scheder showed that the classical analysis of PPSZ is not tight for 3-SAT. We ask how much further a sharper analysis of the unmodified algorithm can go. Our analysis uses auxiliary distributions on variable orderings that correlate the positions of variables sharing critical clauses. Such correlations make forcing more likely for some variables, can make it less likely for others, and incur an entropy cost. We choose the correlated pairs at random on short components, so that every pair is selected with the same probability, and we express the forcing loss as a sum of weights attached to these pairs in advance. Gains and losses then scale by the same factor and can be compared directly. Combining the resulting estimate with the complementary estimate of Scheder through an explicit linear-programming certificate gives a uniform improvement for every uniquely satisfiable formula. We prove that the original PPSZ algorithm, with a sufficiently large but fixed implication strength, solves Unique-3-SAT in randomized time $O^*(1.306969598^n)$. Through the lifting framework of Scheder and Steinberger, it solves general 3-SAT in randomized time $O^*(1.307031578^n)$. Both bounds improve the guarantees obtained from the analysis of Scheder for the same algorithm. These improvements follow entirely from the analysis, while PPSZ continues to use a uniformly random variable order and unbiased guesses. It shows that the known guarantees for PPSZ can be strengthened by analysis alone.

♻ ☆ Interactive Particle Systems on Hypergraphs, Drift Analysis and the WalkSAT algorithm
Gabriel Istrate, Cosmin Bonchis, Mircea Marin
We analyze the expected running time of WalkSAT, a well-known local search procedure for satisfiability solving, on satisfiable instances of the k-XOR SAT problem. We obtain estimates of this expected running time by reducing the problem to a setting amenable to classical techniques from drift analysis. A crucial ingredient of this reduction is the definition of (new, explosive) hypergraph versions of interacting particle systems, notably of coalescing and annihilating random walks as well as the voter model. The use of these tools allows to show that the expected running time of WalkSAT depends on structural parameter (we call odd Cheeger drift) of the dual of the formula hypergraph.

♻ ☆ Nash Incentive-compatible Online Mechanism Learning via Weakly Differentially Private Online Learning ICML 2024
Joon Suk Huh, Kirthevasan Kandasamy
We study a multi-round mechanism design problem, where we interact with a set of agents over a sequence of rounds. We wish to design an incentive-compatible (IC) online learning scheme to maximize an application-specific objective within a given class of mechanisms, without prior knowledge of the agents' type distributions. Even if each mechanism in this class is IC in a single round, if an algorithm naively chooses from this class on each round, the entire learning process may not be IC against non-myopic buyers who appear over multiple rounds. On each round, our method randomly chooses between the recommendation of a weakly differentially private online learning algorithm (e.g., Hedge), and a commitment mechanism which penalizes non-truthful behavior. Our method is IC and achieves $O(T^{\frac{1+h}{2}})$ regret for the application-specific objective in an adversarial setting, where $h$ quantifies the long-sightedness of the agents. When compared to prior work, our approach is conceptually simpler,it applies to general mechanism design problems (beyond auctions), and its regret scales gracefully with the size of the mechanism class.

♻ ☆ Power of Conditional Sampling on Hypergraphs
Debarshi Chanda, Buddha Dev Das, Arijit Ghosh, Gopinath Mishra
Let $G=(V,E)$ be a $d$-uniform hypergraph with $|V|=n$ and $|E|=m$. Estimating $m$ from uniformly random hyperedge samples requires $Θ(\sqrt{m})$ samples by a birthday-paradox argument. We investigate the power of conditional sampling through a hierarchy of oracles: for an integer $1\le s\le d$, given pairwise disjoint subsets $X_1,\ldots,X_s\subseteq V$, the $s$-colorful oracle returns a hyperedge chosen uniformly at random from all hyperedges contained in $X_1\cup\cdots\cup X_s$ that intersect every $X_i$, provided one exists. For $s=1$, this samples a uniformly random hyperedge induced by $X_1$. For every $1\le s\le d$, we give a randomized algorithm that outputs $\widehat{m}\in[(1-\varepsilon)m,(1+\varepsilon)m]$ with probability at least $2/3$ using $O_{s,d}(\operatorname{poly}(1/\varepsilon,\log n))$ conditional samples, where the exponent of $\log n$ is an absolute constant independent of both $s$ and $d$. Conditional sampling thus yields an exponential separation over uniform hyperedge sampling. We also study the decision version, in which the oracle only reports whether a qualifying hyperedge exists. ♻ ☆ Amplified Pruning and Path Selectors for Deterministic Radio Gossiping
Qin Xin
We study deterministic gossiping in unknown strongly connected directed radio networks with $n$ vertices, $m$ arcs, and distinct identifiers in $[N]$, where $N=n^{\mathcal O(1)}$. Messages are unrestricted and collision detection is unavailable. Our neighborhood-clearing procedure reuses an elected receiver to secure its entire closed in-neighborhood in $\mathcal O(n\log^2n\log\log n)$ rounds. Charging each distinct closed neighborhood once yields $\mathcal O(m\log^2n\log\log n)$ rounds under an explicit degree-concentration condition, for every polynomial identifier range. A complementary adaptive-pruning procedure combines quadratic contraction with completion sensitive to the number of unsecured sources. If $a\ge1$ sources remain at completion entry, permutation selectors reduce the balanced completion budget by a factor of $\log^{2/3}(a+1)$ relative to interleaved path selectors. A geometric execution attains $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(k^2+nτ+\min\{u^2,(nu)^{2/3}\})$ for a path cover of scale $k$, weight $τ$, and $u$ exceptions. For directed graphs with common in- and out-degree $Δ$, the portfolio achieves $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\min\{n\sqrtΔ,n^2/Δ\})$. Every graph also admits $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\min\{n^{4/3},N+(mn)^{3/5}\})$ rounds. Only $n,N$ are inputs; degrees, covers, and structural constants are used solely in the analysis. The unrestricted polynomial-identifier density extension remains open.

♻ ☆ Exact Algorithms and Complexity of the Smallest Grammar Problem
Xiangle Chen
The Smallest Grammar Problem (SGP) asks for a smallest straight-line grammar generating a given string, with size measured by the total right-hand-side length. For an input of length $n\ge2$ over at most $σ$ symbols, where $2\leσ\le n$, let $R=\log_σn$. We give a deterministic exact algorithm running in $n^{O(1)}2^{O(n\log\log n/(R\sqrt{\log n}))}$ time and polynomial space, with constants independent of $σ$. This gives time $2^{O(n\log\log n/(\log n)^{3/2})}$ for fixed alphabets and $2^{O(n\log\log n/\sqrt{\log n})}$ for arbitrary alphabets, up to polynomial factors. The algorithm combines pairwise disjoint representative occurrences of dictionary strings, a sparse interval certificate, and the Boolean tensor contraction theorem of OpenAI. It explicitly enforces dictionary membership, rule boundaries, and the grammar-size budget. Independently of that contraction theorem, disjoint representatives yield exact algorithms in $O^*(1.872258^n)$ time and $2^{O((n/R)\log(R+1))}$ time using $O(n)$ words of space. Anchor-based grammar projections establish binary NP-completeness and reductions with tunable alphabet size. We state their consequences as unconditional quantitative transfers from SGP algorithms to sparse SAT algorithms, and quantify the additional loss from sparsifying general formulas.

♻ ☆ A Search-Based Algorithm for Multistage Robust Discrete Linear Optimization and its Implementation
Michael Hartisch, Ulf Lorenz
Robust optimization models that allow decisions to adapt to newly revealed information remain computationally challenging and are often limited to two decision stages or are addressed heuristically or via approximation schemes. This challenge is further amplified when uncertainty sets depend on earlier decisions. Conventional approaches to such problems often require problem-specific reformulations and tailored algorithms, highlighting the need for an exact model-and-run approach. We address this gap by presenting an algorithmic framework for solving multistage robust discrete linear optimization problems. This exact approach adapts and integrates ideas from game-tree search, quantified conflict-driven clause learning, and integer linear programming to explore the minimax tree and is implemented in the open-source solver Yasol. The solver supports discrete decision variables across multiple stages, discrete uncertainty sets with polyhedral or decision-dependent structure, and mixed-integer recourse in the final stage. It can be used as a standalone executable or through a C++ library interface, allowing it to serve as a reference solver for exact methods in multistage robust discrete linear optimization. In an extensive computational study, we demonstrate the capabilities of our solver. The results show competitive performance against a reference approach from the literature for a protection-interdiction problem, as well as strong performance on bilevel benchmark instances. For a multistage cutting-stock problem, we demonstrate the substantial loss in solution quality that can arise from neglecting the multistage structure and show that our solver significantly outperforms decision-rule approximations in terms of computational efficiency while certifying the true multistage optimum.
♻ ☆ Report the tolerance, not the count: exact phylogenetic distance deconvolution with incomplete references
Haotian Tang
Phylogenetic distance deconvolution represents mixture-to-reference distances by weighted sources placed on a reference tree. We study the minimum number of strictly interior sources that explains a distance profile, and what incomplete references cost. On a positive-rational reduced tree we give an exact algorithm with 2^O(K) poly(B) bit complexity for a free positive mean pendant and any nonempty set of observed leaves; the single-exponential dependence on K is tight under ETH, already with every leaf observed. With arbitrary missing references and a prescribed pendant the problem is W[1]-hard in the source budget K, even on positive binary trees supplied with a strict explanation using at most K+1 sources. We then use the algorithm as an instrument. Removing references can only lower the minimum source count, never raise it, at every error tolerance. Which references are missing then matters far more than how many. Under nearest-to-source deletion a median of two references can be erased before the generating support stops being the unique smallest explanation, and that budget stays nearly flat as the panel grows (Spearman 0.10 over 20 to 361 references, against 0.82 for random loss). Discarding 7,931 of 10,575 references from a real 16S panel at random costs 1.8% of the tolerance at which one source suffices. Discarding comparably many as whole clades costs 90%. That tolerance needs neither ground truth nor an error model, which makes the model measurable on real data. The generating configuration's worst-leaf residual exceeds the one-source optimum in all 296 real ANI cases, and in 248 after refitting the common pendant. The phenomenon is not unobservable in practice. A point estimate of the count alone does not quantify it. Methods for this problem should report the tolerance at which an explanation of a given size exists.
