♻ ☆ Polynomial and Hyperplane Avoidance over the Integers with Applications to Integer-Point Counting
Grigorii Dakhno, Dmitry Gribanov, Nikita Kasianov, Anastasiia Kats, Andrey Kupavskii, Nikita Kuz'min, Stanislav Moiseev
We study the \emph{Polynomial Avoidance Problem}: given a finite collection $F \subseteq Z[x_1,\dots,x_n]$ of nonzero polynomials, find a vector $x \in Z^n$ of minimum $\ell_p$-norm such that $f(x)\neq0$ for every $f \in F$. Each polynomial is given sparsely as a list of its nonzero terms. The affine special case is the \emph{Hyperplane Avoidance Problem}. This special case arises in Barvinok-type algorithms for counting integer points in polyhedra, when certain rational generating functions must be evaluated at a removable singularity. Our main results are as follows: 1) Let $D:=\sum_{f\in F} \text{deg} f$. Every instance admits a feasible vector $x$ satisfying \[ \|x\|_1 \leq \min\left\{D,\frac{D+n}{2}\right\}. \] This bound is tight. Such a vector can be found by a deterministic algorithm whose bit complexity is polynomial in $D$ and the sparse encoding length of the input. Our proof uses a new algorithmic variant of the \emph{Combinatorial Nullstellensatz}; 2) In the affine case, with $m$ input polynomials, such a vector can be constructed by a fast deterministic polynomial-time algorithm using $O(nm)$ arithmetic operations. For the homogeneous case, Barvinok's original approach provides only the weaker guarantee $\|x\|_1 = O((nm)^{n-1})$; 3) The problem is NP-hard for every $p\in R_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}$, even for homogeneous linear forms; 4) As an application, we improve the complexity bound for counting integer points in general rational polytopes. We also improve the bound for polyhedra of bounded codimension; this includes the counting version of the \emph{Unbounded Subset-Sum} problem as a special case.

♻ ☆ Online Service with Per-Batch Maximum Delays
Tianhang Lu, Runtian Ren, Shengcai Liu, Ke Tang
We study online service with per-batch maximum delays on a finite metric space. Requests arrive over time, and a server serves each batch by following a walk visiting its requests' locations. Movement is instantaneous. The objective is to minimize the total distance traveled by the server plus the sum, over all batches, of the maximum waiting time in each batch. We give deterministic polynomial-time algorithms with competitive ratios $10$ on finite lines, $12$ on weighted trees, and $20$ on arbitrary finite metrics. Unlike additive delay, maximum delay allows younger requests to join a batch without increasing its delay charge. Our algorithms coordinate service using a common waiting clock and a threshold determined by the metric. The analysis uses certificates for groups of requests to compare this policy with any offline schedule, even one that serves different batches and follows different walks. This comparison shows that coordinating service in this way loses only a constant factor. The bounds hold whether the server must serve requests it visits or may leave them unserved; the two rules also have equal offline optimal costs. Offline optimization is polynomial on lines and explicitly represented weighted trees, and NP hard on general metrics. When arrivals are announced but locations are hidden until visited, we give an $84$-competitive algorithm on a known finite line under either rule.
♻ ☆ A Faster Auction Algorithm for Weighted Matroid Intersection
Tatsuya Terao
We consider the weighted matroid intersection problem in the independence-oracle model. A sequence of works by Huang--Kakimura--Kamiyama~[SODA'16 \& Math. Program'19], Chekuri--Quanrud~[SODA'16], Quanrud~[ICALP'24], and Dudeja--Grilnberger~[IPCO'26] has developed efficient $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation algorithms for this problem. We present a simple deterministic auction algorithm that, given two matroids on a common ground set of size $n$, computes a $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximate maximum-weight common independent set using $O(n \varepsilon^{-2} \log^2(n))$ independence-oracle queries. This is the first deterministic $(1-\varepsilon)$-approximation algorithm for the weighted matroid intersection problem whose query complexity is nearly linear in $n$ and polynomial in $1/\varepsilon$. Our algorithm builds on the auction algorithm for unweighted matroid intersection by Huang--Kobayashi~['26].

♻ ☆ A Competitive Algorithm for Agnostic Active Learning
Eric Price, Yihan Zhou
For some hypothesis classes and input distributions, active agnostic learning needs exponentially fewer samples than passive learning; for other classes and distributions, it offers little to no improvement. The most popular algorithms for agnostic active learning express their performance in terms of a parameter called the disagreement coefficient, but it is known that these algorithms are inefficient on some inputs. We take a different approach to agnostic active learning, getting an algorithm that is competitive with the optimal algorithm for any binary hypothesis class $H$ and distribution $D_X$ over $X$. In particular, if any algorithm can use $m^*$ queries to get $O(η)$ error, then our algorithm uses $O(m^* \log |H|)$ queries to get $O(η)$ error. Our algorithm lies in the vein of the splitting-based approach of Dasgupta [2004], which gets a similar result for the realizable ($η= 0$) setting. We also show that it is NP-hard to do better than our algorithm's $O(\log |H|)$ overhead in general.

☆ Honeycomb-Surfels: Compact Hexagonal Primitives for Efficient Scene Rendering and Reconstruction
Prakhar Kaushik, Soumava Paul, Alan Yuille
Novel view synthesis and 3D reconstruction have advanced rapidly with radiance fields and splatting, yet their memory and computational demands limit their use in resource-constrained settings. We introduce Hexels: novel planar hexagonal surface elements with a first-order spatial color field for efficient 3D scene and 2D image representation. Hexels exploit locally planar regions with smoothly varying appearance using fewer, larger primitives. Their hexagonal support provides an area- and boundary-efficient domain, while the local color field captures appearance variation within each primitive. Across 34 scenes, Hexels improve PSNR over memory-matched 2DGS by 4.53-7.38 dB across datasets while requiring lower training VRAM. We further ablate Hexels by showing that per primitive, Hexels outperform 2DGS by upto 4.74 dB while using lower training VRAM and having 2.31 times faster FPS. Even in unconstrained settings, Hexels offers complementary quality-memory trade-offs. For efficient 3D surface reconstruction, Hexels reduce error by 28.9% compared with memory-matched 2DGS across 15 DTU scans. On 6 Tanks and Temples surface scenes, Hexels achieves the highest mean F1 among compared methods (34.38%, versus 31.72% for 2DGS). We further also show that Hexels, as a general primitive, improves 2D image representation over Image-GS. Hexels push the Pareto frontier on the quality-efficiency trade-off of surface-aligned primitives.

☆ CTBakeGS: training-free baking of photorealistic CT volume renderings into 3D Gaussian splats
Haruki Fukuda, Constantin Lux, Wolf Schweitzer, Tadashi Hosoya, Lars Ebert
Photorealistic volume rendering of computed tomography (CT) may convey anatomy intuitively, but it needs the CT data, dedicated software and a powerful graphics processor. Three-dimensional Gaussian splatting (3DGS) displays photorealistic scenes in real time on less powerful devices, but existing medical approaches train each scene for minutes to hours. We aimed to convert a CT volume rendering directly into a 3DGS model without training. Splats were placed on an adaptive octree of millimetre-sized cells. Their colour and opacity were computed on the graphics processor with the renderer's lighting, cell opacity was converted analytically into splat opacity, and faint splats were retained stochastically above the 1/255 opacity floor of standard 3DGS rasterisers. Six public CT scans (1.5-mm voxels) were converted with three transfer functions and compared with converged references by peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), structural similarity and a learned perceptual metric. Two-million-splat models reached a PSNR of 32.6 +/- 0.9 dB for bone, 30.1 +/- 1.0 dB for contrast-enhanced vessels and 32.3 +/- 1.5 dB for skin and bone. A conversion pass (usually two or three per model) took 4-30 s on a current laptop and 9-60 s on a desktop with a 4-GB graphics card. On the laptop, the models rendered at 68 frames per second at 800 x 800 pixels. Training-free conversion produced real-time 3DGS models of photorealistic CT renderings in about a minute or less per conversion pass on ordinary computers, with fidelity that we consider sufficient for interactive viewing.

☆ DomeGS: Perceptually Consistent Background Completion for Unbounded 3D Gaussian Splatting in Virtual Reality
Anastasiya Pechko, Agata Szymańska, Piotr Borycki, Joanna Szelewska, Michał Mnich, Sławomir Tadeja, Przemysław Spurek
The growing need for high-quality 3D content remains among the most prevalent challenges in virtual reality (VR). Recently, novel view synthesis techniques have enabled rapid content creation, with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) emerging as one of the most promising methods for generating high-fidelity 3D scenes. However, using 3DGS to reconstruct unbounded outdoor scenes can leave horizons incomplete due to missing data or a lack of distant geometric constraints. Such incompleteness can break immersion, compromising VR's core characteristic. A typical workaround in traditional VR systems is a textured cube (skybox) or sphere (skydome) surrounding the environment to create the illusion of a distant background. However, these approaches struggle to blend seamlessly with the reconstructed 3DGS geometry. To address these issues, we introduce the DomeGS framework, which incorporates the skydome concept not as a flat background shell, but as a part of the reconstructed Gaussian Splatting 3D scene. By integrating the horizon and sky into the 3DGS representation, our method provides a unified, scene-consistent environment bridging foregrounds and backgrounds for greater realism. Experimental evaluation with N=36 users demonstrates that DomeGS significantly increases spatial presence (p=0.042) and realism (p=0.042) compared to the skybox baseline.

☆ GraftRay: Physically Based Mesh Insertion into Gaussian Splatting Scenes via Path Tracing
Jinxin Ai, Matthias Niessner
Inserting virtual objects into scenes reconstructed via 2D or 3D Gaussian Splatting (2DGS/3DGS) is a key capability for content creation, AR, and synthetic data generation. However, realistic insertion requires the object to be seamlessly lit by the scene and, conversely, to cast physically plausible shadows onto it. Existing approaches either harmonize appearance in image space, sacrificing multi-view consistency, or approximate scene illumination with an environment map at the insertion point, which cannot represent near-field area lights such as windows and lamps that dominate indoor lighting. To address this, we present GraftRay, a unified method for physically consistent insertion of mesh objects into indoor scenes represented by either 2D or 3D Gaussians. We recover scene materials and surface normals with diffusion-based priors, and explicitly model spatially-extended emitters, solving their radiance in closed form. A hybrid renderer then jointly path-traces the scene Gaussians and inserted meshes, producing view-consistent shading, interreflections, and soft shadows. On FIPT, GraftRay sets a new state of the art, outperforming recent baselines such as ComGS and EAG-PT in rendering fidelity and shadow accuracy, while maintaining highly competitive rendering efficiency.

☆ Total Deformation-based MPM for Perfectly Elastic Solid Simulation with Strong Deformations
Timna Böttcher, Rick Ridder, Jan Bender
We present a new MPM-based solver for perfectly elastic solids, which can recover from strong deformations and naturally allows contact handling. Typical MPM solvers suffer from numerical fracture, where objects break apart even when no plasticity is included in the elasticity model. Previous approaches for perfectly elastic solids solve this by computing forces on a fixed grid in the reference configuration. However, this makes them incompatible with standard collision handling methods. We instead propose a method for deformable solid simulation without numerical fracture, that still computes elastic forces on the current grid. Our method can easily include grid-based collision handling, as commonly used in MPM frameworks. Additionally, we compute the deformation directly from the particle positions and a fixed reference grid, without any updates based on velocity gradients. This facilitates the inclusion of particle-level collision handling and zero-energy mode suppression, which counteracts artifacts from non-linear deformations. As a result, we can efficiently simulate complex scenarios of elastic objects undergoing large deformations, including contact.
☆ From Walks to Skeletons: A Reusable Transport Architecture for Interactive Monte Carlo PDE Solvers
Yunfeng Liao, Yucheng Wu, Xiucheng Li
Walk-on-Spheres (WoS) enables mesh-free solutions of elliptic PDEs, but conventional methods repeatedly discard sampled trajectories, limiting their efficiency in interactive applications. We introduce reusable transport skeletons, a persistent representation that organizes stochastic walks into a shared computational graph. Our framework enables efficient reuse across spatial queries and boundary conditions, while supporting progressive refinement, structure-preserving compression, and local adaptation to dynamic geometry. It further enables interactive editing of transport between selected regions. Experiments demonstrate substantial computational savings while preserving solution accuracy. By transforming transient stochastic trajectories into reusable transport structures, our method provides a flexible foundation for interactive Monte Carlo PDE solving.

☆ LingGS: Decoupling Feed-Forward Geometry Prediction from Camera Tracking for High-Fidelity Gaussian SLAM
Da Li, Yihan Zang, Ivan Viola, Zeyu Wang, Dominik Engel
Achieving high-quality reconstruction and responsive camera tracking remains challenging for monocular RGB Gaussian splatting SLAM under a limited optimization budget. We present an online system that addresses this challenge by combining learned geometric priors with explicit geometric camera tracking, which can substantially outperform learned camera prediction in terms of tracking accuracy. A frozen streaming 3D foundation model serves as a virtual RGB-D sensor, providing depth estimation from monocular video. An explicit geometric solver then estimates camera motion through feature reprojection, and a streaming Gaussian mapper integrates the resulting observations into a dense scene representation. We further show that geometric tracking improves camera pose accuracy across representative streaming geometry backbones, demonstrating the broader applicability of our approach. Extensive experiments on indoor and outdoor datasets show that our method significantly improves reconstruction quality over existing approaches, achieving about 7 FPS on a single RTX 4090 and delivering 2-3 times the throughput of the evaluated prior SLAM systems.

☆ Frequency-Adaptive Gaussian Grids for Efficient Image Representation
Anatolii Evdokimov, Stavros Diolatzis, Bingxuan Li, Akshay Jindal, Patsorn Sangkloy, Qi Sun
Neural representation has emerged as a powerful tool to mitigate the demand for increasingly higher-resolution images. However, a fundamental trade-off remains between structured (e.g., feature grids, which offer spatial consistency) and unstructured (e.g., 2D Gaussians, which adaptively capture local variances) representations. This choice significantly affects how well an algorithm represents an image. Here, we present Gaussian-Grid, a hybrid neural representation that adaptively integrates structured and unstructured primitives. By dynamically partitioning computation between 2D Gaussian distributions and their attached multi-resolution feature grids based on the local frequency content, Gaussian-Grid automatically optimizes its architecture to match the underlying complexity of the target signal. We evaluate our approach using high-resolution natural images and demonstrate its applications as GPU-native dataset storage, showing $1.24$-$2.14\times$ decoding speed improvement and PCIe data traffic reduction of $23\times$-$176\times$ over JPEG + ZIP data archiving. The results show that Gaussian-Grid maintains small memory footprints while achieving high reconstruction fidelity across a variety of datasets.
☆ MesoCraft: a web-based tool for interactive procedural modeling of mesoscale biological structures
Donggang Jia, Alexandre Kouyoumdjian, Ondrej Strnad, Uros Smajdek, Ciril Bohak, Dilara Bayraktar, Alejandra Esparza, Peter Mindek, Tobias Klein, Stefan T. Arold, Ivan Viola

☆ GOFish: Full Field-of-View Gaussian Splatting and Mesh Extraction
Allen Tu, Meher Gitika Karumuri, Shifan Zhu, Hailing Wang, Yen-Liang Lin, David Held, Matthias Zwicker, Tom Goldstein
Robots, autonomous vehicles, and consumer 360° cameras use fisheye lenses for wide fields of view (FoVs). Together, the Gaussian scene and mesh form a LiDAR-free, simulation-ready digital twin that renders in real time, ray traces the full FoV, and provides collision geometry.

☆ Callosal: Rethinking the Kernel Page Cache for Disaggregated Storage and Memory
James McMahon, Amit Samanta, Seung-seob Lee, Ryan Stutsman, Anurag Khandelwal
Classic distributed filesystems like NFS centralize filesystem operations on a server and use custom, filesystem-specific approaches for managing cache consistency across client nodes. This limits scalability, and it leads to weak consistency, ruling out applications that concurrently access and mutate shared files. Our key insight is that these legacy designs are now too storage-centric; with today's large memories, OS page caches do most of the "heavy lifting" in accessing data, not the filesystem and block storage layers. In response to this, we present Callosal, a page cache for a cluster of machines that is centered on shared disaggregated memory. Rather than serializing operations through a filesystem server and using ad hoc caching like NFS, Callosal is built around the existing Linux page cache paths and is accelerated by RDMA and programmable switches. Callosal delivers the strong consistency that programs expect with local filesystems (like in ext4 and tmpfs) while avoiding the additional network access delays and bottlenecks that host-based solutions incur. We compare Callosal against NFS, CephFS, and local filesystems; often Callosal can provide caching performance similar to local filesystems while also scaling across nodes. For mixed read/write workloads, on average Callosal has 4.6x higher throughput than NFS when contention is not extreme, and it offers strong consistency guarantees like CephFS, while outperforming it. For a file-backed key-value store with 1% writes and uniform access, Callosal scales 1.7x across 4 nodes, while CephFS collapses across nodes.
☆ safeL4: Rethinking Safe-Language-Based Single Address Space Microkernels ASPLOS'27
Zheyuan Xu, Xiaobei Zhang, Zihan Li, Zekai Nie, Guangtao Zhu, Zhuang Lu, Qianying Zhang, Shijun Zhao, Haibo Chen, Rui Hou
Microkernels provide good features such as high security, safety, and flexibility, but their reliance on hardware-based isolation leads to heavy performance overhead, limiting their widespread adoption. Although constructing single-address-space microkernels based on safe language-based isolation is a promising approach to addressing the performance issue, this approach still faces challenges in maintaining the principle of minimality and enforcing capability-based, fine-grained access control over system resources. We propose safeL4, an approach to constructing microkernels based on safe languages. It guarantees minimality through low-level abstractions for unsafe operations, and implements a capability system based on the safe language's object capabilities to achieve fine-grained access control over all operations on system resources. We implement a prototype system in Rust, and our evaluation results show that the primitives provided by safeL4 are, on average, 1.9$\times$ faster than those of seL4.

♻ ☆ No Provenance Left Behind: Revisiting Reference Monitor Guarantees with Learned Kernel Scheduling EuroSys '27
Jinsong Mao, Benjamin E. Ujcich, Shiqing Ma
Provenance plays a critical role in maintaining traceability of a system's actions for root cause analysis of security threats and impacts. Provenance collection is often incorporated into the reference monitor of systems to ensure that an audit trail exists of all events, that events are completely captured, and that logging of such events cannot be bypassed. However, recent research has questioned whether existing state-of-the-art provenance collection systems fail to ensure the security guarantees of a true reference monitor due to the 'super producer threat' in which provenance generation can overload a system to force the system to drop security-relevant events and allow an attacker to hide their actions. One approach towards solving this threat is to enforce resource isolation, but that does not fully solve the problems resulting from hardware dependencies and performance limitations. In this paper, we show how an operating system's kernel scheduler can mitigate this threat, and we introduce Aegis, a learned scheduler for Linux specifically designed for provenance. Unlike conventional schedulers that ignore provenance completeness requirements, Aegis leverages reinforcement learning to learn provenance task behavior and to dynamically optimize resource allocation. We evaluate Aegis's efficacy and show that Aegis significantly improves both the completeness and efficiency of provenance collection systems compared to traditional scheduling, while maintaining reasonable overheads and even improving overall runtime in certain cases compared to the default Linux scheduler.

☆ Whole-Route Planning of a Memory Bus Between Two BGAs: An Exact Model of Crossings and Layer Changes, Negotiated to DRC-Clean Copper
Andrew Haas
Routing the bus between a processor and its DRAM remains largely manual in printed circuit board (PCB) design, and published algorithms optimize proxies such as crossing or layer counts. We present a router that plans every lane of a two-layer BGA-to-BGA bus before any copper is laid, and carries the plan to design-rule-clean copper. Its core is an exact constraint-programming model that chooses, for all lanes at once, where each necessary crossing occurs, in what order -- by a rule over every triple of lanes -- and where each lane changes layer, with via room; for the ends it is given, it proves its plan optimal within the model. The model reads naturally as physics: worldlines, frustrated Ising spins and contact forces. A linear program places the lanes in millimeters; the constraints it must violate return to the model as cuts, and audit findings as negotiated congestion prices, until the plan passes an audit against the production router's rules or lays clean. The escapes are chosen by an estimate of the same objective, and differential pairs carry their polarity, with an automatic via crossover where the ends disagree. On an Allwinner H3 and a Zynq-7020, each to a DDR3 device, the router completes every rung of two ladders, of up to 51 nets on the H3 (its whole bus) and 43 on the Zynq, connected and DRC-clean, never with more vias than the human designer (80 against 88 and 62 against 100 at the top rungs) and always with less copper, where the toolkit's production router leaves nets open on four of the ten rungs and Freerouting completes none. On the Zynq board itself it completes 45 of the bus's nets with 70 vias to the designer's 105. On generated buses of three, four and six routing layers every run completes, and every one with a known optimum attains it.

☆ ADACT: PDE-Constrained Thermal Hardware/Software Co-Design Adjoint-Driven Optimal Control via Finite Difference Discretization
Don Li
By virtue of the sheer compute demands of large-scale artificial intelligence (AI) and high-performance computing (HPC), modern multi-core microprocessors suffer from severe power density limits and thermal throttling, entailing an urgent need for principled methods for predictive, physics-informed dynamical thermal management (DTM). Here, we introduce a hardware/software co-design physics-informed PDE-constrained DTM throttling optimal control algorithm, ADACT (Adjoint-Driven Adaptive Co-Design Thermal Control), that minimizes thermal risk and cooling energy overhead while maximizing core execution throughput. The underlying physical temperature field is governed by a two-dimensional nonhomogeneous parabolic reaction-diffusion equation with spatially localized, time-varying power source terms and variable convective cooling subject to nonhomogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions. We employ a discretization of the continuous state PDE via a Crank-Nicolson finite difference scheme with a 5-point discrete Laplacian operator on a uniform spatial mesh, which preserves unconditional numerical stability and $O(Δx^2 + Δy^2 + Δt^2)$ second-order temporal and spatial accuracy. To efficiently compute exact gradients, we construct and implement a continuous backward-in-time adjoint state method with L-BFGS as our optimization algorithm of choice. The accuracy and second-order convergence rates of both forward state and backward adjoint solvers are quantitatively verified via the Method of Manufactured Solutions (MMS), and gradient accuracy is validated using directional derivative Taylor tests. Numerical experiments demonstrate that ADACT dynamically throttles software power workloads and modulates hardware cooling efforts to strictly uphold physical thermal thresholds while maintaining operational throughput.
☆ Realizing Low-Precision Storage and Sparse Execution in a Folded FPGA Speech Enhancer ISCA
Clément Laroche
Hardware-aware training often optimizes FPGA resources, latency, or bit operations, but folding changes what compression saves: shared arithmetic decouples precision, weight storage, and scheduled work. We derive training objectives from the measured costs of a folded streaming speech-enhancement accelerator. Mixed-precision weights reduce on-chip storage; structured channel sparsity removes entire operations from the engine schedule. We deploy a bit-exact implementation on the Kria KV260. The best sparse model eliminates 47.3% of convolution MACs, cutting frame time by 45% (1.86 to 1.03 ms) and dynamic energy by 60% (83 to 33 $μ$J/frame) against a storage-priced model measured in the same session, with higher speech quality. Relative to the most efficient dense build measured separately, frame time falls by 42% and energy by 48%. Reducing weight width from 8 to 6 bits lowers dynamic power by 27% but changes frame time by less than 5%. Plain channel group-lasso removes more work than cycle-cost-weighted lasso. Thus, folded-accelerator training should price storage and removable work separately, rather than rely on a single bit-operation metric.

☆ When and Why to Use TMA: A Workload-Grounded Characterization of Tensor Memory Acceleration on NVIDIA Hopper GPUs SC
Christin David Bose, Cesar Avalos, Junrui Pan, Timothy Rogers
The Tensor Memory Accelerator (TMA), introduced on NVIDIA Hopper, is used pervasively in production GPU kernels yet its usage is conspicuously selective: expert kernel developers in CUTLASS, FlashAttention-3, and FlashInfer apply TMA to some tensor operands within a kernel but fall back to cp.async, direct global loads, or distributed shared memory (DSM) for others. We address this gap with a workload-grounded characterization study on H100. We further compare TMA multicast against DSM as competing cluster-broadcast primitives, and isolate the FlashAttention-3 paged-key-value (KV) TMA-vs-cp.async dispatch on H100 across page size, datatype, model shape, and execution phase. Our results yield concrete, predictive guidance on when TMA pays off and when multicast helps or hurts on Hopper, and we extend the analysis to server Blackwell (SM100) via a CUTLASS GEMM study on B200 that shows the qualitative dispatch rules carry across generations while absolute crossovers shift with tensor-core and bandwidth scaling.

☆ PaFeDSE: Pareto-Feedback-Guided Microarchitecture Design Space Exploration for Efficient Pareto Search SP
Joonyong Park, Hyojeong Lee, Sejeong Yang, Joo-Young Kim
Efficient microarchitecture design space exploration (DSE) is critical for optimizing performance, power, and area trade-offs in modern out-of-order processors. While machine-learning-based DSE significantly reduces the cost of exhaustively evaluating large design spaces, existing methods still waste expensive simulations on Pareto-miss candidates that do not improve the Pareto frontier and repeatedly process redundant candidates during iterative exploration. In this paper, we propose PaFeDSE, a Pareto-feedback-guided microarchitecture DSE framework for efficient Pareto search. PaFeDSE leverages Pareto-hit feedback to prioritize candidates likely to improve the frontier and Pareto-miss feedback to suppress exploration of unpromising local regions, while filtering redundant candidates that remain low priority across iterations. ☆ SPARTA: A Sparse Ternary LLM Accelerator with Adaptive Attention and Unified Datapath for Long-Context Edge Inference
Yanzhen Zhou, Zhengyu Han, Qunyou Liu, Zheyu Yan, Weihong Xu, Cheng Zhuo
LLMs on edge devices are constrained by limited memory and bandwidth. Existing accelerators also use separate datapaths for ternary linear layers and attention, incurring hardware and execution overhead. We present SPARTA, a sparse ternary LLM accelerator for efficient long-context edge inference. SPARTA integrates: (1) a phase-aware sparse dataflow exploiting heterogeneous sparsity across compute-bound prefill and memory-bound decode; (2) a unified compute fabric mapping ternary SpMM/SpMV and bit-serial attention onto shared processing elements to exploit zero-weight and zero-bit sparsity; and (3) an adaptive two-stage attention engine combining page-level Top-k selection to reduce K-cache accesses with token-level Top-p pruning to reduce V-cache traffic. SPARTA reduces KV traffic by 4.1-4.8x over full attention and 1.3-1.5x over a state-of-the-art sparse-attention scheme, with task-dependent quality loss. Compared with NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin, SPARTA achieves 3.9x higher generation throughput and 8.3x higher end-to-end energy efficiency on average; versus AMD Ryzen 7 9700X, the gains are 5.0x and 81.7x. ☆ MCF: A Memory- and Communication-Aware Co-Designed Framework for Long-Context LLM Inference
Huaizhi Zhang, Jiacheng Zhu, Xuqi Zhu, Junkyu Lee, Klaus McDonald-Maier, Xiaojun Zhai
This paper presents Memory- and Communication-Aware Framework for Long-Context LLM Inference (MCF) that optimizes long-context inference through communication-aware and stage-adaptive KV-cache layout transitions. At the core of MCF is {Memory-Aware Replication and Scheduling} (MARS), a dynamic orchestration mechanism for chunked prefill. MARS exploits early-stage memory headroom by selectively replicating highly reused KV blocks, improving locality and hiding KV propagation latency behind attention computation. As prefill progresses and memory pressure increases, MARS progressively drains redundant replicas and converges the KV cache into a sharded decode-ready layout, avoiding expensive layout conversion at the prefill--decode boundary. For decoding, MCF adopts a query-centric execution model in which KV shards remain stationary while lightweight query tensors are routed across workers. This changes communication from KV-cache-dependent transfer to query-dependent transfer, reducing communication growth with context length. MCF further integrates a hardware-software co-designed execution layer based on an FPGA compute-NIC datapath, enabling metadata-driven KV routing and collective operations directly in the network path. Experiments show that MCF improves TTFT, throughput, and scalability over state-of-the-art distributed inference systems, with larger benefits as context length and cluster size increase.
☆ zkDuel: Characterizing SumCheck Prover Kernels across GPU Programming Models SP'26
Samarth Narang, Alhad Daftardar, Brandon Reagen
SumCheck is a central prover primitive in zero-knowledge proofs, exposing table-parallel work that maps naturally to GPUs but becomes increasingly arithmetic-intensive as finite fields widen. We study how this shift affects the cost of programming SumCheck in Triton rather than CUDA. Our benchmark, zkDuel, provides matched implementations of 25 zkPHIRE-derived constraints alongside diagnostic polynomials, covering 32-, 64-, 128-, and 256-bit prime fields, with independent CPU-reference validation and cross-backend checksum gating. Of 800 configurations attempted on an NVIDIA H100, 758 complete with matching checksums. CUDA's median advantage over Triton falls from 3.5x at 32 bits to 1.4x at 256 bits, and Triton is faster on 19 large-table configurations; the same convergence appears on an A100, where the gap falls from 4.4x to 1.6x. Profiling shows that the small-field gap largely reflects host launch overhead and redundant modular additions in Triton's original block reduction, whose relative costs diminish as multi-limb Montgomery work grows. Replacing that reduction with tl.reduce brings Triton's median kernel time within 8% of CUDA on both GPUs, while specialized point interpolation improves 256-bit evaluation kernels by 1.55-1.75x. At cryptographic widths, these results show that a Python-embedded DSL can approach CUDA performance, although the largest fully unrolled constraints still expose compiler-scalability limits.

☆ Search-Guided Reasoning Trace Curation for Verilog Generation
Matthew DeLorenzo, Kevin Tieu, Prithwish Jana, Piyush Jha, Dileep Kalathil, Vijay Ganesh, Jeyavijayan Rajendran
Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have improved Verilog generation. However, most training pipelines distill natural-language reasoning traces whose intermediate design decisions are weakly structured and are primarily selected according to the correctness of the final generated RTL. We present MCTS-SFT, a verifier-guided framework for curating reasoning traces for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of Verilog generation models. MCTS-SFT formulates trace construction as search over intermediate reasoning states and uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to propagate functional feedback from complete Verilog rollouts to earlier design decisions. Within this framework, we introduce Plan-MCTS, which searches over compact hardware-design plans that explicitly represent interfaces, timing, state, behavior, and update rules. We further study plan-preserving supervision, in which the searched structured state is retained as part of the downstream SFT target. Our experiments find that Plan-MCTS achieves the highest functional trace yield, with 84.2% correct traces compared with 81.9% for one-shot structured planning and 82.0% for unguided tree exploration. For identical Plan-MCTS trajectories and Verilog outputs, retaining the explicit searched plan during SFT raises pass@10 from 52.56% to 57.7%, demonstrating that the representation of intermediate design decisions materially affects downstream learning. In a separate 6,000-trace study, MCTS-curated supervision improves pass@10 over full-response chain-of-thought distillation by up to 3.7 points while reducing downstream generation length by approximately 50% on VerilogEval-v2. These results demonstrate that structured hardware planning, verifier-guided search, and explicit intermediate-state supervision provide complementary mechanisms for constructing more effective and token-efficient training data for Verilog generation.
♻ ☆ Toward Open ISA Supercomputing: A Comparative Study of RISC-V, Arm, and x86 Architectures on HPC Workloads
Emanuele Venieri, Giacomo Madella, Gabriele Ceccolini, Simone Manoni, Federico Ficarelli, Filippo Barbari, Jenny Lynn Almerol, Elisabetta Boella, Nitin Shukla, Simone Bnà, Luca Benini, Andrea bartolini

♻ ☆ FADiff: Fusion-Aware Differentiable Optimization for DNN Scheduling on Tensor Accelerators SP
Shuao Jia, Zichao Ling, Chen Bai, Bei Yu, Kang Zhao, Jianwang Zhai
Efficiently deploying Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) - particularly Large Language Models (LLMs) - on tensor accelerators is essential for achieving high throughput and energy efficiency at the edge. ♻ ☆ Vision Transformers on the Edge: A Comprehensive Survey of Model Compression and Acceleration Strategies

☆ Connecting Quantum Proof Systems and Hoare Logic
Liyi Li, Zoukarneini Difaizi Tagba, Alex Potanin
Verifying quantum programs today requires specialized proof systems built on interactive theorem provers such as Rocq, Lean, Isabelle, or Why3, and even modest quantum algorithms cost substantial human effort to verify. Because quantum programs cannot be effectively checked by runtime testing, formal verification is the only viable path to correctness, making the cost of these bespoke systems the chief barrier to scalable quantum software assurance. We show that verifying a quantum program should be no harder than verifying a classical array program, a paradigm commonly used in parallel programming: the Qafny quantum proof system translates into Hoare logic with array theory completely (every Qafny proof becomes a valid Hoare triple) and soundly (a valid translated triple certifies only true quantum specifications), and we mechanize the completeness direction in Rocq. The embedding determinizes quantum proof obligations and exposes the full machinery of classical automated verifiers (e.g. Dafny and SMT-based separation logic) to quantum code, removing the need for dedicated quantum proof system infrastructure. In particular, the embedding supplies the correctness argument that the existing compilation of Qafny into Dafny lacks.
☆ Executable Archaeology of Programs That Model Programming: Reanimating the Heuristic Compiler (1961) and KBEmacs (1984)
Jeff Shrager, Kent M. Pitman, Mia Golec
Early AI programs were often also theories of how people think, and programs that wrote programs were, in part, theories of how people program. Here we report on the reanimation of two such programs that offer two seemingly different theories of programming and were developed for quite different purposes. The first, Herbert Simon's Heuristic Compiler (1961), was an experimental model of programming as problem-solving, applying the means-ends analysis methods of the General Problem Solver to investigate the nature of the programming task. The second, KBEmacs (1984), was a working prototype of an intelligent programming assistant developed by MIT's Programmer's Apprentice project, built on the view that expert programmers recognize and combine familiar patterns, or "cliches." It let a programmer build up programs by combining cliches through a stylized English command language. We approach both through the methodology of Executable Archaeology, the study of historical computational systems by running their original code, a process we call "reanimation." Our reanimation of the Heuristic Compiler reproduces the compiled code appearing in Simon's 1963 paper, and our reanimation of KBEmacs reproduces the SQRT sequence of its December 1984 self-test. Examined side by side, the two theories turn out to have much in common: the Heuristic Compiler also works by adapting existing routines, and Simon later argued that search alone requires a large body of programming knowledge. To highlight the reflexivity of this project, we deliberately used a modern AI-assisted programming tool, Anthropic's Claude Code (Opus 5.5), to carry out much of the detailed reanimation work, building on a significant base of existing software. The explicit, executable theories of programming that both systems embodied are largely absent from today's far more capable AI coding assistants.
☆ BUSTA: Teaching Small Language Models to Reason About Binary Execution
Ritika Poddar, Aidan Fennelly, Deirdre Murphy, Eric Wolos, Corre L. Steele, Michael Doyle
Large Language Models (LLM) are increasingly applied to Reverse Engineering (RE) workflows. RE, however, differs from common LLM coding tasks as it requires models to reason about low-level program behavior. Furthermore, the deployment of tooling in resource-constrained sandboxed RE environments limits the ability to use frontier-scale solutions. We examine whether a targeted LLM training approach can transfer distinct RE skills to open models via inverse basic-block execution: given an x86-64 basic block and a desired output register state, the model predicts the input register values that produce it. Fine-tuning \texttt{Qwen3-1.7B} and \texttt{Nemotron-3-Nano} with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) raises knowable-register prediction accuracy from 73.2\% to 85.3\% and from 63.3\% to 95.2\%, respectively. We release both fine-tuned models and the training dataset.
☆ AGDecode: Semantic Attribute Grammar Derivatives for Safety-Constrained Decoding of Large Language Models
Rajan Maghera, Asher Tempest Arya, Aren A. Babikian, Marsha Chechik, Maryam Mehri Dehnavi
Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for code generation, motivating techniques that guarantee the structural and semantic correctness of generated programs. Constrained decoding enforces correctness during generation by restricting token choices to those that admit at least one valid completion. While existing approaches can enforce syntactic constraints and selected semantic properties such as type safety, they either target specific properties or rely on operational pruning mechanisms that are difficult to generalize to data-dependent, inductive program properties. We present AGDecode, a programmable framework for semantic constrained decoding based on least-fixed-point semantics. AGDecode extends attribute grammars to partially constructed syntax trees, and reduces semantic constrained decoding to a realizability problem over such trees. The framework compiles semantic specifications into systems of relational constraints that are discharged by external verification oracles, such as SMT and CHC solvers, cleanly separating language semantics from constraint-solving machinery. We prove that our approach is sound and complete relative to an ideal verification oracle. In practice, solvers inevitably encounter uncertain queries; conservatively, our system can reject tokens for soundness or speculatively continue to generate until later queries resolve the entire generation. We mechanize the core metatheory in Rocq and evaluate the framework on semantic-constrained generation of C programs, enforcing path-sensitive safety properties including null-pointer safety, division-by-zero freedom, and initialized-variable usage. Our results demonstrate that solver-backed semantic constrained decoding can enforce rich semantic guarantees while incurring modest computational overhead at regular program boundaries.
comment: 28 pages, 8 figures, 1 table. Rocq mechanization available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21287418
☆ safeL4: Rethinking Safe-Language-Based Single Address Space Microkernels ASPLOS'27
Zheyuan Xu, Xiaobei Zhang, Zihan Li, Zekai Nie, Guangtao Zhu, Zhuang Lu, Qianying Zhang, Shijun Zhao, Haibo Chen, Rui Hou
Microkernels provide good features such as high security, safety, and flexibility, but their reliance on hardware-based isolation leads to heavy performance overhead, limiting their widespread adoption. Although constructing single-address-space microkernels based on safe language-based isolation is a promising approach to addressing the performance issue, this approach still faces challenges in maintaining the principle of minimality and enforcing capability-based, fine-grained access control over system resources. We propose safeL4, an approach to constructing microkernels based on safe languages. It guarantees minimality through low-level abstractions for unsafe operations, and implements a capability system based on the safe language's object capabilities to achieve fine-grained access control over all operations on system resources. We implement a prototype system in Rust, and our evaluation results show that the primitives provided by safeL4 are, on average, 1.9$\times$ faster than those of seL4.

♻ ☆ Interaction Improvement
Adrienne Lancelot, Giulio Manzonetto, Guy McCusker, Gabriele Vanoni
The relational semantics of linear logic is a powerful framework for defining resource-aware models of the $λ$-calculus. However, its quantitative aspects are not reflected in the preorders and equational theories induced by these models. Indeed, they can be characterized in terms of (in)equalities between Böhm trees up to extensionality, which are qualitative in nature. We employ the recently introduced checkers calculus to define a quantitative contextual preorder on $λ$-terms, and demonstrate that it coincides with the preorder associated to the relational semantics.

♻ ☆ On the Expressive Power of Hook-Indexed State: Comprehending State in the React UI Library (Extended Version)
Toby Ueno, Ezra Cooper, Simon Fowler, Sam Lindley
React is currently the most popular JavaScript library for front-end web development. It endows UI components with state via the useState construct, through a mechanism that we name hook-indexed state. However, React's implementation of hook-indexed state is both fragile and poorly understood. Programmers frequently experience unexpected behavior, and the benefits of programming in this paradigm are wholly unclear. We define $λ^\mathsf{react}_\mathsf{hook}$, a core calculus that captures the essence of hook-indexed state, and give it a type system to statically rule out unexpected behavior. We then translate $λ^\mathsf{react}_\mathsf{hook}$ into a calculus $λ^\mathsf{react}_\mathsf{ref}$ based on ML-style references, and prove that this translation preserves typability and semantics, thus showing that hook-indexed state is expressible in terms of standard references. The translation is local and admits a compositional definition, but involves significant code duplication. As well as illuminating the essence of state in React, our observations can inform the design of future reactive programming systems.
♻ ☆ Ownership Refinement Types for Pointer Arithmetic and Nested Arrays
Yusuke Fujiwara, Yusuke Matsushita, Kohei Suenaga, Atsushi Igarashi
Tanaka et al. proposed a type system for verifying functional correctness properties of programs that use arrays and pointer arithmetic. Their system extends ConSORT--a type system combining fractional ownership and refinement types for imperative program verification--with support for pointer arithmetic. Their idea was to extend fractional ownership so that it can depend on an array index. Their formulation, however, does not handle nested arrays, which are essential for representing practical data structures such as matrices. We extend Tanaka et al.'s type system to support nested arrays by allowing ownership to depend on the indices of enclosing arrays. We formalize the extended calculus in Rocq using a mixed embedding and provide a machine-checked proof of type soundness. We have implemented a verifier based on the proposed type system and demonstrated that it can verify the correctness of programs that manipulate nested arrays, which were beyond the reach of Tanaka et al.

♻ ☆ Certified Panic Mode: Repair-Invariant Error Recovery for Maximal-Munch Lexing
Nicklas Nidhögg
Classical panic-mode recovery skips a failed scan forward by convention, promising progress but no repair-universal boundary guarantee for where it lands. We choose the resume position by theorem, via a committed-prefix lemma over certificates posted for parallel lexing (arXiv:2608.03473, arXiv:2608.09761): a position is a sound recovery point when every prefix repair whose scan commits through the certificate's returned evidence places a token boundary there. This quantifier is strictly stronger than its complete-repair special case, and at a sound recovery point the inclusion is strict precisely when no repair completes while some scan commits through the evidence. The evidence-returning interface pairs each answer with the interval it rests on, so a caller who knows what the scanner cannot check decides whether the boundary transfers to the intended input. The search provably terminates; the guarantee is relative to the active mode's automaton. For an automaton of n states the quantifier is decided exactly from the preserved tail by at most n + 1 representative repairs, and a shortest reaching repair has at most n - 1 symbols and a shortest counterrepair at most n, both tight. Every anchor of an m-byte input is profiled in $O(nm + n|Σ|)$ time. The shipped repair routines are the complete-repair case, which proves their contracts. In a deterministic corruption study of munch's procedure beside skip-one and delimiter recovery, every certified answer whose evidence survives the damage passes its landing check on every move, 40,885 of 40,885, while the 5,595 first answers resting on evidence the damage reached land 1,589 times, reported, not asserted. Of the 16,808 trials labeled beyond repair, 13,522 resume on a suffix that tokenizes whole, where both quantifiers are provably empty, and 3,286 stay undetermined, their damaged inputs not archived.

☆ An $O(n^{0.1537})$-Coloring Algorithm for 3-Colorable Graphs
Kaisei Deguchi, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Hirotaka Yoneda, Masataka Yoneda
We present a polynomial-time randomized algorithm that colors every $n$-vertex $3$-colorable graph with $O(n^{0.1537})$ colors. This improves upon the previous bound of $O(n^{0.1954})$ colors by Bansal, Huang, and Lee (SODA '27). To achieve this result, we introduce a new algorithmic framework for finding a large independent set from a solution to the vector coloring SDP. Combined with the (combinatorial) dense-progress algorithm of Kawarabayashi, Thorup, and Yoneda (STOC '24), this framework yields an $n^{1/6 + o(1)}$-coloring algorithm. Furthermore, we strengthen the SDP-based algorithm specifically for 3-colorable graphs to achieve $O(n^{0.1537})$ colors.
☆ Tiny Suffix Arrays

☆ Query Complexity of Learning Axis-Aligned Partitions
Hadley Black, Arya Mazumdar, Barna Saha, Geelon So
This work establishes the query complexity of learning high-dimensional boxes in the exact query learning setting. Let $X \subset \mathbb{R}^d$ be a set of $n$ points, labeled by a collection of $k$ boxes: the label of each point in $X$ is determined by the unique box containing it. A learner aims to exactly recover the labels for every point in $X$ given access to a label oracle. We consider three increasingly-structured settings. In the most general setting, there are no conditions on the boxes, and they may overlap. We show that CAL (Cohn-Atlas-Ladner 1994), a classical active learning algorithm, is minimax-optimal with query complexity $Θ(kd \log (n/kd))$. When the boxes are disjoint, we examine two regimes. In the large-$k$ regime, CAL remains minimax-optimal. For the constant-$k$ regime, an optimized variant of the Halving algorithm (Hegedüs 1995) can beat CAL by a $\log(d)$ factor. Finally, in the most-structured settings, the boxes correspond to the leaves of a constant-sized decision tree. We prove a tight query complexity of $Θ(d + \log n)$ that is additive in $d$ and $\log n$, generalizing a recent result (Black-Larsen-Mazumdar-Saha-So 2026) that establishes the query complexity of learning decision stumps.
☆ Computation-Friendly Compression of Matrices: Speeding up Linear and Boolean Algebra
Gonzalo Navarro
Large sparse matrices---adjacency matrices of web and social graphs, feature matrices of machine-learning pipelines, the relations of graph databases---are routinely compressed to save space. Classical compressors, however, force the matrix to be decompressed before it can be used, so the space savings often yield a computational overhead. A recent line of work studies \emph{computation-friendly} compression: formats that are not only small, but let us carry out matrix--vector products, and in some cases matrix--matrix products, sums and closures, directly on the compressed representation {\em in time proportional to that compressed size}. This survey organizes that literature around the kind of redundancy each format exploits: spatial clustering of the nonzeros ($k^2$-trees and their variants), similarity between rows (Boldi--Vigna-style referencing and set-difference compression), repeated substrings (grammar compression), and dense bipartite substructures (bicliques). For each family we describe the representation, the algorithms, the theoretical guarantees and the empirical performance. Further, we show that the last three families are instances of a single general scheme, which we call a {\em sparse straight-line factorization} of the matrix, whose forward and backward substitutions evaluate right and left products in time proportional to the size of the representation, and that the product of two such factorizations is again one, which gives matrix--matrix products with a compressed output. An appendix reviews formats that must be decompressed (at least partially) before operating, for completeness. We close with a cross-family comparison and a list of open problems.
☆ Improved Quasi-Polynomial-Time Approximation for Maximum Independent Set
Zhao Song
We give a deterministic quasi-polynomial-time algorithm for the maximum independent set problem. If an $N$-vertex graph contains an independent set of size $2^{-t}N$, where $t$ is an integer with $1\le t\le\sqrt{\log N}/12$, then our algorithm finds an independent set of size $2^t$ in time $N^{O(t)}$. This gives an approximation ratio of $N/2^{Ω(\sqrt{\log N})}$ in time $N^{O(\sqrt{\log N})}$, improving on the approximation ratio $N/2^{Ω((\log N)^{1/3})}$ obtained by Lovett [Lov26]. A limitation of this form of analysis follows from the integrality gaps of Tulsiani [Tul09]. These gaps imply that a universal guarantee of the form $\operatorname{Las}_{R(t)}(G)\ge2^{-t}N\Longrightarrowα(G)\ge2^t$, at $2^{o(\sqrt{\log N\log\log N})}$ levels, cannot hold over a range extending beyond $t=O(\sqrt{\log N\log\log N})$. Thus, the exponent $1/2$ is the limit of this form of analysis, up to a factor of $\sqrt{\log\log N}$.
☆ From Linear to Polylogarithmic: Dynamic Submodular Maximization over Matroids
Kiarash Banihashem, Samira Goudarzi, MohammadTaghi Hajiaghayi, Peyman Jabbarzade, Morteza Monemizadeh
We study the problem of submodular maximization under a matroid constraint. In this problem, there is a monotone submodular function $f: 2^{N} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^+$ and a matroid $M(N, I)$, accessible via value and independence oracles and the goal is to find the independent set $S \in I$ maximizing the objective function, i.e., $f(S) = \max_{I \in I} f(I)$. In the fully dynamic setting, the active ground set $N \subseteq N$ evolves through element insertions and deletions. The goal is to maintain a solution $S \subseteq N$ with $S \in I$ and $f(S) = \max_{I \subseteq N,, I \in I} f(I)$ after each update. Two approximation algorithms have been proposed to solve this problem: Dütting et al. developed a $(4+ε)$-approximation algorithm with an expected amortized update time of $O\!\bigl(\frac{k^2}ε \log k \log n \log^3 \frac{k}ε\bigr)$, and Banihashem et al. achieved a similar approximation with a better update time $O\!\bigl(k \log k \log^3 \frac{k}ε\bigr)$. However, these methods both become inefficient when the rank $k$ of the matroid is large (e.g., when $k = n^{o(1)}$). The central open question in this line of work is whether a fully dynamic algorithm with polylogarithmic update time exists. We resolve this question by presenting the first fully dynamic algorithm for submodular maximization over matroids with polylogarithmic update time. Our algorithm maintains a $(4+ε)$-approximate solution with expected amortized update time $poly(\log n, ε^{-1})$, where $n = |N|$. In particular, the update time is independent of the matroid rank $k$, thereby eliminating the dependence on $k$ and settling the open problem.
☆ Attention under Bounded Key-Query Similarity: Space Complexity and Transformer Anisotropy
Justin Y. Chen, Ying Feng, Piotr Indyk, Michael Kapralov, Ekaterina Kochetkova, Boris Prokhorov
A growing body of work on KV-cache compression aims to reduce the memory requirements of autoregressive generation. These methods compress the stored context while approximately preserving attention computation, enabling generation with reduced memory consumption. Almost tight theoretical results on the worst case space complexity of attention have recently been obtained, but they are achieved by instances where queries can be perfectly aligned with keys. This directly contradicts the observed anisotropy of the key/query geometry in real transformers. In particular, it has been shown empirically that key/query cosine similarities are small even for most correlated pairs, i.e., near-perfect alignment of queries with keys does not happen in practice. In this paper, we study the space complexity of attention approximation under the assumption that key/query cosine similarities belong to a small interval around zero, i.e., a query cannot align too much with any key. We give a nearly space optimal algorithm and a corresponding space lower bound. Surprisingly, the distribution of key/query cosine similarities in our lower bound exhibits the anisotropy property empirically established and studied in the literature. Additionally, we show that the simple uniform sampling algorithm is nearly optimal for a typical range of parameters, supporting its surprising effectiveness on real datasets.
☆ Order-selection Prophet Inequality (Almost) Goes I.I.D
Bo Peng, Zhihao Gavin Tang
We study the order-selection prophet inequality. A gambler knows the distributions of independent nonnegative random values, chooses the order in which to inspect them, and may irrevocably accept at most one value, at the time it is revealed. Since the order is irrelevant when the values are i.i.d., no algorithm can guarantee more than the optimal i.i.d.\ ratio $Γ_\star\approx0.74544$ of the expected maximum. We give an algorithm with competitive ratio $0.74500001$, less than $4.5\times10^{-4}$ below this bound. The algorithm samples independent arrival times and uses item-dependent thresholds. It is designed so that, conditional on its value, every item is selected with probability equal to a fixed fraction of its probability of being the maximum. We show that the constructions of this form that apply one local rule to every item are exactly those given by an allocation kernel, that is, by a coupling of two uniform random variables. Requiring every rarely winning item to follow the optimal i.i.d.\ threshold path singles out a baseline kernel, for which we prove analytically a ratio $Γ_0\approx0.7389$, defined through a one-dimensional control problem. Our final kernel perturbs this baseline, and its guarantee combines analytic estimates with a rigorous computer-assisted certificate.

☆ When is greedy exact for weighted minimum 3-cut?
Armando Rodado Amaris
The greedy algorithm for minimum 3-cut splits a global minimum-weight cut and then the cheaper of its two sides; it is a tight $4/3$-approximation. We show that it is exact, from every global minimum-weight cut, whenever some optimal 3-partition has two parts joined by total weight at least the weighted edge connectivity $λ$. This extends both theorems of Hochbaum and Shmoys from unweighted planar graphs to all weighted graphs, recovers the $4/3$ bound, and generalizes to minimum $k$-cut with a one-paragraph proof. The remaining hard instances have all pair weights below $λ$; for them we prove a merge lemma for Gomory-Hu sides. Finally, we give a 7-vertex graph with a unique Gomory-Hu tree whose unique optimal 3-partition contains no side of that tree, so the first stage of Kapoor's algorithm does not suffice on its own. The main theorem, the 4/3 bound, the merge lemmas and the counterexample are verified in the Lean 4 proof assistant.

☆ Linear Hashing Is Poissonesque
Vinayak M. Kumar
The Poisson approximation theorem states that when $m$ balls are tossed uniformly and randomly into $n$ bins, the distribution of the bin loads can sometimes be approximated by $n$ independent Poisson random variables of mean $m/n$. We show that when the balls are tossed via a random $\mathbb{F}_2$-linear map, the load distribution is still governed by this Poisson distribution, albeit in a (necessarily) weaker manner. This allows us to show that a random linear map behaves like a fully random map in the following ways: 1. $\textbf{Expected loads.}$ We show that the expected value of the $r$th largest bin is governed by the $r$th largest value among $n$ independent Poisson random variables of mean $m/n$. Specializing to the canonical setting of $m=n$ and $r=1$, our bounds show that the expected maximum load when $n$ balls are hashed into $n$ bins by a random $\mathbb{F}_2$-linear map is no more than the corresponding expectation for a fully random map $\textit{plus}$ $\frac{3}2 + o(1)$. Specializing to $m\gg n\log n$, we show that the expected max load is $(1+o(1))m/n$ in this regime. 2. $\textbf{Concentration.}$ When $m\gg n\log (1/ρ)$ balls are tossed into $n$ bins randomly, we expect with $0.99$ probability that $\textit{every}$ density-$ρ$ subset of bins will contain a $(1\pm o(1) )ρ$-fraction of the balls. We show that this remains the case if the $m$ balls are tossed using a random linear map, and our bound matches the $o(1)$-factor produced by the fully random case. This gives a stronger version of the Leftover Hash Lemma for linear maps. An implication of this is that all bins have load $m/n \pm O(\sqrt{(m/n)\log n})$ with probability $0.99$. We obtain these results by showing that the average of a log-potential on the bin loads is upper bounded by the cumulant generating function of a Poisson random variable of mean $m/n$.

☆ The Complexity of Value Optimization in Sporadic Task Systems
Christopher Harbourne, Nathan Fisher, Kecheng Yang, Pontus Ekberg, Sanjoy Baruah, Zhishan Guo
Value-based Task Selection (VTS) asks whether a real-time system can select tasks whose total value reaches a required target while preserving every deadline. We study its threshold decision form for finite collections of independent sporadic tasks scheduled preemptively on one processor. We prove that this problem is $Σ_2^P$-complete for both constrained and arbitrary deadlines, even when all numerical parameters are encoded in unary. The existential choice is the selected task set, while the universal condition requires every processor-demand window to remain feasible. Our hardness reduction already uses constrained-deadline tasks. It represents universal assignments by Chinese Remainder Theorem timestamps and uses polynomial-weight local selector locks to enforce a valid existential assignment. The reduction produces polynomially many tasks, and every generated execution time, deadline, period, task value, and target is polynomially bounded in the source-instance size.

☆ Coordinating Boundaries: Improved Approximation Algorithms for Unweighted Non-Preemptive Throughput Maximization
Weitian Tong, Yao Xu
We study unweighted nonpreemptive throughput maximization: given jobs with processing times, release times, and deadlines, schedule a maximum-cardinality subset on identical machines. For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, we give randomized algorithms with expected approximation ratios $17/13+\varepsilon$ in polynomial time and $21/17+\varepsilon$ in pseudopolynomial time. These improve the respective $4/3+\varepsilon$ and $5/4+\varepsilon$ guarantees of Armbruster, Grandoni, Tinguely, and Wiese [STOC'26]. Both results hold for an arbitrary number of identical machines. Our algorithms build on their configuration-LP and matching framework, which partitions time into blocks. For a job whose window meets multiple blocks, its boundary blocks are the first and last such blocks. Crossing boundary pairs, whose endpoints strictly interleave, permit additional assignments to sampled execution slots, while a noncrossing family can be coordinated using consistent joint distributions on at most three blocks. The exchange analysis accounts for fractional job assignments that must be erased before inserting an additional job. For the pseudopolynomial implementation, we project configurations onto selected job identities and use separate configuration distributions with common placement marginals. This retains efficient pricing while enforcing the correlations needed for coordination.

♻ ☆ Exact algorithms for optimal discretization
László Kozma, Junqi Tan
The optimal discretization problem asks, given two disjoint sets of points $R$ and $B$ in the plane, for a minimal family of horizontal and vertical lines that separate the two sets, so that no cell delimited by the lines contains points from both sets. The problem arises as a pre-processing in supervised machine learning, and has received significant attention in parameterized algorithmics. Answering the question raised by Bonnet, Giannopoulos, and Lampis [IPEC 2017] and Froese [PhD thesis, 2018], it was shown by Kratsch, Masařík, Muzi, Pilipczuk, and Sorge [SODA 2021] that optimal discretization admits a fixed-parameter algorithm with running time $2^{\mathcal{O}(k^2 \log k)} \cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}$, where $k$ is the solution size and $n = |R| + |B|$. In this paper we give an algorithm for optimal discretization that runs in time $\mathcal{O}(1.9602^n)$. We also study the related point separation problem that asks to separate all input points by axis-parallel lines. For this problem we obtain an algorithm with runtime $\mathcal{O}(1.8899^n)$. Our guarantees follow from structural observations about bichromatic and monochromatic point sets. To our knowledge, these are the first improvements over the trivial $2^n$ bound for both problems.

♻ ☆ Online Multi-Level Aggregation with Maximum Delay
Tianhang Lu, Runtian Ren, Shengcai Liu, Ke Tang
We study Multi-Level Aggregation with Maximum Delay (MLA-MD) on finite weighted rooted trees. Each service pays for a rooted subtree and for the maximum waiting time among the requests it clears. For the offline problem, we give an exact polynomial-time algorithm that computes both the optimum and an optimal service schedule. For the online problem, we give a deterministic $2$-competitive algorithm and, against an oblivious adversary, a randomized $e/(e-1)$-competitive algorithm. Both competitive ratios are optimal, even on rooted paths. We also extend the offline result and both online guarantees to service systems with submodular costs.
♻ ☆ Tensor Decomposition for Non-Clifford Gate Minimization
Kirill Khoruzhii, Patrick Gelß, Sebastian Pokutta
Fault-tolerant quantum computation requires substantial resources for non-Clifford gates. Their count also enters exponentially into the runtime of several classical simulation methods. We present algebraic methods for minimizing the non-Clifford CCZ (Toffoli) count in {CNOT,CCZ} phase circuits using tensor decomposition over $\mathbb{F}_2$. On standard cubic benchmarks, these methods match or improve prior automated Toffoli counts, with most instances optimized in under a minute on a single CPU. With additional computation, the decompositions provide effective initializations for existing T-count methods, improving T counts on several circuits. We also present algebraic methods for CNOT-count optimization at fixed Toffoli count. This gives competitive ancilla-free finite-field multiplication circuits and reduces the CNOT count of XOR AES S-box circuits.
♻ ☆ Single-Machine Guarantees on Unrelated Machines and Beyond: Proportional Fairness for Nonclairvoyant Polytope Scheduling
Sven Jäger, Alexander Lindermayr, Bart Zondervan
The Polytope Scheduling Problem (PSP) is a general framework for preemptive scheduling in which feasible instantaneous job allocations form a packing polytope. In the nonclairvoyant online setting, Proportional Fairness (PF), a practical allocation rule rooted in market theory, is the leading general-purpose algorithm. On a single machine, PF coincides with weighted Round-Robin, which is $2$-competitive for weighted completion time with uniform release dates (optimal for nonclairvoyant algorithms) and $(2+\varepsilon)$-speed $O(1/\varepsilon)$-competitive for weighted flow time. Known guarantees beyond a single machine are weaker. This raises a central question: Are these heterogeneous environments intrinsically harder than a single machine under nonclairvoyance? We show that they are not: PF retains both single-machine guarantees on the novel class of support-submodular polytopes, which includes unrelated machines and polymatroids. On unrelated machines, this refutes a belief (JACM'18, SODA'25) that PF is not $O(1)$-speed $O(1)$-competitive for weighted flow time. Our main technical contribution is an equivalent characterization: a polytope is support submodular if and only if, for all job weights, the optimal weighted completion time is concave in the processing requirements. This structural insight underlies both guarantees. For general PSP, the picture changes: we give the first lower bound above $2$, showing that every randomized nonclairvoyant algorithm has a competitive ratio of at least $2+10^{-5}$ for weighted completion time, even with uniform release dates. On the positive side, we substantially improve the best known guarantee for weighted completion time from $27$ (SICOMP'26) to $4$ via a new dual-fitting analysis of PF. For weighted flow time, we show that PF achieves the first $O(1)$-speed $O(1)$-competitive nonclairvoyant guarantee for graph scheduling.

♻ ☆ Amplified Pruning and Path Selectors for Deterministic Radio Gossiping
Qin Xin
We study deterministic gossiping in unknown strongly connected directed radio networks with $n$ vertices, $m$ arcs, and distinct identifiers in $[N]$, where $N=n^{\mathcal O(1)}$. Messages are unrestricted and collision detection is unavailable. Our neighborhood-clearing procedure reuses an elected receiver to secure its entire closed in-neighborhood in $\mathcal O(n\log^2n\log\log n)$ rounds. Charging each distinct closed neighborhood once yields $\mathcal O(m\log^2n\log\log n)$ rounds under an explicit degree-concentration condition, for every polynomial identifier range. A complementary adaptive-pruning procedure combines quadratic contraction with completion sensitive to the number of unsecured sources. If $a\ge1$ sources remain at completion entry, permutation selectors reduce the balanced completion budget by a factor of $\log^{2/3}(a+1)$ relative to interleaved path selectors. A geometric execution attains $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(k^2+nτ+\min\{u^2,(nu)^{2/3}\})$ for a path cover of scale $k$, weight $τ$, and $u$ exceptions. For directed graphs with common in- and out-degree $Δ$, the portfolio achieves $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\min\{n\sqrtΔ,n^2/Δ\})$. Every graph also admits $\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\min\{n^{4/3},N+(mn)^{3/5}\})$ rounds. Only $n,N$ are inputs; degrees, covers, and structural constants are used solely in the analysis. The unrestricted polynomial-identifier density extension remains open.

♻ ☆ A deterministic polynomial-time algorithm for the Bilu--Linial signing problem on bipartite graphs
Zhiqiang Xu
We give a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm for the Bilu--Linial signing problem on bipartite graphs. For every finite simple bipartite graph of maximum degree at most an integer $Δ\ge3$, the algorithm assigns signs to its edges so that the signed adjacency matrix has operator norm strictly less than $2\sqrt{Δ-1}$. Our algorithm builds on the randomized recursive repair framework of Jadbabaie, Saberi, and Sra~\cite{JSS26}, with deterministic rules for sign selection and vertex deletion.

♻ ☆ GPU-Accelerated Search for Fast Matrix Multiplication over $\mathbb{F}_2$
Zeke Medley, Anagha Gokul, Quan Luu, Panagiotis Manolios
We present a GPU-accelerated algorithm for searching for ways to multiply matrices with few scalar multiplications. Our algorithm searches the matrix multiplication flip graph and, on an NVIDIA H200, performs over a billion search steps per second for matrix sizes up to $7\times7$, a roughly $380\times$ improvement over previous GPU-accelerated search on the tensor for $7\times7$ matrix multiplication. We also prove that, over \(\mathbb{F}_2\), the directed matrix multiplication flip graph is strongly connected with only flip and plus edges. This removes the need for computationally expensive reduction edges in the connectivity argument used by previous work. Using this GPU-accelerated search procedure on our simplified flip graph, we find a way to multiply $7\times 7$ matrices over $\mathbb{F}_2$ using 245 multiplications, a three-multiplication improvement over the previous record.
♻ ☆ Single-Range-Cover Search on DAGs: Average-Case Analysis, Efficient Implementations, Privacy Guarantees
Ladan Kian, Dariusz R. Kowalski
Extracting exact and complete information using range queries is not only time-inefficient, on average, but also susceptible to privacy breaches. As a remedy, Demertzis et al.~\cite{demertzis2016practical} (SIGMOD'16) introduced, in the context of searchable symmetric encryption, the principle of covering a query with a single canonical range using a DAG structure (TDAG). It was later formalized by Falzon et al.~\cite{falzon2022range} (VLDB'22) as the single range cover (SRC) search algorithm -- a faster and potentially privacy-preserving search principle. SRC-search can, however, produce false positives. In this work we study how a family of specific Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) could substantially decrease the false-positive ratio, without asymptotic increase of SRC-search time and storage, compared to the classical 1D-Tree. We perform a competitive analysis of a $c$-DAG with respect to the 1D-Tree and derive only an {\bf\em additive constant Returned Level Overhead} while reaching a substantial {\bf\em multiplicative logarithmic improvement in the Accuracy Ratio}, on average. We also provide a generic framework extending our results to non-uniform datasets whose level overhead distribution is close to that of a uniform dataset. We define and analyze several single-path local selection rules that can substantially speed up the original SRC-search algorithm, which was based on exhaustive search, and their privacy implications. Experiments on the Gowalla and NYC Yellow Taxi datasets show that the level overhead stays at or below the proven constant, up to sampling error, and that the level overhead distribution of real data is close to a uniform baseline in most configurations.
♻ ☆ Improved Lower Bounds on Tree Covers
Yu Chen, Shengtang Huang, Zihan Tan, Hangyu Xu
A tree cover of an $n$-point metric space is a collection of $k$ trees such that every pairwise distance is approximately preserved in at least one tree. For fixed $k$, the best known distortion upper bound is $\tilde{O}(n^{1/k})$ and the best known lower bound is $Ω_k(n^{1/2^{k-1}})$. In this paper, we prove a lower bound of $Ω_k(n^{1/(k-1)^2})$ for all $k\ge 3$, significantly narrowing the gap towards the upper bound. Our approach makes use of a novel algebraic topological notion that we call the \emph{agreement index}: the equivariant topological index of a simplicial complex encoding pairwise agreement among tree labels. This notion helps characterize the power and limit of algebraic and topological methods for analyzing tree covers, and we believe it will also find applications to other problems.

☆ CVTSplat: Multi-view Reconstruction Using an Adaptive Centroidal Voronoi Tessellation
Briac Toussaint, Diego Thomas, Jean-Sébastien Franco, Edmond Boyer, Christian Theobalt
Voronoi tessellations have recently emerged as an adaptive, space-partitioning discretization for shape and appearance reconstruction from multi-view images. However, the irregular cells they produce make the repeated reconstruction of the tessellation during optimization costly. We therefore explore adaptive centroidal Voronoi tessellations (CVTs), which extend standard CVTs with non-uniform cell sizes while preserving the regular, well-shaped cells that make them efficient to compute. In CVTSplat, cell size is modulated by the evolving signed distance field, concentrating sites near the zero level set while keeping cells nearly centroidal. This regularity lets us specialize the whole pipeline, with GPU algorithms for nearest-neighbor queries and approximate Voronoi construction, and a rasterizer tailored to small, nearly isotropic cells. Our Voronoi construction is 5 to 10 times faster than general-purpose alternatives in this regime. On DTU and BlendedMVS, CVTSplat reaches geometric accuracy competitive with the best Gaussian-splatting surface methods while training in about 2.5 minutes on a single GPU.

☆ GaussianSpline: Adaptive Bézier Splines for Highly Dynamic 4D Gaussian Splatting
Tom Yachil, Andrei Sharf
We present GaussianSpline, a dynamic 4D Gaussian Splatting method that models per-Gaussian motion using adaptive, non-uniform piecewise cubic Bézier splines. Unlike approaches based on global deformations, fixed-order polynomials, or uniformly parameterized splines, GaussianSpline jointly learns the Bézier control points and the temporal partition of each Gaussian's trajectory. This adaptive representation allocates greater temporal capacity to highly dynamic regions while maintaining compact trajectories for near-static regions, enabling high-fidelity reconstruction of complex and rapidly changing motion. To jointly achieve globally smooth motion and local adaptivity, we enforce $G^1$ continuity between consecutive Bézier segments using a reflection-based constraint on their control points. This produces smooth, fully differentiable trajectories while preserving the flexibility of non-uniform temporal parameterization. Experiments on the Neural 3D Video benchmark, the Google Immersive dataset, as well as on synthetic scenes demonstrate that GaussianSpline substantially improves reconstruction quality in scenes with highly non-uniform and challenging dynamics, while maintaining competitive performance on general dynamic scenes compared with state-of-the-art 4D Gaussian Splatting methods.
