☆ Blendshape Generation for Models with Disconnected Parts: Adding ARKit/MediaPipe Blendshapes to Anime-Style Character Models WACV 2027
Pramook Khungurn
Blendshape generation for 3D models has been extensively researched, but very few works address generating blendshapes for models with multiple disconnected parts. We present a case study of generating fixed facial expression blendshapes for commercial anime-style 3D character models with up to 11 parts per face. To make sure that our system can handle models in a way that is agnostic to topology, we train neural networks to generate deformation fields around the input meshes. We employ at least one network to independently process each part, and the relative positions of their outputs are coordinated by having each network also process the geometry of an anchor part. We use two mechanisms to improve accuracy: having the networks predict whether the output displacement is zero or not, and classifying blendshapes into different size groups and dedicating a network to each group. We also introduce a method to improve blendshape quality by enhancing bilateral symmetry. Our method is simpler than recent neural-network-based works and solves a real problem of the user community of these models.

☆ Inverse origami inspired by insect morphogenesis: geometric design of wrinkle structures for curved surface formation
Kentaro Morikawa, Yuuki Shimizu, Nen Saito, Yasuhiro Inoue
Insect exoskeletons abruptly acquire complex three-dimensional forms during metamorphosis from larva to pupa. This transformation occurs through the unfolding of preformed wrinkle structures inside the larval body under internal pressure. Inspired by this process, we investigate an inverse-origami strategy for fabricating curved surfaces. Whereas conventional origami produces three-dimensional objects by folding a flat sheet, this strategy produces curved surfaces by unfolding preformed wrinkle structures. The central design problem is to derive a wrinkle structure from a prescribed target surface. Our key idea is to regard a wrinkle structure as a unidirectional stretching field, which specifies at each point the stretch produced when the structure unfolds. We first derive such a field from the target surface geometry and then translate it into spatially varying wrinkle directions and spacing, so that the wrinkle structure unfolds into the target. The resulting numerical designs approximate the intrinsic metric of the target surface. We demonstrate the method on multiple target surfaces through numerical construction, mechanical unfolding simulations, and 3D-printed prototypes. This work provides a biologically inspired geometric design principle for encoding prescribed surface shapes in wrinkle structures, as well as a framework for designing and fabricating deployable curved surfaces.

☆ VISTA: Differentiable Viewpoint Planning for Long-Horizon Task Visualization
Donggang Jia, Da Li, Zainab Alsuwaykit, Sebastian Hartwig, Dominik Engel, Timo Ropinski, Ivan Viola
Long-horizon object-centered tasks in interactive 3D environments involve heterogeneous events that unfold across time and space, including fine-grained manipulations, larger structural motions, and subtle state transitions. Effectively visualizing such processes is essential for helping users understand how actions lead to outcomes over time. However, existing camera control methods in 3D systems are commonly designed for static objects, and often fail to maintain the legibility of task-relevant components and their evolving relationships throughout long-horizon executions. We present VISTA, a differentiable viewpoint planning framework for long-horizon task visualization. We formulate camera control as a visualization policy that optimizes camera parameters under a unified rendering-based quality objective and we propose five quality metrics to satisfy our five design goals. To support both local part inspection and interaction-centric visualization centered on a primary object, our formulation handles targets defined either as object--part pairs or as relational tuples involving another interacting object. This allows the planner to reveal not only what part is important, but also how it functionally relates to surrounding structures during task execution. We demonstrate that the same planner supports two complementary use cases: presentation-driven narrative visualization for communicating how long-horizon tasks unfold to human viewers, and agent-driven active observation within an observe-act loop. Together, these results position differentiable viewpoint planning as a general mechanism for ``how-things-work'' visualization of long-horizon object-centered interactions in 3D environments.
☆ Texture Consolidation of Generated Multi-View Renderings
Hongyi Liu, Yifan Wang, Vladimir G. Kim, Misha Kazhdan
We present a system for consolidating a sparse set of multi-view renderings into a single coherent texture image, providing a framework for integrating generative models for image generation within the geometry-processing pipeline. Given a texture-atlased triangle mesh and a set of renderings with associated camera parameters, we generate the texture image by: (1) back-projecting the renderings onto the mesh to obtain texture images with normalized colors and confidences; (2) solving for flow-fields on the mesh that align the colors to each other, taking into account the associated confidences; and (3) fusing the registered colors into a single texture image using gradient-domain stitching, again incorporating confidences. Our approach complements existing techniques for multi-view image generation, leveraging mesh-based optical flow and texture-space stitching. In doing so it addresses the challenge that while modern image generation pipelines generate highly convincing renderings, they struggle to produce renderings that are consistent across views. Code is available at https://github.com/hliuw/TextureConsolidation.
♻ ☆ Token-to-Token Alignment of Text Embeddings for Semantic Blending NeurIPS 2026
Saar Huberman, Ron Mokady, Or Patashnik, Daniel Cohen-Or
In modern generative models, images are specified and controlled through text prompts. In practice, images are generated from sequences of tokens derived from these prompts. However, the space of token sequences lacks a consistent accessible structure: semantically similar images may correspond to sequences that differ in wording, ordering, and placement of concepts, while similar token sequences may encode very different semantics. This apparent lack of structure makes it difficult to perform smooth transitions in this space, hindering applications such as image blending and continuous control of edits. We argue that this limitation stems not from the absence of semantic structure, but from misalignment between representations. To address this misalignment, we introduce Token-to-Token alignment, a framework that establishes explicit semantic correspondence between tokens across prompts. Our approach transforms prompts into a structured representation in which semantically corresponding concepts are mapped to consistent positions across prompts, and then aligns their token embeddings based on semantic similarity. Concretely, the method consists of two stages: a structural alignment that rephrases prompts into a shared structured form, followed by an embedding-level alignment that matches token representations across prompts. With this alignment in place, simple linear interpolation becomes a meaningful operation, producing smooth and coherent semantic transitions and enabling applications such as blending and continuous editing. Our results show that text embedding spaces in text-to-image models implicitly encode a continuous semantic structure that becomes accessible once representations are properly aligned, suggesting that semantic control can be achieved by organizing existing representations rather than modifying the generative model.

♻ ☆ ClawMMU: Protection-Coherent Virtual Memory for Stateful LLM Agents NeurIPS 2026
Jinhao Hu, Bardia Mohammadi, Mofasshara Binte Rafique, Lars Henning Klein, Akhil Arora, Ashvin Goel, Laurent Bindschaedler
AI agents keep long-term memory, much of which comes from web pages and tool results. Agent harnesses, the software that runs the model, do not record who may act on this memory, so an instruction hidden in a fetched page can be treated as the user's own instruction. We propose ClawMMU, a protection layer that labels every piece of memory with its owner, scope, origins, and authority. ClawMMU checks these labels whenever content enters the model's context, is written to storage, returns from storage, or drives a tool call. Its central rule is that information may rise in authority, but authority never lifts itself. Because the checks depend on where content came from, not on what it says, memory attacks become classical protection faults that the harness stops even when the model is fooled.

♻ ☆ Qurator: Scheduling Hybrid Quantum-Classical Workflows Across Heterogeneous Cloud Providers
Sinan Pehlivanoglu, Ulrik de Muelenaere, Peter Kogge, Amr Sabry
We present Qurator, a quantum-classical task scheduler that jointly optimizes queue time and circuit fidelity across heterogeneous architectures. Qurator models hybrid workloads as dynamic DAGs with explicit quantum semantics, including entanglement dependencies, synchronization barriers, no-cloning constraints, and circuit cutting and merging decisions. Fidelity is estimated through a unified logarithmic success score that reconciles incompatible calibration data from IBM, IonQ, IQM, Rigetti, AQT, Pasqal and other providers into a canonical set of gate error, readout fidelity, and decoherence terms. We evaluate Qurator on a simulator driven by four months of real queue data using circuits from the Munich Quantum Toolkit benchmark suite. Across load conditions from 5 to 35,000 quantum tasks with up to 120,000 classical tasks, Qurator stays within 1% of the highest-fidelity baseline at low load, achieves 30-75% queue time reduction at high load, all at a fidelity cost bounded by a user-specified target while improving on list and fair schedulers as well as state of the art schedulers on both metrics.

☆ ChroNoC: A Tableless TDM Circuit-Switched NoC With Delay-Aware Deployment for Time-Sensitive Neuromorphic Computing
Mohammad Rasoul Roshanshah, Mehdi Modarressi, Saeed Safari
Time-sensitive neuromorphic architectures require predictable interconnects to preserve precise event timing. Statically scheduled time-division-multiplexed (TDM) circuit-switched networks-on-chip (NoCs) provide deterministic latency at the cost of linear configuration-memory scaling with schedule depth. Furthermore, jointly optimizing task placement, routing, and scheduling creates an intractable combinatorial search space. Existing deployment frameworks also ignore algorithmic axonal delays by forcing immediate spike injections that increase network contention. This paper proposes a hardware-software co-design to address these challenges. ChroNoC is a tableless TDM NoC that embeds precomputed full-grid broadcast trees into combinational logic as a fixed routing substrate. This fixed substrate reduces deployment to a spatial search via Delay-Aware Reservations and Spatial Annealing (DARSA). DARSA converts axonal delays into periodic scheduling slack to stagger network injections and reduce communication overhead. In a 130nm CMOS process, ChroNoC reduces per-hop energy consumption by more than 75% relative to the evaluated circuit- and packet-switched baselines. On UCI-HAR and N-MNIST workloads, DARSA reduces per-timestep communication cycles by approximately 60% and 45%, respectively, compared with immediate transmission, while also reducing provisioned communication memory. These results demonstrate how combining lightweight TDM switch design with delay-aware deployment improves both hardware efficiency and system-level communication performance while preserving neural event timing.

☆ Toward Near-Optimal Floorplanning with Complex Design Constraints for Modern SoCs
Fuxing Huang, Hao Ai, Qiyuan Chen, Hao Wu, Wenxing Zhu, Xinning Liu, Ziran Zhu
Modern system-on-chip (SoC) floorplanning is increasingly challenged by highly coupled complex design constraints. Existing methods often relax constraints into objectives via multi-objective optimization, leading to infeasible solutions or degraded quality. To address this, we formulate the problem as a constrained optimization task and propose a novel three-stage analytical framework integrating semidefinite programming (SDP), augmented Lagrangian method (ALM), and second-order cone programming (SOCP), which enforces constraints more rigorously and achieves higher-quality solutions. First, we adopt a two-step SDP formulation to obtain a robust initialization in the non-convex search space. Next, we incorporate differentiable objectives and constraints into an incremental ALM to jointly handle coupled design constraints during global floorplanning. Finally, an SOCP-based legalization strictly enforces feasibility while further reducing area. Experiments on the FloorSet benchmark show that our method satisfies all constraints with the fastest runtime. Compared with state-of-the-art methods, it reduces wirelength and area by 90\% and 16\% on average, and achieves near-optimal results, with wirelength and area being merely 2\% and 1\% larger than the ground-truth references, respectively.

☆ Inductive Grounded Arithmetic: Goodstein, the Hydra, and $Γ_0$
Bryan Ford
What does a recursive definition mean when the logic is grounded, so a sentence is true only if a terminating process makes it so? Kripke's answer for the special case of truth was the least fixed point. This paper takes that answer as a rule. Reflective Grounded Arithmetic (RGA) already writes any recursive definition as one self-referential term, paradoxes included. Inductive Grounded Arithmetic (IGA) adds the elimination rule for every positive one, so that accessibility, the winning positions of a game, and grounded truth itself each come with their own induction principle. The rule is sound for the omega model and the system is consistent, both machine-checked in Isabelle/HOL. With the rule, IGA proves Goodstein's theorem outright, in a logic without implication at an undecided hypothesis, and the Kirby-Paris hydra game for primitive recursive strategies, both with every step of the arithmetic a grounded derivation. It proves the Cantor normal forms well-founded, from which the well-foundedness of the standard $\varepsilon_0$ notation system in HOL follows, and it certifies the Veblen notation as an ordinal below every $\varphi(n,0)$. It defines Kripke truth for RGA's own language as one fixed point that agrees with the omega model exactly. Two equivalences locate what the classical world has and the grounded one does not. IGA proves the whole Veblen notation well-founded exactly when it admits bivalence of accessibility for that order, and bivalence of accessibility for an order is sound exactly when the order is well-founded; so $Γ_0$ lies at most one instance of excluded middle away, at the accessibility predicate of the notation, and that instance, the well-foundedness sentence and one membership claim in a primitive-recursive form of Kleene's $\mathcal O$ are interderivable over IGA.
☆ PastaLean: Deterministic transpilation from Python to Lean for Verification
Anirudh Gupta, Petros Markopoulos, Siddhartha Gadgil
We present PastaLean, an extensible system for deterministic translation from Python to Lean via the Abstract Syntax Tree of Python. This allows for verification of Python code, especially auxiliary code used by AI. Every program is translated into a provable twin written in the form Lean's verification tooling can reason about, and a runnable twin that gives a fast and correct computation support. We also introduce our Type Inference engine TypeInfer which provides static typing support for untyped Python code. Our tool provides coverage of more than 92\% for LeetCode and HumanEval\textsuperscript{+} and more than 98\% for LiveCodeBench ground truth Python files. Further, at least 97\% of each dataset's (numerical) test cases pass, with 100\% on HumanEval\textsuperscript{+} and LiveCodeBench. The TypeInfer engine is the best-scoring tool on the TypeEvalPy static leaderboard, and on a repository-level TypyBench benchmark it scores above every competing repository-level inference tool.
☆ SGL: Towards a Bridging Model for Heterogeneous Hierarchical Platforms
Chong Li, Gaétan Hains
This paper introduces the Scatter-Gather parallel-programming and parallel-execution model in the form of a simple imperative Scatter-Gather Language (SGL). Its design is based on past experience with bulk-synchronous parallel (BSP) programming and BSP language design. ♻ ☆ Special Delivery: Programming with Mailbox Types (Extended Version)
Simon Fowler, Duncan Paul Attard, Danielle Marshall, Simon J. Gay, Phil Trinder
The asynchronous and unidirectional communication model supported by mailboxes is a key reason for the success of actor languages like Erlang and Elixir for implementing reliable and scalable distributed systems. Although actors eliminate many of the issues stemming from shared memory concurrency, they remain vulnerable to communication errors such as protocol violations and deadlocks. Behavioural types make it possible to detect communication errors early in the development process, but most work has addressed channel-based languages rather than actor languages. Mailbox types are a novel behavioural type system for actors first introduced for a process calculus, and capture the contents of a mailbox as a commutative regular expression. Due to aliasing and nested evaluation contexts, moving from a process calculus to a programming language is challenging. This paper presents Pat, the first programming language incorporating mailbox types, and describes an algorithmic type system. Pat is a higher-order functional language with sums, products, and lists, along with interfaces that allow finer-grained reasoning about mailbox contents. Typechecking in Pat detects four classes of behavioural error: protocol violation, unexpected message, forgotten reply and self-deadlock, as well as the usual data type errors. The Pat type system makes essential use of quasi-linear typing to tame some of the complexity introduced by aliasing. We make use of a co-contextual algorithmic type system, achieved through a novel use of backwards bidirectional typing, and we prove it sound and complete with respect to our declarative type system. We implement a type checker, and use it to demonstrate the expressiveness of Pat on a factory automation case study and a series of examples from the Savina actor benchmark suite. This establishes a foundation for applying mailbox typing to practical actor languages.

♻ ☆ Certified Split Windows for Parallel Lexing: Recovering Boundaries Where No Byte Certifies SP
Nicklas Nidhögg
A certified split point lets a parallel lexer cut unlexed input at a single byte with the serial token stream provably preserved, but several conventional token sets in the predecessor's controlled study certify no byte once string, comment, or whitespace-run forms are included (arXiv:2608.03473v2). We generalize from a byte to a bounded window, a byte string after which the position where the current token began is known, regardless of surrounding context. We certify the directly usable form of that recovery, namely that the token covering the window's final byte begins at the reported origin. The certificate is conditional on occurrence and may be vacuous; every applicability figure counts only windows carrying an asserted completely tokenizable occurrence witness. We give a conservative model of a maximal-munch scanner's possible histories across a window, prove it sound, and decide reachability in that model exactly by exhausting a finite quotient of its reachable configurations. Within the flat, completely tokenizable scope, model-positive answers are semantic certificates; negatives are relative to the model, which deliberately refuses some windows a greedy scanner would allow. Whether the model certifies any window, plain or witnessed, is PSPACE-complete, and a shortest window can need $2^{Ω(\sqrt{n})}$ bytes over n live states. Two smaller searches decide plain model existence, the second shipped in release 2.2.0 of munch. In a sample of 400 random token sets, 322 of the 337 sets certifying no byte gain a witnessed window, with zero inconclusive searches, and every exact-empty row of the predecessor's study gains a witnessed window of two to four bytes. The analysis runs once after automaton construction, using only the compiled tables and no input. A boundary profile marks each gap of a window forced, crossed or open, and a window-local cut is sound exactly at forced gaps.

♻ ☆ Certified Split Points for Parallel Lexing: Exact and Modulo Discarded Tokens SP
Nicklas Nidhögg
Table-driven DFA lexing is sequential, since each transition depends on the previous byte's state. Scanning one input in parallel needs each chunk's entry state, which existing methods recover by simulation, speculation, prescanning, or overlap. We give two conditions under which none is needed. For a longest-match scanner restarting from q0 at every token boundary, a byte b is a certified split symbol when no reachable state other than q0 has a b-transition whose target can reach acceptance, and q0 is not re-entrant if it has one. Every occurrence of such a byte in completely tokenizable input begins a token, so chunks starting there reproduce the serial sequence of kinds and lengths. The condition is necessary and sufficient, but one string, comment, or whitespace run can remove every useful certificate. Comments and whitespace are usually discarded, so we weaken the guarantee to equality after deleting a declared discarded set and give a second condition, a precomputed constant-time one-bit test. It is sound but incomplete and more permissive, strictly so on suitable token sets and discarded sets. It recovers newline for a conventional C-like tokenization and tab, newline and carriage return for JSON, and refuses newline where block comments are unrestricted. Safety modulo the discarded set is decidable in PSPACE for every token automaton and in polynomial time for literal vocabularies, and every unsafe byte has a replayable counterexample. On the fifteen token sets studied the second condition is already exact. Splitting at exact certificates in munch reaches 92.6-95.3% parallel efficiency at eight threads on a restricted CPU set and a 512 MiB corpus, and a 3.46-3.94x end-to-end speedup at four threads, on one machine. It turns delimiter-based parallel lexing from a language-specific assumption into a property a compiler checks.

♻ ☆ Internal Reasoning and Consistency in Reflective Grounded Arithmetic
Bryan Ford
Reflective Grounded Arithmetic (RGA) is a paracomplete arithmetic allowing direct expression of any general-recursive computation. Every RGA term is a program and truth is grounded evaluation, with a proof certificate at each universal quantifier. This paper shows, in a machine-checked Isabelle/HOL development, that such a system is powerful enough to carry out its own metamathematics. A fuel-indexed decider of RGA's grounded semantics and checkers for RGA's proofs are compiled primitive-recursive RGA terms. RGA proves internally that its decider never affirms $0 = 1$, and that every well-formed term it affirms has an RGA proof accepted by a transparent test. RGA proves that, for each valid RGA proof, its conclusion is true. RGA proves that each grounded-true well-formed term is provable. RGA proves that no proof in either of two decided classes of RGA proofs derives $0 = 1$. Gödel's incompleteness theorems hold for RGA, but their proofs must be adapted. In RGA an implication $A \rightarrow B$ is strictly stronger than an entailment $A \vdash B$: RGA discharges a hypothesis only when it can prove the hypothesis decided, a restriction needed to defuse Curry's paradox. RGA's implication is material and RGA has the disjunction property, so RGA proves no implication between ungrounded sentences. The implications that the textbook proof of Gödel's second theorem uses are of this kind and fail, but the entailments and rules it uses hold: Gödel's proof survives as entailments. RGA does not prove its consistency sentence $\mathit{Con}$: RGA carries out Gödel's argument internally, with a decided proof predicate and explicit proof transformers, and only the last step, that RGA does not prove $0 = 1$, is left to HOL. RGA also proves its own second incompleteness theorem as an entailment from $\mathit{Con}$, and as a single sentence quantified over proofs.

Data Structures and Algorithms 12
☆ Unconditional Lower Bounds for the Fredman--Khachiyan Algorithms for Monotone Boolean Duality Testing
Khaled Elbassioni
We prove unconditional lower bounds on the number of recursive calls made by the two algorithms of Fredman and Khachiyan (1996) for monotone Boolean duality testing, as a function of the explicit input size $N$. For Algorithm A we show a lower bound of $N^{Ω(\log N)}$ on the dual pairs $(\HH_k,\LL_k)$ obtained from the read-once family of Gurvich and Khachiyan. The proof lower-bounds the number of leaves of every binary decision tree computing the function by the modulus of its multilinear polynomial evaluated at a complex point of the unit circle. It therefore applies to every choice of branching variables and, more generally, to the class of two-child variable-restriction procedures whose terminal calls have polynomially bounded decision-tree complexity. On this family the number of calls is exactly $N^{Θ(\log N)}$. For Algorithm B, a symmetric specialization of the polynomial padding construction of Kobayashi, Kurita, and Wasa, with full polynomial blocks, yields at least $q^{(t-1)k-t(t-1)/2}$ calls, where $k=4t^2$ and $q=Θ(t^2)$; as a function of $N$ this is $N^{Ω(\sqrt{\log N/\log\log N})}$ along an infinite family. In an appendix we show that applying the padding construction directly to Algorithm A gives $Ω(2^n)$ calls on a dual pair obtained from an unsatisfiable $n$-variable formula, which is only $N^{Ω(\sqrt{\log N/\log\log N})}$ in terms of the input size. No complexity-theoretic assumption is used. The proofs allow every branching-variable choice permitted by the respective algorithm. They concern the original recursive procedures, counting every invocation separately, without sharing calls or adding positive stopping tests.
☆ Online Discrepancy of Sparse Random Vectors over Long Horizons
Nicola Wengiel
We study online vector balancing for independent sparse random vectors: each arrival is supported on a uniformly random set of $s$ of the $n$ coordinates, with nonzero entries equal to one (binary model) or independent random signs (signed model), and an online algorithm must sign each vector upon arrival. The prefix discrepancy is the largest $\ell_\infty$-norm of the signed sums up to the horizon $T$, the terminal discrepancy that of the sum at time $T$. For binary inputs with $2\le s\le n/2$ and $T=Θ(n)$, Altschuler and Tikhomirov showed that both are of order $\sqrt{s}+\log\log n$; they asked how the prefix discrepancy depends on the horizon and whether a matching terminal bound holds at every prescribed time. Over long horizons, the two differ. For every fixed $s\ge2$ and $c>0$, every online algorithm, even a randomized one that knows the horizon, incurs with high probability prefix discrepancy $Ω(\log\log n+(\log T/\log n)^{α_s})$ for $n\le T\le e^{cn}$, where $α_s=(2^{s-1}-1)/(2^s-1)$. For $s=2$, quadratic Greedy matches this bound; for fixed $s\ge3$, we give an online algorithm with prefix discrepancy $O(\log\log n+(\log T/\log n)^{1/2})$. In contrast, a deterministic algorithm that knows neither the horizon nor the failure probability keeps the terminal discrepancy $O_k(\sqrt{s}+\log\log n)$ at each prescribed time, with failure probability $n^{-k}$ for every fixed $k$, for $2\le s\le n/2$ (binary model) and $2\le s\le n$ (signed model); for $s=2$, quadratic Greedy itself does so. This answers their terminal question, and the same order is optimal for prefixes at all polynomial horizons $T\ge n$. The lower bound uses a finite safety game realized within the input law; the upper bounds use moments of elementary symmetric polynomials and, at terminal times, a $\cosh$ potential or a translated gauge with a Foster inequality at every state.

☆ A Nearly Optimal Min-Cut Algorithm for Dynamic Streams
Yang P. Liu, Hoai-An Nguyen, Vihan Shah, David P. Woodruff
We give an $\tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon)$-space algorithm for the minimum cut problem in dynamic streams, resolving the space complexity of streaming min-cut up to polylogarithmic factors. Ding, Garces, Li, Lin, Nelson, Shah, and Woodruff (ITCS 2025) obtained the same $\tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon)$ space bound for insertion-only streams, while prior to our work the best known bound even for unweighted dynamic streams was $\tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^2)$, due to Ahn, Guha, and McGregor (PODS 2012). Our main technical contribution is an $\tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon)$-space for-each cut sketch in dynamic streams, which may be of independent interest. The construction applies to weighted graphs under general $\pmΔ$ updates, a stronger model than the standard dynamic-stream setting in which weighted edges are inserted and deleted in their entirety. Despite this stronger guarantee, our algorithm is surprisingly simple and relies on very little machinery. We also obtain applications to minimum $\ell_p$-norm $k$-partitioning for every fixed $k$, weighted correlation-clustering evaluation for two clusters, and for-each cut sketching in balanced directed graphs.
♻ ☆ Faster Parallel Batch-Dynamic Algorithms for Low Out-Degree Orientation
Guy Blelloch, Andrew Brady, Laxman Dhulipala, Jeremy Fineman, Kishen Gowda, Chase Hutton
A low out-degree orientation directs each edge of an undirected graph with the goal of minimizing the maximum out-degree of a vertex. In the parallel batch-dynamic setting, one can insert or delete batches of edges, and the goal is to process the entire batch in parallel with work per edge similar to that of a single sequential update and with span (or depth) for the entire batch that is polylogarithmic. In this paper we present work-efficient parallel batch-dynamic algorithms for maintaining a low out-degree orientation of an undirected graph, both in the amortized and worst-case settings. All results herein achieve polylogarithmic depth; the focus of this paper is on minimizing the work, which varies across results. In the amortized setting, we give a parallel batch-dynamic algorithm that maintains a $O(c)$-orientation in $O(\log n)$ work per update in expectation, where $c$ is a known upper bound on the arboricity over the update sequence. Our algorithm has optimal work in expectation. This result is the parallelization of the classic dynamic orientation algorithm of Brodal and Fagerberg [WADS '99], and, in this setting, is a logarithmic factor faster than the best prior parallel amortized algorithm of Liu et al. [SPAA '22]. In the worst-case setting, we give an $O(c+\log n)$-orientation with worst-case expected work per update $O(\log n)$. This is work-efficient, matching the best known sequential dynamic work of Berglin and Brodal [Algorithmica '20], and implies the existence of an $O(c)$-orientation algorithm with $O(\log^2 n)$ worst-case expected work per update. Both of our algorithms also have deterministic bounds with an additional logarithmic factor in the work. Similar to Berglin and Brodal, by adjusting the parameters of our worst-case algorithm, we achieve tradeoffs where the work is faster than $O(\log n)$ in expectation per update but the max out-degree is worse.

♻ ☆ Optimal regret for dynamic matching with static priorities
Süleyman Kerimov, Pengyu Qian, Mingwei Yang, Sophie H. Yu
We study centralized two-way dynamic matching markets in which agents arrive stochastically over time and wait in queues associated with their types. We focus on static priority policies that assign each agent type a fixed ranking over its neighbors and make online matching decisions using only queue availability. We propose the General Priority ($\mathbf{GP}$) policy, which builds on the Tree Priority ($\mathbf{TP}$) policy introduced in [KAG25]. Under the general position gap condition, $\mathbf{GP}$ selects matches using a nondegenerate optimal basic solution of the fluid relaxation. It prioritizes children over parents within trees and consistently gives clockwise neighbors priority over counterclockwise neighbors on each cycle. We establish the optimal all-time regret scaling $O(ε^{-1})$ for $\mathbf{GP}$ on general two-way matching networks, where the general position gap $ε$ measures the fluid relaxation's distance from degeneracy. In particular, this resolves the conjecture by [KAG25] on the optimal $ε$-dependence for $\mathbf{TP}$.
♻ ☆ 0.4633-Approximation for Online and Offline on-Monotone DR-Submodular Maximization
Vaneet Aggarwal, Donglei Du
We obtain the approximation coefficient $α_0=54440750000/117486474169>0.463378$ for maximizing nonnegative, non-monotone DR-submodular functions over compact convex down-closed subsets of the unit cube, with queries allowed throughout the cube. This improves the previous best coefficient, $4/9$. A coordinatewise map, exponential on small coordinates and linear-fractional on large ones, turns the learner's state, a feasible point, into a feasible action whose value is at least $α_0$ times that of any feasible point, up to a linear term. The proof is analytical and reduces to one-dimensional inequalities. The linear term has an unbiased estimator from one noisy-gradient query, so online maximization reduces to linear learning. Under a uniform bound on the gradients, the expected approximate regret is $O(\sqrt T)$ with projected ascent and $O(T^{3/4})$ with one linear-maximization call per round; with one noisy value per round, it is $O(T^{3/4})$ and $O(T^{7/8})$. Online-to-batch conversion gives offline guarantees. With exact values and gradients, contracting the objective toward a feasible interior point yields an expected $(α_0-\varepsilon)$-approximation for every nonnegative $C^1$ DR-submodular objective, with oracle counts polynomial in $n$ and $\varepsilon^{-1}$ and no gradient or smoothness parameter. Sampling and standard rounding give $(α_0-\varepsilon)$-approximation for submodular set functions under one matroid or a fixed number of knapsack constraints.
♻ ☆ k-SUM Hardness Implies Treewidth-SETH
Michael Lampis
We show that if k-SUM is hard, in the sense that the standard algorithm is essentially optimal, then a variant of the SETH called the Primal Treewidth SETH is true. Formally: if there is an $\varepsilon>0$ and an algorithm which solves SAT in time $(2-\varepsilon)^{tw}|φ|^{O(1)}$, where $tw$ is the width of a given tree decomposition of the primal graph of the input, then there exists a randomized algorithm which solves k-SUM in time $n^{(1-δ)\frac{k}{2}}$ for some $δ>0$ and all sufficiently large $k$. We also establish an analogous result for the k-XOR problem, where integer addition is replaced by component-wise addition modulo $2$. As an application of our reduction we are able to revisit tight lower bounds on the complexity of several fundamental problems parameterized by treewidth (Independent Set, Max Cut, $k$-Coloring). Our results imply that these bounds, which were initially shown under the SETH, also hold if one assumes the k-SUM or k-XOR Hypotheses, arguably increasing our confidence in their validity.

♻ ☆ Prefix Sharing Is a Sorting Problem
Rong He
LLM serving reuses KV cache by exact prefix match, so when a prompt is assembled from a set of reusable pieces -- retrieved passages, tool definitions, few-shot exemplars -- the order chosen for those pieces determines how much computation can be shared. Every deployed system fixes that order by a single global convention. We prove this is optimal only when requests contain at most two pieces, and asymptotically wrong in general. Our main result is a structure theorem: the minimum prefix-trie cost equals min_H sum_x w(x) t_x(H) over binary hierarchies H on the requests, where t_x(H) is the canonical decomposition size of the set of requests needing chunk x. Choosing chunk orders is therefore equivalent to choosing one hierarchy over requests. The identity yields an O(3^m) exact algorithm, identifies the two-chunk case as minimum vertex cover, and shows that on the leave-one-out family the optimum is the minimum external path length of a binary tree -- the merge-sort recursion -- so a global order pays Theta(n^2) against a true cost of Theta(n log n). Agglomerative clustering by common intersection is a tight 1/2-approximation for the achievable saving. On BM25 retrieval traces over three BEIR corpora the resulting layout reduces prefill by 17-36% against production RAG ordering, and the margin widens with retrieval depth as the theory predicts. Serving requests in the hierarchy's DFS order finally lets a cache holding one request's context attain the unbounded-cache optimum exactly, so cache capacity and reorder window act as substitutes.

♻ ☆ Geometry-Dependent Approximation for Non-Monotone $k$-Submodular Maximization
Vaneet Aggarwal
We study the maximization of a nonnegative $k$-submodular function, not assumed monotone, in which each of $n$ elements receives one of $k\ge2$ labels or stays unselected and the selected elements must satisfy a constraint. For a compact convex down-closed region $P\subseteq[0,1]^n$ of fractional selection vectors, the diagonal level $ζ(P)=\max\{t\in[0,1]:t(1,\ldots,1)\in P\}$ is the largest selection probability that can be given to all elements at once; it equals $r/n$ for a uniform matroid of rank $r$, and $ζ(P)=1$ means that the constraint is vacuous. We give approximation coefficients that increase continuously with $ζ$. They come from one inequality for the multilinear extension of the objective: the algorithm computes an action without querying the objective, and objective feedback gives a comparator-independent update direction. For $k\ge3$, feasibility of the action reduces to three polynomial inequalities in one variable; for $k=2$ the profile is closed form. At $ζ=0$ the coefficients are $0.4456\ldots$ for $k=2$ and $0.4541\ldots$ for every $k\ge3$. For every finite $k\ge3$ they reach $1/2$ above an explicit level, which is $0.4026\ldots$ for $k=3,4,5$, and at $ζ=1$ they reach $1/2$ for $k=2$, $(\sqrt{17}-3)/2$ for $k=3,4$, and $k/(2k-1)$ for $k\ge5$. Online linear optimization gives $O(\sqrt T)$ approximate regret, with gradient feedback or with value queries made after each action. The coefficients carry over to matroids, with expected and high-probability guarantees, and to any fixed number of knapsacks: the zero-level coefficients hold on every instance, and the larger ones when items are small or when prefix enumeration keeps the diagonal level on one branch. For one budget the enumeration exponent is $O(1/\varepsilon)$.
♻ ☆ A Near-Optimal Lower Bound for $\ell_p$-Subspace Embeddings, $1\leq p<2$
Yi Li
For $d \geq 2$, $p \geq 1$ and $ε> 0$, let $N_p(d,ε)$ be the smallest integer $N$ such that for every integer $n$ and every $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$, there exists a matrix $Φ\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}$ satisfying $(1-ε)\lVert Ax\rVert_p\leq \lVertΦA x\rVert_p\leq (1+ε)\lVert Ax\rVert_p$ for all $x\in\mathbb{R}^d$. For every constant $p\geq 1$ with $p\not\in 2\mathbb{Z}$, when $d\gtrsim_p \log(1/ε)$, the bound \[ N_p(d,ε) \gtrsim_{p} \frac{d}{ε^2 \operatorname{polylog}(d/ε)} \] is established. This improves the previous lower bound $Ω(1/(ε^2\operatorname{polylog}(1/ε)))$ due to Li et al. (SICOMP 2021) and is optimal up to logarithmic factors for $1\leq p<2$. The central technical idea originated from ChatGPT 6 Pro.

☆ RacerGS: Residual-Autoregressive Context for 3D Gaussian Splatting Compression
Wenqing Wang, Huimin Zeng, Yun Fu
3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as a promising approach for fast, high-quality novel-view rendering. However, its large parameter size remains a bottleneck for storage and transmission. We propose RacerGS, a compact 3DGS compression framework that achieves scene reconstruction at significantly reduced storage sizes with preserved fidelity. To obtain compact and spatially coherent features, we design a hash projection model that projects the anchor hash encodings into compact context features. Additionally, we introduce a FiLM-modulated autoregressive GRU context model that utilizes causal dependencies among anchor feature groups and uses recurrent hidden states to refine entropy-model parameters, improving bitrate efficiency. Furthermore, we adaptively entropy-code quantized anchor-attribute residuals relative to their context-predicted means, leveraging spatial consistency to reduce symbol entropy. Overall, RacerGS achieves more than 150x compression over vanilla 3DGS and 28x over Scaffold-GS, while providing comparable rendering quality. Extensive experiments across five datasets demonstrate that RacerGS outperforms prior 3DGS compression methods in both storage reduction and rate-distortion performance.
☆ Single Camera based Simulation and Try-on Ready Avatar Modeling
Honghu Chen, Bo Peng, Jun Xiang, Jingtao Zhou, Xujing Chen, Juyong Zhang
Reconstructing and rendering high-fidelity, animatable, and try-on-ready virtual avatars using only a single camera is crucial for many applications. However, existing try-on-ready human avatar reconstruction methods struggle to achieve this from monocular videos or single images, primarily due to two issues: (1) existing approaches typically use a single layer to represent both garments and the underlying body, ignoring their distinct motion characteristics; and (2) input captured from a single camera often fails to fully cover both the garments and the underlying body. To address these challenges, we propose a physically integrated, double-layer GS-Mesh representation for reconstructing decoupled 3D clothed human avatars from monocular videos or even single images by incorporating 2D generated data. While 2D generation methods can produce video sequences from given pose sequences, they struggle to maintain inter-frame consistency. We decompose the inter-frame inconsistencies in the generated data into geometric and appearance inconsistencies. Based on our 3D representation, we ensure that the final reconstruction and rendering results are both 3D-consistent and temporally coherent. Compared with existing 3D garment transfer methods that rely on multi-view reconstruction and most 2D approaches that use diffusion models for image-based garment editing without novel-view or novel-pose synthesis, our method requires only a monocular video or even a single image while producing visually plausible cloth dynamics during virtual try-on and novel-pose animation. This represents a significant advancement toward virtual try-on with visually plausible cloth dynamics using only a single camera.

♻ ☆ Building Drift: Documenting On-Site Construction Adaptations Across Material Lifecycles
Ritik Batra, Martin Tamke, Tom Svilans, Jan Huls, Amritansh Kwatra, Steven J. Jackson, Thijs Roumen, Mette Ramsgaard Thomsen
In a circular economy for construction, reclaimed materials carry prior lives of use and go on to have post-lives in future buildings. Yet working with such materials introduces unpredictability that requires on-site improvisation, making their reuse challenging to document and scale across building lifetimes. Without documentation, the on-site adaptations that make construction with reclaimed materials possible leave collaborators, evaluators, and inheritors without the information they need to continue, assess, and reuse materials. We define the collective deviation of the physical state from the digital model through these adaptations as "building drift." Through a case study, ReShelter, a reclaimed timber pavilion constructed in the forest, we develop a preliminary description of building drift as emerging from five practices essential to building with reclaimed materials: Tending the Site, Foraging for Fit, Interpreting the Material, Marking Measurements, and Coordinating Across Communities. We then present Pentimento, a documentation tool that leverages video documentation and 3D Gaussian Splatting to spatially, temporally, and semantically represent on-site adaptations in relation to the designed model. Pentimento enables each stakeholder to navigate material histories in ways that reduce barriers to material reuse. Together, these contributions open pathways towards computational tools that support the on-site improvisation essential to construction with reclaimed materials, enabling more sustainable cycles of recovery, repair, and reuse.

♻ ☆ Semantic-Structural Alignment for Generative Pictorial Charts
Zhida Sun, Yulin Zhang, Zheng Gu, Min Lu, Bongshin Lee, Daniel Cohen-Or, Hui Huang
Traditional statistical graphics are precise but often lack the visual appeal, memorability, and engagement of pictorial charts. We present a generative framework for the automated synthesis of pictorial charts that bridges the gap between semantic expression and structural faithfulness. Rather than treating charts merely as images to be stylized, we frame the problem as a dual-conditioned generation task guided by two parallel external control signals: a text prompt capturing the semantic context of the editing intent, and a context image providing the abstract statistical chart's global structure. To reinforce these controls within a Multi-Modal Diffusion Transformer, we introduce two complementary feature-level mechanisms: structural alignment to anchor spatial layouts to the input chart, and semantic alignment to transfer expressive textures from reference images. Generalizing across major visual channels (i.e., length, area, angle, and position) and diverse semantic domains, our method produces pictorial charts that are both artistically compelling and structurally consistent. Extensive quantitative evaluations and perceptual user studies demonstrate that our framework outperforms traditional controllable generation and image editing baselines, providing a foundation for high-fidelity, data-driven generative modeling in expressive visual storytelling. ☆ Ring-0 by Default: Agents as Operating Systems (AaOS)
Xiang Li, Aichun Huang, Zuyao Xu, Fasheng Miao, Zheli Liu
LLM agents now read and write files, start processes and subagents, fetch network content, load third-party extensions, and save and resume state, which are the responsibilities of an operating system. They gained these functions within about three years, but not the protections that operating systems built over five decades: whatever the model outputs, whether it follows the user, a tool result, or a downloaded skill, runs with the full authority of the host user, in effect at ring 0. Prior work builds an operating system for agents (OS4A); we argue that a deployed agent already is like an operating system (AaOS) and analyze it as one. Taking the mechanisms of standard OS textbooks as a checklist, we map 116 mechanisms in 19 subsystems to their agent counterparts, from product documentation, source code, and the literature, and label each as isomorphic, degraded (function present, protection dependent on the model), missing, mutated, or novel. Only 11 are isomorphic and 65 are missing, most of them protections: privilege separation, resource quotas, transactions, and tamper-proof audit. Existing defenses confine the whole agent or review its extensions and leave this interior unprotected, and by Anderson's reference-monitor criteria (non-bypassable, tamper-proof, verifiable), rules written in the prompt satisfy none. We test the map with thirteen experiments on Claude Code, Codex CLI, and Gemini CLI, covering 25 mechanisms whose enforcement documentation cannot settle: 16 match the analysis, 7 gain a more precise description, and 2 change verdict. Checks sit in single tools, so the shell bypasses them; all three CLIs load forged transcript records on resume; and task control, sub-agent permissions, and model-based monitors are weak. We identify five problems for which no OS defense transfers and derive a prioritized list of open problems from the gaps of the map.

☆ Operating System Support for Accelerators as First-Class Compute Elements in Heterogeneous Systems
Mohammad Shahidzadeh, Lesley Shannon
Heterogeneous systems-on-chip (SoCs) increasingly rely on domain-specific hardware accelerators (HAs) to deliver performance and energy efficiency gains beyond what general-purpose processors can provide. However, operating systems (O/S), such as Linux, assume that processors are the Compute Elements (CEs) and HAs are peripheral devices rather than specialized CEs. As a result, HA execution incurs substantial software (SW) overheads that reduce the effective benefits of hardware (HW) acceleration. This work demonstrates that significant inefficiencies arise from ``O/S peripheral device taxes'' (O/S taxes), including SW stack-accelerator communication, invocation, interrupt handling, and scheduling. We built MEERKAT, a Field-Programmable Gate Array (FPGA)-based framework for configurable multicore systems that include HAs; it allows the user to migrate Linux O/S support from SW to HW and identify, study, and quantify the impact. Here we study four major sources of O/S tax and introduce techniques to mitigate them. These include a virtual-memory-aware (VMA) communication method that enables efficient HA access to application data, a lightweight HA invocation interface, Interrupt Policy Adaptation (IPA) for dynamic polling and interrupt selection, and an HA-aware scheduler that dynamically distributes work across Central Processing Units (CPUs) and multiple HA instances. Experimental results demonstrate up to $260\times$ faster communication and $2$--$6\times$ performance improvements in wall-clock time over conventional schedulers. Furthermore, our study reveals that overall system performance depends not only on HA speed, but also on O/S overheads, resource availability, and scheduling decisions. These findings highlight the importance of treating HAs as first-class computational resources and motivate a more accelerator-centric O/S design for future heterogeneous systems.

☆ Real-Time Recurrent Qubit-State Discrimination on a Heterogeneous AI-Engine-FPGA Accelerator
Vu Le, Yilun Xu, Neel Vora, Gang Huang, Deepak Ganesan, Phuc Nguyen
Qubit readout, or qubit state discrimination, remains one of the slowest and most error-prone operations in superconducting quantum processors, making fast, accurate, and hardware-efficient classification essential for scalable quantum computing. Conventional discriminators integrate the I/Q record into a single point, discarding evidence a relaxing qubit leaves behind; full-resolution feed-forward classifiers recover it but are too costly to deploy under this constraint. Recurrent classifiers retain full resolution far more cheaply, but their loop-carried hidden state cannot fit a single AI Engine (AIE) tile's register capacity and spills to data memory, adding latency that grows with sequence length. We present a hardware-aware LSTM accelerator for an AMD Versal device that resolves this by partitioning at the dependency boundary - independent input projections on the AIE array, the loop-carried update in programmable logic (PL) - keeping pace with a 64 ns input cadence under a 1,000 ns budget. On the qubit affected by mid-readout state transitions, our design matches a full-resolution feed-forward network's accuracy (96.59% vs. 96.37%) using 403x fewer parameters and roughly 103x fewer DSPs while cutting relative error by 68.2% over GMM, 43.6% over QubiCML, and 16.82% over MF-NN. Using 4 out of 400 AIE tiles and 17 DSP58, it needs 2.2-8.2x fewer DSP58 blocks than QubiCML and MF-NN, and the AIE-PL partition cuts exposed tail latency by 60.3% versus a pure-AIE mapping and DSP58 usage 31.1x versus an all-PL implementation. Across eight qubits, a compact variant reaches 96.78% mean accuracy, the strongest hardware-deployed operating point. Temporal resolution and recurrent-state placement must then be treated jointly when designing low-latency quantum readout accelerators.

☆ Adapting DNNs to Embrace Voltage Scaling Induced Errors via Accumulation-Aware Quantization
Alexis Maras, Panagiotis Chaidos, Georgios Alexandris, Dimitrios Soudris, Sotirios Xydis
The pervasive deployment of Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) across the computing spectrum has brought growing attention to energy efficiency, alongside raw performance. While voltage scaling is a potent knob for power reduction, its application in next-generation nodes is hampered by severe throughput degradation and susceptibility to process variation. This work aims to address these energy-reliability trade-offs by introducing a hardware-software co-design methodology, based on the observation that timing-robust bit ranges within the accumulator of a systolic array vary significantly across different voltage-frequency operating points. At the software level, we propose an Accumulation-Aware Training approach that incorporates this information as a first-order optimization constraint during training. By integrating mixed-precision quantization with unstructured pruning, we effectively constrain the model's weight distribution to avoid activating the hardware's sensitized high-rank carry chains. At the architectural level, we complement this with selective MSB-masking and fine-grained clock-gating to minimize unnecessary dynamic switching activity. Furthermore, we employ a layer-wise Dynamic Frequency Scaling strategy to further adapt to the accumulator's reduced dynamic range, based on the numerical sensitivity of each layer. We experimentally evaluate the proposed solution on a deep scaled 2nm technology node and demonstrate that, when operating at a low-voltage frontier, our co-optimized post-layout architecture delivers substantial energy and energy-delay product gains of 39% and 23% respectively, relative to the nominal voltage implementation, while maintaining accuracy degradation within 1%.

☆ ZipFusion: Fusing Lossless KV Decompression with Attention for Efficient LLM Decoding
Yuetao Chen, Peng Wang, Hong Xu
The key-value (KV) cache places growing demands on GPU memory capacity and bandwidth as large language models serve longer contexts and more concurrent requests. Its footprint limits concurrency, while repeated KV reads during attention can make decoding memory-bandwidth bound. Prior lossless KV cache compression methods reduce transfers between devices but restore the uncompressed KV cache in GPU memory before attention. They therefore leave these repeated KV reads unchanged and require additional work and memory for decompression. We present ZipFusion, which enables attention to consume the losslessly compressed KV cache directly. ZipFusion co-designs the compression format and attention kernel to reduce the bytes read from GPU memory during attention. The kernel reconstructs the original values directly in registers using simple bit operations, avoiding a separate decompression pass and easing the memory-bandwidth bound. ZipFusion also reassigns memory freed by compression across layers to store additional KV cache. Its memory manager reserves space for ongoing generation and makes the remaining capacity available to new requests. We implement ZipFusion on top of FlashInfer and integrate it with SGLang. On an NVIDIA H100, ZipFusion achieves about 1.25x speedup over FlashInfer in decode attention. With 8K context, it improves model decode throughput by up to 26.3% over uncompressed SGLang under the same GPU memory budget. At each method's largest supported batch, it also delivers up to 2.76x the throughput of the best-performing lossless KV cache compression baseline.
☆ Optimizing Physical Neural Networks through Device Variable Selection
Sjoerd van den Belt, Manuel Escudero, Mohamadreza Zolfagharinejad, Wilfred G. van der Wiel, Nikolaos Alachiotis
Physical neural networks (PNNs) leverage device physics in electronics, optics, and related domains to enable energy-efficient neural networks. Broken-isomorphism PNNs replace linear operations with multivariate physical transformations, modifying how input signals are mapped to device variables. We exploit network architectures emerging from these mappings by treating the routing between signals and input variables of physical devices as trainable parameters. This approach expands device-level tunability of the physical transformations while, at the system level, demonstrating improved expressivity of PNNs with negligible additional inference overhead. This discrete routing is optimized by training a categorical distribution using Gumbel-Softmax reparametrization. We evaluate this approach using experimentally calibrated differentiable models of multivariate electronic RNPUs for MNIST and CIFAR-10 classification. Using variable selection, we improve the MNIST classification accuracy for a small CNN model from 94.5% to 96.9% and the accuracy of CIFAR-10 from 62.8% to 68.0%. Our method uniquely leverages the induced biases of broken-isomorphism PNN architectures, paving the way towards more efficient physics-based neural network inference.
☆ DiffRouter: A Differentiable Routing Framework for UltraScale FPGAs
Xiaohan Gao, Zhili Xiong, Yusu Wang, David Z. Pan
Routing has become a major bottleneck in large-scale FPGA design flows, where traditional routers rely on sequential net-by-net processing and rip-up-reroute heuristics. This inherent sequentialism limits parallelism, leads to suboptimal utilization of routing resources, and significantly increases runtime on modern FPGAs. This paper introduces DiffRouter, the first differentiable, gradient-based FPGA routing framework with GPU acceleration. By lifting routing into a continuous optimization space, DiffRouter enables highly concurrent routing and improved global optimality before projecting solutions back onto the discrete fabric. The framework consists of three key stages: (1) Resource-pruned preprocessing, which extracts compact routing variables per net to reduce memory footprint and problem complexity; (2) Differentiable global routing via gradient descent, which formulates routing as a Lagrangian-relaxed optimization that minimizes wirelength while iteratively enforcing connectivity and congestion constraints as relaxed terms; and (3) Postprocessing, which maps the continuous wire distributions to a valid UltraScale routing solution and completes solutions by detailed routing. Experiments on the FPGA 2024 Routing Contest benchmarks show that DiffRouter outperforms state-of-the-art parallel routers, achieving over 5x speedup over RWRoute and 2x over Vivado, and running 18% faster than the state-of-the-art academic FPGA router Potter while delivering competitive critical path wirelength.
☆ Combinatorial Lock for Trojan-Resilient AI Engines via Hash-Assisted Spectral Filtering
Soumita Chatterjee, Sudip Ghosh, Tamal Ghosh, Bhargab B. Bhattacharya, Hafizur Rahaman
Multi-trigger backdoor attacks, in which heterogeneous trigger mechanisms (e.g., patch, blend, warping, and the latent-domain Spy-Watermark) coexist within a single poisoned model, cannot be addressed by existing shortcut-based defenses that assume a single, static attack mechanism. Current multi-trigger remedies (BU+FT, BU+ANP, and BU+ABL) rely on backdoor unlearning, which incurs high training costs and loses effectiveness at low poisoning ratios. This paper proposes Combinatorial Lock, a training-free, reference-calibrated defense that reduces multi-trigger identification to a combinatorial problem over binary activation signatures. This formulation is the key novelty: it yields a multi-trigger defense that is training-free, trigger-oblivious, and directly implementable in hardware, since its decision rules can be evaluated with integer and logic operations. The framework combines anomaly-gated spectral scrubbing, which removes the malicious subspace only from inputs identified as anomalous, with a hash-compressed combinatorial signature verified by an ensemble of detection experts, including a compound-attack-aware expert for multi-trigger inputs. A joint threshold-shrinkage search identifies an operating point ($τ$ at the 97.1st percentile, $γ= 0.78$) that reduces the attack success rate (ASR) to as low as 0.1% while preserving 97.8% clean accuracy. Evaluation on six models (ResNet18, VGG16, MobileNet, AlexNet, ViT-Small, ViT-Base) shows strong generalization: near-100% ASR drops to single digits across poisoning rates of 1-10% and 1-9 simultaneous triggers, with only gradual degradation in clean accuracy. ASIC and FPGA implementations confirm practicality, adding a consistent 19-20% latency overhead across all evaluated architectures and establishing Combinatorial Lock as a hardware-native approach to multi-trigger backdoor mitigation.

☆ The Analytical Investigation of Inductor-Coupled Mixers
Arash Ghasemi
Inductor- and transformer-coupled balanced mixers are usually analyzed with switching approximations, conversion matrices, harmonic balance, or circuit simulation. This paper instead derives their loaded quasistatic equations from the device laws, keeping the transformer ratios, reflected load, bias, and the connections that set the IF common mode. The functions needed to solve these equations are surprisingly varied. A Shockley single-balanced pair is solved by the principal Lambert function $W_0$ under a quantified one-diode-dominance condition. The double-balanced ring has a unique loaded solution that reduces to an exact inverse-hyperbolic-tangent form only for an open IF port. Vacuum diodes and triodes lead to Cardano cubics and structured sextics, whereas depletion JFET/MESFET crossbars need only square roots. Bilateral MOS channels give piecewise quadratics in strong inversion, or a Ferrari quartic when the IF winding floats. In weak inversion they give an exact $W_0$, the real $r$-Lambert function, or the inverse of $\sinh x+ρx$, depending on topology and IF connection. In matched BJT and HBT Gilbert cells, equal emitter resistors turn the hyperbolic-tangent current steering into an exact $r$-Lambert function. Fourier projection brings in Beta/Gamma, modified Bessel, and complete elliptic functions. We checked the equations against 37 LTspice simulations using seven independent Python programs. In the 12 primary comparisons, which implement the assumed device laws exactly, every RMS waveform error was below 0.017% of the analytical RMS value; built-in and vendor device models were examined separately. Two design examples show how the closed forms size a thermionic IF load and a Gilbert-cell tail current without parameter sweeps.

☆ Operating System Support for Accelerators as First-Class Compute Elements in Heterogeneous Systems
Mohammad Shahidzadeh, Lesley Shannon
Heterogeneous systems-on-chip (SoCs) increasingly rely on domain-specific hardware accelerators (HAs) to deliver performance and energy efficiency gains beyond what general-purpose processors can provide. However, operating systems (O/S), such as Linux, assume that processors are the Compute Elements (CEs) and HAs are peripheral devices rather than specialized CEs. As a result, HA execution incurs substantial software (SW) overheads that reduce the effective benefits of hardware (HW) acceleration. This work demonstrates that significant inefficiencies arise from ``O/S peripheral device taxes'' (O/S taxes), including SW stack-accelerator communication, invocation, interrupt handling, and scheduling. We built MEERKAT, a Field-Programmable Gate Array (FPGA)-based framework for configurable multicore systems that include HAs; it allows the user to migrate Linux O/S support from SW to HW and identify, study, and quantify the impact. Here we study four major sources of O/S tax and introduce techniques to mitigate them. These include a virtual-memory-aware (VMA) communication method that enables efficient HA access to application data, a lightweight HA invocation interface, Interrupt Policy Adaptation (IPA) for dynamic polling and interrupt selection, and an HA-aware scheduler that dynamically distributes work across Central Processing Units (CPUs) and multiple HA instances. Experimental results demonstrate up to $260\times$ faster communication and $2$--$6\times$ performance improvements in wall-clock time over conventional schedulers. Furthermore, our study reveals that overall system performance depends not only on HA speed, but also on O/S overheads, resource availability, and scheduling decisions. These findings highlight the importance of treating HAs as first-class computational resources and motivate a more accelerator-centric O/S design for future heterogeneous systems.

♻ ☆ Proba: A High-Performance, Low-Traffic Probabilistic Spatial Memory Streaming Prefetcher
Yinting Huang, Jacky Wong, Sam Ainsworth
Spatial Memory Streaming (SMS) prefetchers exploit recurring spatial access patterns across memory regions to predict future accesses. Despite their effectiveness, existing SMS-based designs suffer from coarse control over aggressiveness and limited adaptability to dynamic program behavior and historical patterns. In this work, we present Proba, a feedback-directed SMS-based prefetcher that integrates probabilistic pattern updates, marginal accuracy decision-making, and pattern accumulation over several trigger features to achieve fine-grained accuracy-coverage trade-offs. When accuracy deteriorates, Proba moderates its aggressiveness to prioritize accurate prefetches; conversely, when accuracy rises, it expands its coverage. Proba achieves higher speedups than any other SMS-based prefetcher while being vastly more efficient in DRAM traffic. This culminates in a large performance gap between Proba and other techniques in multicore and DRAM-constrained scenarios without harming single-threaded throughput.

♻ ☆ SimSkip: StrongARM-Comparator Similarity Sensing with SAR-Skipping-Aware Training for Analog Compute-in-Memory ISCA
Yufei Liu, Junjie Wang, Shuang Liu, Yang Wang, Yang Liu
Analog-to-digital conversion dominates the energy and area of analog compute-in-memory (CIM) accelerators. This paper presents SimSkip, a successive-approximation-register (SAR) skipping scheme that combines a lightweight StrongARM-comparator similarity detector with SAR-skipping-aware training. Programming Languages 3
☆ A Rowhammer attack on Fil-C's software capabilities
Amir Naseredini, Suhas Viswanath, Martin Berger
Rowhammer is a hardware vulnerability in dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) in which repeated accesses to one or more aggressor rows can induce bit-flips in nearby victim rows. This phenomenon violates a core assumption of conventional programming language semantics: that reading from or writing to one memory location does not modify others. Despite the security importance of this phenomenon, there is no widely established formal framework connecting Rowhammer faults with program behaviour. This makes it difficult to reason rigorously about the efficacy of proposed Rowhammer defences and the program-level guarantees they provide. To address this gap, we present a probabilistic small-step operational semantics for an idealised imperative language subject to Rowhammer-style faults. The semantics is high-level in that it abstracts from DRAM internals and semiconductor physics. A general probabilistic fault model parameterises the semantics, representing Rowhammer-style faults by assigning probabilities to bit-flips during read or write operations within a specified victim region. The resulting distributions are propagated through programs using the standard monadic structure of probabilistic computation. As a case study, we formalise physical separation, a well-known defence that places program variables sufficiently far apart in physical memory that an access to one variable cannot disturb another. We prove a distribution-independent semantic collapse theorem: for every finite execution, including prefixes of terminating and non-terminating executions, the protected projection of the probabilistic Rowhammer semantics is the Dirac distribution of the corresponding Rowhammer-free execution. Furthermore, we develop an observation-parametric account of secure information flow.
☆ Can Agentic Code Review Catch Vulnerabilities Before They Are Merged?
Yihan Yang, Chenyuan Yang, Zijie Zhao, Anh Nguyen Hoang, Ziqi Zhang, Weidong Wang, Zhiheng Lyu, Lingming Zhang
LLM agents have been increasingly adopted for security-focused code review, but existing benchmarks mostly derive the reviewed changes from their later fixes instead of the real-world bug-introducing commits. In this paper, we present SecPRBench, the first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating agentic security review on validated real-world code changes. More specifically, we have built an automated agentic pipeline to autonomously harvest, build, and validate massive real-world bug-introducing commits for popular software systems. To ensure precise evaluation, we also adopt LLM-as-a-judge to credit the findings matching ground-truth vulnerabilities. To date, we have collected 265 tasks from nine critical systems, including Firefox, V8, and OpenSSL with millions of lines of code. Across eight frontier models, recall ranges from 32.5% to 78.1%, with Grok 4.6 and GPT-6 sol ranking at the top. Our study also shows that most misses are not simply oversights: in 68% of them, the agent reports on a file that holds the flaw but argues for a different mechanism. Security-focused harnesses also behave differently: OpenAI's Codex Security lowers recall by 8.3 points, mostly by abstaining, while Anthropic's Defending Code harness raises it by 7.7 points, at a higher cost.

♻ ☆ A Calculus of Inheritance
Bo Yang
Just as the $λ$-calculus is a pure functional programming language built from three primitives (abstraction, application, variable), we present inheritance-calculus, a pure declarative programming language built from three primitives (mixin, definition, reference). We introduce multi-target $\mathtt{this}$ to solve the problem that inheritance conflicts with modularity, and thereby unify object-oriented and configuration constructs: modules, classes, objects, methods, fields, and locals become a single mixin abstraction, and inheritance becomes simply set union, the deep merge of configuration languages. Consequently, composition is commutative, idempotent, and associative, structurally eliminating the multiple-inheritance linearization problem; the inheritance relation has substitutability; every path of every program can be inherited and extended; every subtree can be split into parts that, inherited together, behave as the whole; and programs have no side effects and cover every input. We call these properties modularity by construction. The calculus has no functions, yet it macro-expresses the lazy $λ$-calculus and is more macro-expressive than it in Felleisen's sense. Its semantics is first-order, denotational, and evaluated by tabling, even for cyclic inheritance hierarchies. Inheritance-calculus is distilled from MIXINv2, an implementation in which the same code acts as different function colors and ordinary arithmetic agrees with the relational semantics of logic programming.

☆ New Fault-Tolerant $(α,β)$-Spanners
Merav Parter, Elizaveta Popova, Elad Tzalik
We present new constructions of vertex fault-tolerant (VFT) $(α,β)$-spanners with substantially improved size bounds. Given a bound $f$ on the number of vertex faults, an $f$-VFT $(α,β)$-spanner is a subgraph of $G$ that preserves an $(α,β)$-spanner of $G- F$ for every fault set $F \subseteq V$ with $|F| \le f$. Most existing constructions of VFT $(α,β)$-spanners rely on a black-box sampling technique that incurs an exponential blow-up compared to their non-fault-tolerant counterparts. We develop two independent techniques for constructing $f$-VFT $(α,β)$-spanners that improve the dependence on $f$, reducing it to polynomial overhead. For every unweighted graph $G=(V,E)$ on $n$ vertices, stretch parameter $k$, and fault parameter $f$, we show: (i) $f$-VFT $(O_ε(k^ε), O_ε(k))$-spanners with $\tilde{O}(f^{exp(1/ε)}\cdot n^{1+1/k})$ edges for any $ε\in (0,1)$. For any fixed constant $ε$, this introduces a $poly(f)$ overhead compared to the non-faulty constructions of [Ben-Levy and Parter, SODA 2020] and [Chechik and Lifshitz, SODA 2026]. (ii) For any $δ\in (0,1)$, an $f$-VFT $\left(O_δ(\sqrt{k}),O_δ(k) \right)$-spanner of size $\tilde{O}(f^{1+δ} \cdot n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})$. Hence, it incurs a size overhead of only $f^{1+δ}$ compared to the above-mentioned non-faulty analogs. (iii) $f$-VFT $(k,k-1)$ spanner of size $O(k^3 \cdot f \cdot n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})$, nearly matching the lower bound $Ω(f^{1-\frac{1}{k}}n^{1+\frac{1}{k}})$ known for $f$-VFT $(2k-1,0)$ spanners by [Bodwin et al., SODA 2018]. The first result is obtained via a new adaptation of the Baswana--Sen framework to the VFT $(α,β)$ setting. The other two bounds follow from a highly involved analysis of a natural extension of the fault-tolerant greedy spanner.

☆ Random Deletion for Circuits in Regular Matroids
Karthekeyan Chandrasekaran, Chandra Chekuri, Weihao Zhu
We study a simple random deletion algorithm to find short circuits in regular matroids. Given a regular matroid $M$ with $m$ elements, the algorithm randomly deletes each element independently with probability $1-1/m^{Θ(1/g)}$ where $g$ is the size of a minimum circuit, and returns the remaining set $R$ of elements. We establish the following results: 1. With high probability, $R$ contains no circuit and is therefore independent. 2. For every $α$-approximate minimum circuit $C$ where $α\geq 1$, with probability at least $m^{-O(α)}$, the entire circuit $C$ survives in $R$ and moreover, the minimum number of elements that need to be removed to make $R$ independent is $O(α\log(α+2))$. This yields an algorithmic proof that the number of $α$-approximate minimum circuits is $m^{O(α)}$, improving the previous known bound of $m^{O(α^2)}$ by Gurjar and Vishnoi, and resolving a recent conjecture of Khanna, Putterman, and Song. Our results yield two immediate applications. First, we obtain very simple RNC algorithms for computing the shortest circuit, and for enumerating all $α$-approximate circuits, of a regular matroid without computing the Seymour decomposition. Second, we obtain a randomized parallel algorithm to find a basis of a regular matroid under independence-oracle access. It uses polynomially many independence-oracle queries in $O(\log m)$ adaptive rounds and succeeds with high probability. This follows from the framework of Khanna, Putterman, and Song and the improved counting bound, and extends their result from graphic matroids to regular matroids.
