☆ Engineering Lightweight Burrows-Wheeler Transform Construction on the GPU via Prefix-Free Parsing
Florian Kurpicz, Maximilian Paris
The Burrows-Wheeler transform (BWT) is the core of many compressed full-text indexes and most read aligners. The fastest BWT constructions on GPUs need about $20$ bytes of device memory per character, which severely limits the texts that can be processed there. For example, on our GPU with 24 GB device memory, this is only about 1200 MiB. Prefix-free parsing (PFP) can be used to reduce the memory requirements of BWT construction algorithms in main memory. But, it uses hash tables and inverted lists which are not suited for GPUs. We show that PFP-based BWT construction can work with only sorting, scanning, and simple data-parallel procedures. This results in our GPU construction gpupfpbwt that on an NVIDIA RTX 3090, reduces the peak memory on an English text 11.9 bytes per character. There, it can processes 2108 MiB input files, which is 1.8 times larger than what the current state of the art libcubwt can handle. It is faster than or on par with libcubwt on eight of nine repetitive texts but 2.0 to 3.2 times slower on five of six non-repetitive ones, i.e., it provides a trade-off between required device memory and throughput.
☆ A Factor-6 Truthful-in-Expectation Mechanism for Cascade Position Auctions
Qiuyu Chen, Baao Xie
Position auctions require assigning advertisers to positions and determining their display order. Under cascade behavior, earlier clicks prevent users from reaching later placements, coupling the click probabilities of selected advertisements. Existing methods achieve constant-factor welfare approximation and monotone logarithmic-factor approximation separately, leaving open whether both guarantees can be attained simultaneously. The difficulty is that welfare-preserving rounding need not maintain the monotonicity of individual click-through rates required for truthful bidding. We resolve this problem by developing a polynomial-time randomized mechanism that achieves a factor-6 welfare approximation and truthfulness in expectation for arbitrary public advertiser-position click probabilities. The mechanism optimizes a reach-weighted linear relaxation and converts its solution into feasible ordered allocations through matching decomposition, probability-bounded grouping, and calibrated attenuation. This construction compensates for cascade dependence and ensures that each advertiser's expected click-through rate equals exactly one sixth of its fractional click coordinate. The resulting identity transfers the monotonicity of the fractional allocation to the implemented mechanism, enabling exact incentive-compatible payments while preserving the welfare guarantee. The allocation lottery and payments are computable in polynomial bit time, with exact sampling in expected polynomial time.
☆ Approximation algorithms for identical parallel machine scheduling with assignment costs
Wei Li
We study non-preemptive scheduling on identical parallel machines in which assigning job $j$ to machine $M_i$ incurs the cost $c_{ij}=a_iw_j+b_j$. The processing times $p_j$ and the cost weights $w_j$ are independent, and the number of machines is part of the input. For every fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ and every deadline $T$ for which a schedule with makespan at most $T$ exists, we give a polynomial-time algorithm that computes a schedule with makespan at most $(1+\varepsilon)T$ whose total assignment cost does not exceed the minimum under the deadline $T$. Using this algorithm as a subroutine, we obtain a polynomial-time approximation scheme for minimizing the makespan under a total cost budget whose schedules always satisfy the budget; this answers an open question for costs $c_{ij}=l_ic_j$ with $c_j$ independent of $p_j$. For the lexicographic problem, in which the total assignment cost is minimized among all schedules of minimum makespan, we obtain in polynomial time a schedule with makespan at most $1+\varepsilon$ times the minimum and total assignment cost at most that of a lexicographically optimal schedule; this improves the makespan guarantees of earlier algorithms for additive and multiplicative costs. For costs $c_{ij}=s_i+a_iw_j$, where $s_i$ is charged for each job processed on $M_i$, the same guarantees are obtained in quasi-polynomial time.
♻ ☆ On the Number of Circuits in Regular Matroids (with Connections to Lattices and Codes)
Rohit Gurjar, Nisheeth K. Vishnoi
We show that for any regular matroid on $m$ elements and any $α\geq 1$, the number of $α$-minimum circuits, or circuits whose size is at most an $α$-multiple of the minimum size of a circuit in the matroid is bounded by $m^{O(α^2)}$. ♻ ☆ List coloring uncrowded hypergraphs at the shattering threshold
Abhishek Dhawan, Abhishek Methuku, Minh-Quan Vo
Improving an earlier bound of Frieze and Mubayi, Iliopoulos showed that any $k$-uniform uncrowded hypergraph of maximum degree $Δ$ has list chromatic number at most $(1+o(1))(k-1)\Big(\fracΔ{\logΔ}\Big)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}$. Determining the optimal leading constant in this bound remains a major open problem. A natural target for the constant, suggested by the coupon-collector heuristic, is $(k-1)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}$, which also arises independently as the shattering threshold for coloring and finding large independent sets in sparse random hypergraphs. We show that uncrowded hypergraphs attain this threshold: for $k \ge 2$, every $k$-uniform uncrowded hypergraph $\mathcal{H}$ of maximum degree at most $Δ$ satisfies $$χ_\ell(\mathcal{H})\le(1+o(1))\Big((k-1)\fracΔ{\logΔ}\Big)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}.$$ This matches the coupon-collector heuristic, and establishes a 2019 conjecture of Molloy for uncrowded hypergraphs. As a consequence, we show that every $k$-uniform linear hypergraph of maximum degree at most $Δ$ has chromatic number at most $c_k\Big(\fracΔ{\log Δ}\Big)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}$ with $c_k=1-o_k(1)$, resolving, in a stronger form, a recent conjecture of Verstraëte and Wilson on the independence number of linear hypergraphs. Our techniques yield the first palette sparsification result for hypergraph coloring using $o(Δ^{\frac{1}{k-1}})$ colors per vertex. Specifically, for an uncrowded $k$-graph $\mathcal{H}$ and $q=Θ\Big({\Big(\fracΔ{γ\logΔ}\Big)^{\frac{1}{k-1}}}\Big)$, sampling $Θ(Δ^{\fracγ{k-1}})$ colors per vertex suffices to obtain a proper $q$-coloring of $\mathcal{H}$ w.h.p. As an algorithmic consequence, we obtain a single-pass streaming algorithm using $O\big(n^{1+o(1)}\big)$ space for uncrowded $k$-graphs, and, via hypergraph partitioning, for linear hypergraphs as well.

☆ Explicit Frictional Contact Modeling in Neural Garment Simulation WACV 2027
Thanh-Nguyen Truong, Vu-Hoang Tran, Ching-Chun Huang
Graph neural networks (GNNs) simulate garments efficiently through physics-based self-supervision, but leave body-garment friction implicit or inside training energies, with no friction coefficient $μ$ to set at inference; retraining the tested GNNs with a coefficient-specific friction term barely responds to $μ$. Optimization-based solvers decide each contact's stick-slip state from the reaction that governs it, but only by re-optimizing the garment state at every step; a friction term in a one-pass training objective acts as a fixed-weight penalty on sliding, and the trained weights apply no per-contact Coulomb condition at inference. We therefore resolve friction explicitly after prediction: a Modular Contact Operator, attached to a frozen predictor without retraining or an iterative solve, estimates the normal load, applies a closed-form Coulomb correction along a path across body triangles, and propagates the induced motion through the cloth as a linearized membrane wave. On HOOD and ContourCraft, it raises sticking and lowers slip monotonically in $μ$, tracking a reference solver's response while coefficient-specific retraining stays flat, with low penetration and reference-consistent stretching at an eighth of the solver's runtime.

☆ Walk on Multiscale Media: Compiling Editable and Composable Transport Medium Prefabs for Monte Carlo PDE Solvers
Yunfeng Liao, Xiucheng Li
Monte Carlo PDE solvers enable mesh-free pointwise queries, but repeatedly traversing heterogeneous media is costly, particularly in scenes with reusable or dynamically changing materials. We introduce Walk on Multiscale Media (WoMM), which compiles fine-scale transport into reusable, editable and composable transport prefabs. ♻ ☆ MS-COOT: Comparing Morse-Smale Complexes with Co-Optimal Transport
Guangyu Meng, Mingzhe Li, Erin Wolf Chambers
Understanding and comparing structures in scalar fields is a central challenge in scientific visualization, with applications ranging from feature analysis to temporal and structural comparison. The Morse-Smale (MS) complex provides a natural representation by decomposing a scalar field into regions induced by gradient flow. However, existing approaches typically rely on graph-based representations, matching critical points without explicitly establishing correspondences between regions. In this work, we represent the MS complex as a hypergraph, where critical points form nodes and regions define hyperedges. We introduce MS-COOT, a co-optimal transport distance that jointly computes correspondences between critical points and regions. This formulation enables explicit region-to-region matching within a distance-based framework, allowing identification of region-level events such as splitting and merging. ♻ ☆ uGDS: A User-Space Development Kit for High-Performance GPU Direct Storage
Jing Wei, Guanyi Chen, Qi Chen, Chuyu Zhang, Zheng Zhou, Chutian Wang, Jian Zhang
Data-intensive GPU applications increasingly rely on NVMe SSDs to extend GPU memory. NVIDIA GPU Direct Storage (GDS) enables direct SSD-GPU transfers via PCIe peer-to-peer DMA, eliminating host staging. However, GDS retains three key limitations: (1) its control path traverses the kernel for every I/O request, introducing software overhead; (2) its stream-ordered path imposes strict total ordering even when workloads only require batch-level ordering; and (3) its closed-source user-space engine hinders diagnosis and custom scheduling. We present uGDS, https://github.com/ScaleX-IO/uGDS, an open-source user-space GPU Direct Storage library built on four principles: user-space control, unified cross-vendor abstraction, workload-aware scheduling, and end-to-end transparency. The host CPU manages the entire NVMe command lifecycle in user space, while payloads move via PCIe P2P DMA without consuming GPU compute. A minimal kernel module is used only for one-time privileged setup. uGDS partitions hardware queues between synchronous and batch traffic, enforces DMA-safe buffer lifetimes, and provides cuFile-compatible synchronous, batched, and stream-ordered APIs with polling or interrupt completion on both NVIDIA CUDA and AMD HIP. Evaluated on an A100 with a Samsung 990 PRO, uGDS achieves up to 12.12x GDS's batch-read bandwidth and 30.69x its batch-write bandwidth (8- and 4-KiB requests, batch size 128), and reduces 4-KiB synchronous write latency by 84.1% with two threads. Its stream-ordered path sustains 5.49 GiB/s on a single stream, where GDS retains only 40.8% of its synchronous bandwidth. In LMCache, uGDS reduces median time-to-first-token (TTFT) by up to 62.7% and raises serving goodput by 2.63x. On AMD, uGDS achieves up to 1.19x hipFile's synchronous-write bandwidth.

☆ Embodied Emissions of 2.5D and 3D Scaling in Chipletized GPUs for Low-Carbon AI
Hannah Watson, Arjun Singh, Jennifer Stephenson, Job Soethoudt, Debjyoti Bhattacharjee, James Myers

☆ Toward Open ISA Supercomputing: A Comparative Study of RISC-V, Arm, and x86 Architectures on HPC Workloads
Emanuele Venieri, Giacomo Madella, Gabriele Ceccolini, Simone Manoni, Federico Ficarelli, Filippo Barbari, Jenny Lynn Almerol, Elisabetta Boella, Nitin Shukla, Simone Bnà, Luca Benini, Andrea bartolini
RISC-V is emerging as a candidate architecture for high-performance computing (HPC), but its competitiveness against established x86 and Arm processors remains difficult to assess because existing studies use different platforms, workloads, and methodologies, and rarely consider energy efficiency. Our work provides an evaluation of contemporary HPC RISC-V, Arm, and x86 processors using a common software stack and methodology. We characterize the Sophgo SG2044 and compare it with the NVIDIA Grace CPU Superchip and Intel Xeon Platinum 8480+ across four HPC benchmarks (STREAM, HPL, HPL-MxP, and HPCG) and four representative applications (iPIC3D, PLUTO, OpenFOAM, and llama.cpp). The SG2044 reaches 8.02 GFLOP/s of FP64 performance and 7.8 GB/s of memory bandwidth per core, increasing to 294 GFLOP/s and 107 GB/s at 64 cores. L2-cache-aware process placement enables near-linear scaling up to 16 cores, beyond which memory-bandwidth saturation limits further gains. Across generations of RISC-V hardware, in the context of the Monte Cimone (MC) project, the SG2044-based MCv3 doubles both single-core performance and peak memory bandwidth relative to MCv2, while improving memory bandwidth, node-level FP64 performance, and energy efficiency by approximately 100x, 158x, and 10x, over MCv1. Against the Arm and x86 systems, the SG2044 shows 8-15x less absolute performance, reflecting its narrower vector resources and less advanced technology node, but delivers competitive scaling and energy efficiency on several workloads. Its most notable result is LLM decode, where 64-core execution provides the highest relative scaling and energy efficiency among the evaluated platforms while exceeding Intel in throughput. Our results show that RISC-V processors are closing the gap with the competition.

☆ Automated Multi-Line Bug Repair Using Model Checker Aided LLMs: A Case Study on RISC-V Processors
Debleena Das, Chandan Kumar Jha, Muhammad Hassan, Rolf Drechsler, Ansuman Banerjee, Subhra K. Das, Amlan Chakrabarti
Functional verification of Register-Transfer Level(RTL) designs has become a critical bottleneck in modern semiconductor development, often consuming the majority of project resources. Debugging, in particular, remains an especially time-consuming task, requiring careful analysis of simulation traces or counterexamples. Recent work has explored applying Large Language Models (LLMs) to RTL debug and repair with great promise. However, the existing approaches typically are for single-line bugs, operate in open-loop, rely heavily on prompts or retrieval, lack scalability, and often produce incorrect fixes. In this paper, we propose a novel automated RTL bug repair methodology that combines LLMs, assertions, and a model checker to provide scalable, trustworthy semantic grounding and formal guarantees. Concretely, the methodology leverages failing assertions to guide the LLM in generating patches for multi-line bugs. Each candidate patch is formally verified using a model checker (JasperGold), ensuring only valid repairs are accepted. We evaluate the efficacy and scalability of our methodology on the BRISC-V RISC-V core using mutation testing, introducing several mutation types, and demonstrate that our methodology can repair up to 98% of single-line RTL bugs and 93% of the multi-line bugs. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to show automated, LLM-assisted RTL multi-line bug repair results on a complex RISC-V processor design in a scalable manner.
☆ PARIS: Parallel Arithmetic Reduction and Integrated Selection for MSDF Inner Products
Malik Zohaib Nisar, Ic-Pyo Hong, Dorit Merhof, Muhammad Usman
Existing most-significant-digit-first (MSDF) accelerators serialize input-term processing, causing inner-product latency to increase with fan-in. This paper presents PARIS, an MSDF architecture that eliminates input-term serialization through parallel bit-plane reduction and shared carry-save residual selection. For $B$-bit operands, PARIS processes $B^2$ bit-position pairs, independent of fan-in within the supported tile, while generating exact MSDF outputs. Synthesized in 45\,nm at 100\,MHz, the 784-lane INT8 core occupies 0.008701\,mm$^2$ and completes an inner product in 88 cycles. Evaluation on two MNIST multilayer perceptrons demonstrates arithmetic-workload speedups of 240.44$\times$ and 180.47$\times$ over a matched reused-MSDF implementation, reducing arithmetic energy by 98.89\% and 98.58\%, respectively. A synthesized 16-engine accelerator occupies 0.90273\,mm$^2$ and meets the 100\,MHz timing constraint. Assuming immediate weight availability, it delivers a projected throughput of 24.33\,GOPS and an estimated energy efficiency of 1,581.14\,GOPS/W at unchanged mapped power. Bias-aware ReLU detection further reduces modeled arithmetic cycles by 5.52\% and 4.86\%. A separate 256-engine RTL implementation completes Model A in 259 cycles, demonstrating output-parallel scalability.
☆ TranSAGE: Automating Spatial Accelerator Generation for Large-Scale Transformer Models ASPLOS 2027
Chi Zhang, Yi Sun, Weichuang Zhang, Quan Chen, Jieru Zhao
Large-scale Transformers have achieved remarkable success across NLP, computer vision, and robotics, but their growing computational and memory demands challenge efficient inference. Spatial accelerators offer advantages in on-chip bandwidth and energy efficiency, yet designing them for large models remains costly and time-consuming. Existing accelerator generators typically focus on single-operation support, overlooking the benefits of multi-operation pipelining, which limits throughput and energy efficiency. We propose TranSAGE, a compiler-based framework that jointly optimizes scheduling for large-scale Transformers under multi-operation pipelining and generates corresponding accelerator implementations. Our approach integrates a design space exploration (DSE) engine with multi-operation dataflow optimization and a dynamic programming-based graph partitioning algorithm to efficiently identify pipeline-friendly operation clusters and their optimal dataflow parameters. We further design spatial architecture primitives and lowering passes to automatically generate efficient RTL implementations. Evaluations on four representative LLMs demonstrate that TranSAGE achieves up to 2.41x higher throughput and 13.04x higher energy efficiency than commercial GPU/NPU baselines. Compared with state-of-the-art accelerator generators, TranSAGE achieves up to 11.33x higher throughput and 6.07x higher energy efficiency. Our code is available at https://github.com/sjtu-zhao-lab/TranSAGE.

☆ A Resource-Efficient and Precision-Scalable FPGA Accelerator for Spiking Neural Networks Using Minifloat Arithmetic
Ali Mehrabi, Philip C. Jose
Spiking neural networks (SNNs) are well suited to event-driven edge intelligence, but the arithmetic complexity, memory footprint, and power consumption associated with conventional floating-point implementations can limit their deployment on resource-constrained hardware. This paper presents a precision-scalable and resource-efficient SNN hardware accelerator architecture based on parameterized minifloat arithmetic. The proposed architecture implements recurrent leaky integrate-and-fire (LIF) neurons and leaky-integrator (LI) readout neurons using a unified minifloat datapath whose word length and mantissa width can be configured at synthesis time. A valid-driven arithmetic subsystem employs pipelined minifloat multiplication and addition, while time-multiplexed processing enables arithmetic resources to be shared across synaptic accumulation, neuron-state updates, recurrent connections, and output processing. This organization reduces replicated arithmetic hardware and provides a scalable design approach for different SNN sizes and precision requirements. The architecture supports E8M23, E5M10, E5M6, and E4M3 formats within the same RTL framework, allowing precision, memory capacity, timing, and power to be traded without modifying the underlying neuron architecture. FPGA implementation results demonstrate that reducing minifloat precision substantially decreases arithmetic-resource utilization and estimated dynamic power, while also reducing the theoretical storage requirement of synaptic parameters. A recurrent event-based classification network is used as a hardware validation case, demonstrating that substantial precision reduction can be achieved while retaining competitive inference accuracy. The results establish parameterized minifloat computation as an effective basis for scalable, low-resource, and energy-efficient FPGA SNN accelerators.
☆ How AI Load Swings Reduce Datacenter Power Conversion Efficiency
Heetarth Joshi, Ali Alenezi, Fang Chen Lin, Prasad Enjeti, Xiaoyang Wang, Xin Chen
AI training and inference workloads make GPU power swing repeatedly between compute and communication phases. As a result, the cascaded converters in a datacenter rack keep moving between operating points with different efficiencies. This paper uses an energy based, cycle averaged method to quantify that effect along a 240 V AC to 400 V DC to 51 V DC rack power path, using measured efficiency curves of commercially available GaN based PFC and LLC converters. For a 1 Hz, 50% duty-cycle load swinging between 5 kW and 510 W, conversion efficiency falls to 95.97%, compared with 96.82% at the equivalent constant average load, a loss of 0.88%. When 51/48V to 1V point of load conversion to the chip rail is included, it is shown that the loss grows to 1.66% (89.45% vs. 90.96%). These results show that efficiency rated at average load overstates real world performance, and that the penalty grows with each load dependent conversion stage between the grid and the GPU.
☆ Embodied Emissions of 2.5D and 3D Scaling in Chipletized GPUs for Low-Carbon AI
Hannah Watson, Arjun Singh, Jennifer Stephenson, Job Soethoudt, Jonas Svedas, Debjyoti Bhattacharjee, James Myers
GPU compute resources are scaling to support growing AI workloads, leading to further rises in the climate change impact of hyperscale data centers that rely on high-performance GPUs. To model how packaging decisions impact the carbon footprint we have estimated the embodied emissions (EE) of a chiplet-based GPU that has been scaled in 2.5D and 3D using the imec.netzero modelling platform. We doubled the number of compute units and high bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity in the 2.5D and 3D scaled systems and found that 3D scaling results in an 89% increase in EE, as compared to an increase of 141% for 2.5D scaling, positioning 3D scaling as an attractive solution to limit the embodied emissions due to GPU scaling.
♻ ☆ MERA: A Mid-Circuit-Measurement Error-Aware Compilation Framework for Quantum Circuits
Ming Zhong, Zhemin Zhang, Xiangyu Ren, Chenghong Zhu, Siyuan Niu, Zhiding Liang
Mid-circuit measurement (MCM) enables qubit reuse and dynamic control in quantum processors, underpinning resource-efficient algorithms and quantum error correction. However, MCM introduces non-negligible errors, including measurement-induced crosstalk, idling-qubit decoherence, and reset infidelity. Additionally, MCM error rates vary across qubits within the same processor, with some exhibiting substantially higher errors. Existing compilers, including Qiskit-compiler and QR-Map, ignore MCM error variations, leading to fidelity loss in circuits with frequent MCM operations. In this paper, we present MERA, an MCM-error-aware quantum compilation framework that integrates MCM error modeling into the layout, routing, and scheduling stages. We first use the lightweight profiling method to capture a per-qubit MCM error distribution that remains stable for over 24 hours. The distribution guides MERA in applying MCM-error-aware layout and routing, while MERA further incorporates context-aware dynamic decoupling to improve fidelity. In evaluation, MERA delivers 24.94% -- 80.48% average fidelity gains over the Qiskit-compiler (optimization level 3) across different groups in selected benchmarks without additional SWAP overhead. It also boosts the fidelity of QR-Map-generated circuits by 29.26% on average (up to 122.58%), demonstrating its effectiveness for circuits with frequent MCM operations.

♻ ☆ HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference
Junhyeok Park, Jungeun Yoon, Kyungmin Kim, Hunsoon Lee, Shinyoung Ahn, Kangho Kim
Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. As a result, AI accelerators achieve varying levels of efficiency across the two phases. GPUs are well suited to prefill, but are often underutilized during decode. By contrast, NPUs can utilize their compute resources more effectively during decode. Motivated by this observation, we explore a heterogeneous architecture that integrates GPUs and NPUs, mapping prefill to GPUs and decode to NPUs to achieve high performance and efficiency. However, realizing such heterogeneous integration is challenging because LLM phase disaggregation requires KV cache transfer from prefill to decode (that is, from GPUs to NPUs), which is complicated by differences in inter-device communication mechanisms and KV cache tensor layouts. To address this, we propose HAWK, a transfer mechanism that enables rapid KV cache transfer across heterogeneous devices. HAWK establishes a remote direct memory access (RDMA) path for direct cross-device transfers while handling inter-device tensor layout discrepancies as part of the transfer process. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. We implement and evaluate HAWK on a system built with commercially available GPUs and NPUs, demonstrating performance within 7.88% of a GPU-only system under the same SLO constraints while achieving up to 1.26$\times$ higher power efficiency and 1.72$\times$ higher cost efficiency.

☆ On the Expressive Power of Hook-Indexed State Comprehending State in the React UI Library
Toby Ueno, Ezra Cooper, Simon Fowler, Sam Lindley
React is currently the most popular JavaScript library for front-end web development. It endows UI components with state via the useState construct, through a mechanism that we name hook-indexed state. However, React's implementation of hook-indexed state is both fragile and poorly understood. Programmers frequently experience unexpected behavior, and the benefits of programming in this paradigm are wholly unclear. We define Lambda-React-Hook, a core calculus that captures the essence of hook-indexed state, and give it a type system to statically rule out unexpected behavior. We then translate Lambda-React-Hook into a calculus Lambda-React-Ref based on ML-style references, and prove that this translation preserves typability and semantics, thus showing that hook-indexed state is expressible in terms of standard references. The translation is local and admits a compositional definition, but involves significant code duplication. As well as illuminating the essence of state in React, our observations can inform the design of future reactive programming systems.
☆ GLP Networking API Specification
Dan Bachar, Ehud Shapiro
Multiagent deterministic GLP (madGLP) runs Grassroots Logic Programs (GLP) as agents that communicate solely by asynchronous message passing, and assumes fair delivery: every message sent is eventually delivered. Each agent runs on a person's smartphone, named by the person's public key. A grassroots platform depends on no global resource but the network, so agents must reach one another over Bluetooth Low Energy, on a local network, and across the Internet from behind NAT, via rendezvous servers anyone can run. What madGLP requires of such a network, and what the network requires of the platform, has not been specified. We specify the networking layer beneath madGLP. Anything that can be done in GLP is done in GLP: the layer provides only what GLP cannot reach - the network stack, the location service, application attestation, and cryptographic primitives. Three transport underlays share one interface. Over PAN and LAN, an agent advertises a rotating recognition token derived from its key and time slot, so a peer who knows the key recognises it before contact and nobody else can track it; whether it answers unrecognised contacts is a per-underlay trust level set by GLP. Over WAN, a rendezvous server is a static-address host running the headless layer and a GLP rendezvous agent that coordinates hole punching, relays no payload, and belongs to no social graph. On every underlay, peers establish a mutually authenticated Noise session from Ed25519 keys, then attest, bound to the handshake hash, that each runs the application published under its developer's key, verified against Apple's and Google's roots with no third party contacted. One message protocol, over UDP datagrams and GATT writes, provides the fair, unordered, atomic and authenticated delivery madGLP assumes.
☆ On the Expressiveness of Asynchronous Processes with Nested Session Types
Sjors Wortelboer, Dan Frumin, Jorge A. Pérez
Session types are a type-based approach to correct message-passing programs, where types specify protocols for channels and type checking guarantees communication correctness. Traditional session types express regular protocols only, which leaves out many protocols found in practice. Aiming at overcoming this limitation, Nested Session Types (NSTs) can specify nested recursive protocols. NSTs are known to be characterised by one-counter machines and deterministic context-free languages. In this paper, we study the expressiveness of ASP (Asynchronous Session Processes), the process model associated with NSTs as introduced by Das et al. We prove that AST is Turing complete by giving a correct encoding of two-counter machines into typed processes. Our encoding uses a non-trivial state-aware representation of counters and instructions that is neatly governed by NSTs.

☆ Are LLM-Generated Formal Specifications Faithful to Their Requirements? A Study on Bimodal Formal Specification Generation
Wenhan Wang, Shiming Chen, Zeyu Sun, Shenghua Feng
Formal specification generation is a critical step towards formal verification and correctness assurance of software. Recently, large language models have been widely adopted in automatic generation of formal specifications. However, existing LLM-based specification generation approaches often assume that the input program is correct, which deviates from the real purpose of formal verification---to figure out whether the program meets its requirements when its correctness is unknown. Therefore, we conduct an empirical study of LLM-based specification generation under the bimodal setting: given both the natural language requirement and its corresponding code implementation, whose correctness is unknown. We construct a benchmark of 1,403 C programs and evaluate ACSL specification generation with Frama-C verification. Our study reaches several interesting findings: first, when the code implementation is correct, providing the natural language requirement does not further improve the pass rate of generated specifications. Second, when the code implementation is incorrect, LLM-based specification generation may erroneously pass verification by producing weak or implementation-misled specifications, and this cannot be fully alleviated by advanced prompting approaches. Compared with the non-formal HoarePrompt approach, LLM-generated formal specifications are less effective in predicting requirement-code consistency. Our study takes a first look at applying LLM-based formal specification generation to verifying program correctness against requirements, and points to requirement-faithful specification generation as a new direction for future LLM-based formal specification research.
☆ Responsibility Boundaries for Agentic Systems: A Comparative Evaluation of Probabilistic Agentic Programming
Khadim Lo, Soukaina Alaoui Ismaili, Jean-Sébastien Dessureault
Language-model agents combine reasoning, tools, memory, delegation, and external effects. Authority is often distributed across prompts, host code, configuration, and logs. We compare PAP with programming paradigms, agent-oriented and BDI systems, workflow and policy languages, and LLM tool-use frameworks along seven dimensions. A mediation kernel formalizes checks before tool execution, operation-bound approval, resource limits, and evidence. Inductive proofs establish conditional safety for arbitrary model responses, assuming correct guards and complete mediation. A backward simulation matches graph executions to source executions in a defined sequential Touba subset. Its human checkpoints release program continuations; operation-bound approval remains a separate kernel contract. Finite-state exploration checks the model. Shared English instructions and exact checks of stored outcomes yield 1,225 assessed observations. Native Touba completes all 35 observations on six models and 30 on the remaining model; structured Touba ranges from 21 to 35. Native matches the highest observed count for each model, including ties with the frameworks. All paired case intervals include zero. These observed counts describe the evaluated tasks; the runtime authority boundary remains fixed. The results connect responsibility contracts, execution rules, and evidence.

☆ Acyclic 2-edge-coloured graphs: polynomial-time linkages and colouring hardness
Jørgen Bang-Jensen, Stéphane Bessy, Amadeus Reinald
A path or cycle in a $2$-edge-coloured graph is alternating if consecutive edges have different colours. These objects are natural analogues of paths and cycles in graphs, and even more so in digraphs. A well-studied class is that of acyclic $2$-edge-coloured graphs, that is, those forbidding alternating cycles. In particular, (acyclic) $2$-edge-coloured graphs generalize directed (acyclic) graphs (DAGs), but the complexity of problems on DAGs is much better understood than that of the corresponding problems on acyclic $2$-edge-coloured graphs. We investigate the complexity of two such problems, whose directed counterparts are well-known. Our first result is an $n^{O(k)}$-time algorithm for the alternating $k$-linkage problem on acyclic $2$-edge-coloured graphs. This problem asks for $k$ vertex-disjoint alternating paths joining $k$ prescribed pairs of vertices. This generalizes the directed $k$-linkage algorithm for DAGs. For every fixed $k$, this also yields a polynomial-time algorithm for finding $k$ internally vertex-disjoint alternating $(s,t)$-paths, solving an open problem by Abouelaoualim et al. from 2008. We then consider the alternating chromatic number of $2$-edge-coloured graphs, defined as the minimum number of parts in a vertex partition such that each part induces an acyclic subgraph. This notion is analogous to the dichromatic number of digraphs, where it is known that deciding dichromatic number $2$ is NP-complete even on tournaments. Our second result is that deciding alternating chromatic number $2$ is NP-complete on complete $2$-edge-coloured graphs. Equivalently, deciding whether a graph can be partitioned into two induced threshold graphs is NP-complete.

☆ Learning Positive Linear Combinations of ReLUs in Fully Polynomial Time
Ilias Diakonikolas, Jelena Diakonikolas, Puqian Wang, Nikos Zarifis
We study the fundamental problem of PAC learning positive linear combinations of $k$ arbitrary ReLU activations on $\mathbb{R}^d$ with respect to the Gaussian distribution, in $L_2$ loss. We give the first fully-polynomial time algorithm for this learning task. Specifically, our algorithm draws $\mathrm{poly}(d, k, 1/ε)$ samples, runs in $\mathrm{poly}(d, k, 1/ε)$ time, and outputs a hypothesis that is $ε$-close to the target concept with high probability. All prior algorithms for this problem require super-polynomial time in $k$ or $1/ε$, or leverage strong well-conditioned assumptions on the weight matrix. On a technical level, our approach develops a novel boosting procedure of a ``weak'' learner that recovers the hidden weight vectors to constant error, in a list-decodable manner. To develop this ``weak'' learning procedure, we leverage implicit tensor computation techniques to construct a low-degree filtering procedure based on Hermite tensors of the target function. This procedure amplifies separation among (clusters of) weight vectors to learn each of them to constant error. We then argue that the same procedure can be used for improving the estimation of hidden weight vectors, using gradient descent-like updates on the Euclidean sphere. A crucial ingredient of our algorithm is a pruning approach that ensures only relevant vectors that approximate the hidden weight vectors at target accuracy are maintained by the algorithm.
☆ Compressed Boolean Matrix Multiplication using Bicliques
Nicolás Araya, José Zagal, Cecilia Hernández, Gonzalo Navarro
Boolean matrix multiplication is a fundamental operation in many graph-based applications, including web and social network analysis, information retrieval, and graph query evaluation. However, conventional sparse representations such as CSR}/CSC still incur significant space and time costs on large graphs. In this work, we introduce a method for multiplying Boolean matrices based on a biclique-based compressed representation that decomposes them into dense bipartite subgraphs, thereby capturing large sets of implicit edges. We first improve the iterative biclique extraction algorithm of Hernández and Navarro by introducing percentile-based parameter adaptation that dynamically adjusts its parameters according to the degree distribution, enhancing both extraction efficiency and compression ratio. We then extend the Schoor multiplication algorithm to operate directly on biclique-encoded matrices, thereby enabling a compositional form of multiplication where the output can also be represented as a collection of bicliques. Our experimental results on large real-world web and social graphs show that our approach achieves substantial reductions in space and computation time with respect to CSR/CSC-based representations. For example, using biclique extraction just as a tool to speed up multiplication (but retaining the CSR/CSC format) yields up to 34-fold speedups. Further, using the biclique-based representation for input and output yields up to a 10-fold reduction in space and up to a 290-fold speedup for matrix multiplication. These results demonstrate that our representation based on bicliques is both space- and time-effective to support an algebra of Boolean matrices.

☆ Low-Crossing Spanning Paths via Quasi-Uniform Sampling and their Applications
Mónika Csikós, Nabil H Mustafa
Given a set system (X, S), we consider the problems of constructing perfect matchings and spanning paths (also known as Welzl orders) of X with low crossing numbers with respect to S. Matchings with low crossing numbers were originally introduced in the late 1980s in the seminal works of Welzl [29, 30] and Chazelle-Welzl [7], who showed that any set system (X, S) with bounded dual VC-dimension d has a matching with crossing number O |X| 1-1/d , which is optimal. Low-crossing matchings and spanning paths have found several applications outside of their initial realm of computational geometry, including algorithmic graph theory, model checking, and game theory. In many of these applications, |S| is near-linear in |X|, which motivated the search for an improved construction for this regime. However, the proposed new algorithms provided only average-case bounds or sub-optimal crossing number guarantees for d $\ge$ 2 [5, 12]. We propose a new quasi-uniform sampling based framework. This allows us to obtain a simple algorithm with expected running time of __ |X| 2 + |S| $\bullet$ |X| , which improves upon the previous-best Our result has several immediate consequences, including new faster algorithms for constructing low-crossing spanning paths for set systems induced by balls, as well as for neighborhood set systems of graphs. Moreover, we get the first quadratic-time algorithm for combinatorial discrepancy, with nearly optimal discrepancy (up to a log factor), for bounded dual VC-dimension set systems. Beyond these applications, we believe that our idea could be applied to other algorithms that use the multiplicative weight update method.
☆ Proximity Theorem and FPT Algorithm for Minimum Group-labeled Matroid Bases
Siyue Liu, Taihei Oki, Chao Xu
Let $M$ be a matroid, let $\ell:E(M)\to G$ be a labeling of its elements by an abelian group, and let $F\subseteq G$ be finite. A base is $F$-avoiding if the label sum of its elements is not in $F$. We prove that, for arbitrary costs, a min-cost $F$-avoiding base can be obtained from any min-cost base using at most $|F|$ exchanges. This resolves the weighted proximity conjecture by Hörsch, Imolay, Mizutani, Oki, and Schwarcz. Our main technical tool is to replace the assumption of Schrijver--Seymour Conjecture in Liu and Xu's earlier work with Kemperman--Scherk Theorem. The proximity theorem combined with approaches in earlier work gives an FPT algorithm that finds a min-cost $F$-avoiding base parameterized by $|G|$. We additionally give an FPT algorithm parameterized by $|F|$, answering a question of Hörsch et al.
☆ Structure and Exact Certification of Nonnegative Tensor Decompositions
Haoming Wang, Ming Yuan
We study the structure and exact certification of nonnegative tensor decompositions supplied through rational factor matrices. Every failure of minimality or essential uniqueness contains an irreducible positive exchange between $p$ supplied terms and $q$ competing terms. A dimension count based on the Lovitz--Petrov splitting theorem bounds the effective order of such an exchange, the sum over the modes of the joint factor-span dimension minus one, by $p+q-2$, and this bound is attained at every supplied length $R\ge2$. After eliminating ambient-row variables by the Farkas lemma, the resulting semialgebraic systems have fewer than $2R^2$ real variables. For input bit length $L$, this gives a deterministic $G(R)L^{O(R^2)}$-time algorithm that computes the nonnegative rank and an exact minimum-length decomposition, decides minimality and essential uniqueness, and returns exact witnesses when either property fails. The exponent is independent of the tensor order and the mode dimensions. For Lebesgue moment tensors, positive quadrature determines the nonnegative rank and the uniqueness behavior, and some rational length-$R$ inputs force a common field of degree at least $\lfloor R/2\rfloor!$ for any exact minimum-length output. As an illustration, an implementation resolves all 114 instances left inconclusive by earlier positive-scattering criteria, of which 95 are minimal and essentially unique and 19 are minimal but nonunique.
☆ Selection under partial information
Benjamin Aram Berendsohn
The selection problem asks to find the $k$-th smallest element in a given set $X$. Its complexity is well-known to be $Θ(|X|)$ for every fixed $k$. We consider the poset selection problem where a partial order $P$ on $X$ is given, and the underlying order of $X$ is guaranteed to be a linear extension of $P$. This generalizes several variants of the selection problem studied before. Our main result is an algorithm that performs $O( k + \operatorname{OPT}(P,k) )$ comparisons, where $\operatorname{OPT}(P,k)$ is the minimum number of comparisons required for the input $(P,k)$. If $P$ is a rooted tree, we obtain an $O( \operatorname{OPT} \log (k / \operatorname{OPT}) )$ algorithm, which can be further improved to $O( \operatorname{OPT} \log \log (k/\operatorname{OPT}) )$ or $O( \operatorname{OPT} \log \operatorname{OPT})$ under mild assumptions about $P$. Our algorithms are based on the work of Kaplan, Kozma, Zamir, and Zwick [SOSA 2019], who used Chazelle's soft heaps [J. ACM 2000] to simplify several older results. We introduce a new soft heap variant, the weighted soft heap, which allows greater control over which elements get corrupted.
☆ Deterministic Incremental Triconnectivity in $O(\log^2 n)$ Worst-Case Time
Niels Inselmann Christiansen, Eva Rotenberg
We consider the problem of maintaining the triconnectivity in some biconnected graph G in the incremental dynamic setting. We consider updates in the form of edge insertions and edge subdivisions, and triconnectivity queries, i.e. queries of the form "are there at least three vertex disjoint paths between a and b?". All algorithms presented in this paper are deterministic. All queries and updates are answered in worst-case $O(\log n)$ time. By combining this result for biconnected graphs with a result of Holm and Rotenberg [SODA'20] we get an algorithm for general graphs with a worst-case running time of $O(\log^2 n)$ per operation. ☆ A Linear Lower Bound for Convex Body Chasing in Every $\ell_p$ Norm
Nicolas Christianson
We establish an $Ω(d)$ lower bound on the competitive ratio of convex body chasing in $\mathbb{R}^d$ with $\ell_p$ movement costs for any $p \in [1, \infty)$. Previously, the best known lower bound for general $p$ was $Ω(\max\{\sqrt{d}, d^{1-1/p}\})$, established by Bubeck et al. (2019); a linear lower bound was known only for $p = \infty$, realized by the standard construction of chasing faces of a hypercube. Our lower bound strictly improves on the prior bound for every fixed $p \in [1, \infty)$ and shows that the functional Steiner point algorithm of Sellke (2023) - which has competitive ratio $O(d)$ - is asymptotically optimal for every $\ell_p$ norm. Our lower bound is based on the recursive construction of Bubeck, Coester, and Rabani (2023) for metrical service systems, which gives a lower bound of $Ω(r^2)$ for requests of size $O(r)$ in that setting. We show that requests in their construction, each comprising a set of discrete points in a graph metric, can be mapped to the vertices of convex requests in a $(2r+1)$-dimensional vector space while approximately preserving movement costs up to a factor of $O(r)$, which establishes the lower bound.
☆ Combinatorial Minimum Cost Flow in Almost-Linear Time on Dense Graphs
Bernhard Haeupler, Yonggang Jiang, Thatchaphol Saranurak
We give a combinatorial algorithm that computes an exact minimum-cost flow in $n^{2+o(1)}$ time. This is the first combinatorial improvement since Goldberg-Tarjan [GT87], and is almost optimal in dense graphs. All previous faster algorithms, including the almost-linear-time algorithm of Chen et al. [CKL+22], rely on interior-point methods. Our main conceptual contribution is a black-box reduction from exact minimum-cost flow on general graphs to its approximation on DAGs, with only an $n^{o(1)}$ loss on the running time. This reduction allows future work on combinatorial minimum-cost flow to focus on DAGs, typically much easier to handle than general graphs. Our $n^{2+o(1)}$-time algorithm follows by combining this reduction with a simple augmenting-path-based approximation algorithm on DAGs. The reduction is based on a new structural result, called a flow-cost-preserving DAG projection. Given an arbitrary directed graph, this construction produces a DAG that approximately preserves both the congestion of flows and their cost up to a small loss.
comment: 101 pages
☆ Single-Machine Guarantees on Unrelated Machines and Beyond: Proportional Fairness for Nonclairvoyant Polytope Scheduling
Alexander Lindermayr, Sven Jäger, Bart Zondervan
The Polytope Scheduling Problem (PSP) is a general framework for preemptive scheduling in which feasible instantaneous job allocations form a packing polytope. In the nonclairvoyant online setting, Proportional Fairness (PF), a practical allocation rule rooted in market theory, is the leading general-purpose algorithm. On a single machine, PF coincides with weighted Round-Robin, which is $2$-competitive for weighted completion time with uniform release dates (optimal for nonclairvoyant algorithms) and $(2+\varepsilon)$-speed $O(1/\varepsilon)$-competitive for weighted flow time. Known guarantees beyond a single machine are weaker. This raises a central question: Are these heterogeneous environments intrinsically harder than a single machine under nonclairvoyance? We show that they are not: PF retains both single-machine guarantees on the novel class of support-submodular polytopes, which includes unrelated machines and polymatroids. On unrelated machines, this refutes a belief (JACM'18, SODA'25) that PF is not $O(1)$-speed $O(1)$-competitive for weighted flow time. Our main technical contribution is an equivalent characterization: a polytope is support submodular if and only if, for all job weights, the optimal weighted completion time is concave in the processing requirements. This structural insight underlies both guarantees. For general PSP, the picture changes: we give the first lower bound above $2$, showing that every randomized nonclairvoyant algorithm has a competitive ratio of at least $2+10^{-5}$ for weighted completion time, even with uniform release dates. On the positive side, we substantially improve the best known guarantee for weighted completion time from $27$ (SICOMP'26) to $4$ via a new dual-fitting analysis of PF. For weighted flow time, we show that PF achieves the first $O(1)$-speed $O(1)$-competitive nonclairvoyant guarantee for graph scheduling.

☆ Pareto-optimal Scheduling with Predictions
Ziyad Benomar, Vianney Perchet
We study preemptive single-machine scheduling with predictions, where a prediction of the order of the jobs (or of their sizes) is available, and the objective is to minimize the total completion time. An algorithm is $c$-consistent if it is $c$-competitive when the prediction is correct, and $R$-robust if it is $R$-competitive whatever the prediction. Since the introduction of this problem as one of the first examples of learning-augmented algorithms, the best possible trade-off between consistency and robustness has remained open. We determine it exactly for deterministic algorithms. For every $R>2$, we give an $R$-robust algorithm whose consistency is \[ c(R)=R\Big(1-\Big(1-\frac{R+2}{R^2}\Big)^{\frac R{R+2}}\Big), \] and we prove that no deterministic $R$-robust algorithm, with either predicted order or predicted job sizes, has better consistency. In particular, consistency $1+\eps$ is achievable with robustness $\eps^{-1/2}+\frac16+O(\sqrt\eps)$, whereas all previous algorithms required robustness $Θ(1/\eps)$. Our algorithm is greedy: it follows the predicted order as fast as possible, subject to the invariant that the cost paid so far is at most $R$ times the optimum of the instance in which every unfinished job would end immediately. The matching lower bound rests on the observation that every robust deterministic algorithm must satisfy the same invariant along its run on correct predictions.
☆ Tightening the Online Bin Packing Gap from Both Sides, and the Limits of the Current Methods
Karyamsetty Martin
The asymptotic competitive ratio of online bin packing has been bracketed since 2018 by the lower bound 1.54278 of Balogh, Bekesi, Dosa, Epstein and Levin and the upper bound 1.57829 of their Advanced Harmonic algorithm. We revisit both sides with exact rational computation and prove what the computations say. On the upper bound, we rebuild the certification of Advanced Harmonic as an exact optimization over its dual parameters; with the published parameters this already certifies a ratio strictly below the published one, and re-tuning the algorithm's proportion parameters gives a certified ratio of 1.5782147437 (an exact rational), independently re-verified on all 402 scenarios. To our knowledge this is the first movement of the upper bound since 2018. On the lower bound, we prove that the 2018 adversary construction is rigid: extending it by any finite number of new batches of ordinary size (outside the window of sizes s with 2s > 1 and rational part at most 0.64819), placed anywhere, with counts depending on the observed statistics, adaptive or not, never improves the bound at any truncation level. The load-bearing fact is that the construction is volume-tight at every stop. A single window-sized batch added to the truncation-3 tree is shown by machine-verified certificates to leave its value below the published limit. A computational map covers the remaining natural extensions: unions of structural variants, two-statistic multi-batch adversaries, and three families of adaptive conditions that the paper's separation theorem allows but never uses; none improves the bound. On the algorithm side we show that the marking, bonus-item and red-sand mechanisms of Extreme Harmonic bind nowhere in Advanced Harmonic, whose bottleneck is a two-medium-item bin that no known mechanism targets. We record two corrections to commonly cited numbers.

☆ Exact Algorithms and Complexity of the Smallest Grammar Problem
Xiangle Chen
The Smallest Grammar Problem (SGP) asks for a smallest straight-line grammar generating a given string, where size is the total length of the right-hand sides. Rules may be nested, so locally optimal choices of repeated fragments need not combine into an optimal grammar. We show that in an optimal grammar one can choose an occurrence of the expansion of each auxiliary nonterminal such that these intervals are pairwise disjoint. Hence the dictionary can be read off from a partition of the input, decoupling the choice of the dictionary from the parsing of rules. Using this property together with a bound on references to rules of length two, we obtain an exact algorithm running in $O^*(1.8723^n)$ time and $O(n)$ words of space, improving the $O^*(3^n)$ bound of Casel et al. for general grammars. For alphabet size $σ$ and $R=\log_σn$, we give a $2^{O((n/R)\log(R+1))}$-time algorithm, which is subexponential for fixed alphabets. Conversely, using an anchor-based projection from encoded grammars back to source grammars, we prove that SGP is NP-complete over a binary alphabet, answering a question of Casel et al., and construct instance families of tunable alphabet size on which no $2^{o(n/R)}n^{O(1)}$-time algorithm exists under ETH. Finally, for every $c>1$ we give a constant-degree integer polynomial formulation of SGP with $O(c^n)$ variables. By the barrier theorem of Belova, Golovnev, Kulikov, Mihajlin, and Sharipov, a proof that SGP is $λ^n$-SETH-hard under their deterministic fine-grained reductions would imply new lower bounds for Boolean series-parallel circuits or for arithmetic circuits.

☆ The Exact Robustness of Smith's Rule with Predicted Processing Times
Ohad Ayalon, Ilan Reuven Cohen
Smith's rule orders jobs by their weight-to-processing-time ratios. We study the robustness of its list schedule on identical parallel machines when job $j$ is ordered using a nominal processing time $μ_j$ and its true processing time satisfies $μ_j\leq p_j\leqαμ_j$, where $α\geq1$. We determine the exact worst-case approximation ratio against the clairvoyant optimum, taking the supremum over machine counts. The ratio is an explicit continuous piecewise-algebraic function of $α$. It equals the Kawaguchi--Kyan constant $(1+\sqrt{2})/2$ at $α=1$ and is exactly $\sqrtα$ for $α\geq4$. On one machine the exact ratio is $\sqrtα$. For every fixed machine count, no deterministic nonpreemptive algorithm can improve on this guarantee, even with knowledge of $α$. The proof reduces to a common nominal Smith ratio and endpoint errors. Cutting the list schedule at its minimum final machine load yields a prefix--suffix certificate; two packings of matching lower-bound instances explain the branches below $α=4$. We also prove a nominal-to-robust transfer: any fixed schedule ordered by nominal Smith ratios on each machine, with nominal approximation ratio $1+δ$, has realized ratio at most $\sqrtα+αδ$. A nominal approximation scheme then gives, for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm independent of $α$ with ratio $(1+\varepsilon)\sqrtα$ simultaneously for $1\leqα\leq4$.

☆ Beyond 3SUM-Hardness for Geometric Problems
Nick Fischer
Since the early days of fine-grained complexity, many geometric problems have been shown to require quadratic time under the 3SUM Hypothesis. But as the 3SUM Hypothesis was recently refuted, these lower bounds have lost their foundation. In this note we point out that many of them can be recovered under an alternative hardness assumption, the $k$-AP Hypothesis. The required changes to the proofs are trivial, so the lower bounds remain just as easy to teach.
☆ Subgraph Isomorphism, Integer Linear Programming, and Lattices: A Linear Algebraic Framework
Bryant Gipson
The subgraph isomorphism problem (SGI) asks a deceptively simple question: is a graph B found inside a parent graph A? Famously NP-complete and the subject of volumes of study, attempts to practically solve subgraph problems typically require specialized software or the focused application of problem-specific theoretical knowledge. Here we present a straightforward linear framework that neatly joins SGI to lattice theory and constrained satisfiability and, importantly, yields an easily implemented algorithm that naturally leverages consumer-grade hardware and off-the-shelf libraries to compete with state-of-the-art specialized solvers. We additionally show practical realizations in approachable settings, along with some benchmarks that reduce 6+ hour computations to seconds. Aimed at readability, we develop a series of computable algebraic invariants and demonstrate their application to familiar problems such as the Knight's Tour, with a focus on providing an accessible entry point for researchers in graph theory, abstract algebra, and students in general.

☆ Optimal Worst-Case Open Addressing: Braided Cuckoo Hashing
Andrew Krapivin, William Kuszmaul
In this paper, we introduce braided cuckoo hashing, an open-addressed hash table that supports load factor up to $1 - \varepsilon$ (for some parameter $\varepsilon \in (0, 1)$), while supporting queries, insertions, and deletions in worst-case time $O(\log \varepsilon^{-1})$, with high probability. This result represents an exponential improvement on insertion/deletion time over previous constructions, and disproves a conjecture of Bender, Kuszmaul, and Zhou that the product of worst-case query time and expected insertion/deletion time must be $Ω(\varepsilon^{-1})$.

♻ ☆ The Price of Universal Temporal Reachability
Binh-Minh Bui-Xuan, Nhat-Minh Nguyen, Mélissa Setbel, Sébastien Tixeuil, Yukiko Yamauchi
Dynamic networks are graphs in which edges are available only at specific time instants, modeling connections that change over time. The dynamic network creation game studies this setting as a strategic interaction where a set of $n$ vertices represents players. Players can add or remove time-labelled edges in order to minimize their personal cost. This cost has two components: a construction cost, calculated as the number of time instants during which a player maintains edges multiplied by a constant $α$, and a communication cost, defined as the sum of temporal distances to all other vertices in the network. Communication occurs through temporal paths, which are sequences of adjacent edges with strictly increasing time labels and no repeated vertices. For the strict temporal shortest distance, we show a constant bound for the price of anarchy for $α<2$. When $α\geq\frac{1}{8}n^2$, we show Nash equilibria with a communication cost asymptotically higher than $\frac{1}{49}n^3$. We prove that the game admits a best response cycle: there exists a sequence of individually rational strategy changes that returns to the initial strategy profile without ever reaching a Nash equilibrium. This establishes that the dynamic network creation game is not a potential game and that best response dynamics may cycle indefinitely, so convergence to a Nash equilibrium or a socially optimal outcome is not guaranteed in general.

♻ ☆ Exploration of Always $S$-Connected Temporal Graphs
Duncan Adamson, Paul G Spirakis
\emph{Temporal graphs} are a generalisation of (static) graphs, defined by a sequence of \emph{snapshots}, each a static graph defined over a common set of vertices. \emph{Exploration} problems are one of the most fundamental and most heavily studied problems on temporal graphs, asking if a set of $m$ agents can visit every vertex in the graph, with each agent only allowed to traverse a single edge per snapshot. In this paper, we introduce and study \emph{always $S$-connected} temporal graphs, a generalisation of always-connected temporal graphs where, rather than forming a single connected component in each snapshot, we have at most $\vert S \vert$ components, each defined by the connection to at least one vertex in the set $S$. We use the model of always $S$-connected temporal graphs to study \emph{subgraphs} of always-connected temporal graphs, with motivation coming from networks with a small number of ``choke-points'', such as rail networks, where all external traffic into a subnetwork comes from a limited number of external connections. We use this formulation as a tool for exploring always-connected temporal graphs admitting an \emph{$(r,b)$-division}, a partitioning of the vertex set into $r$ components, each of which is $S$-connected, where $\vert S \vert \leq b$. We show that an always $S$-connected temporal graph with $m = \vert S \vert$ and an average degree of $Δ$ can be explored by $m$ agents in $O(n^{3/2} m^2 Δ^{3/2}\log^{3/2}(n) + + nm^3)$ snapshots. Using this result as a subroutine, we show that any always-connected temporal graph with treewidth at most $k$ can be explored by a single agent in $O\left(n^{4/3} k^{9/2}\log^{5/2}(n)\right)$ snapshots, improving on the current state-of-the-art for small values of $k$.
♻ ☆ Dynamic $(Δ+1)$-Coloring Against an Adaptive Adversary: Faster, Simpler, Worst-Case, and Bounded Recourse
Yuval Gil, Magnús M. Halldórsson
We study fully dynamic $(Δ+1)$-coloring against an adaptive adversary. Behnezhad, Rajaraman, and Wasim (SODA 2025) gave the first algorithm with $\tilde O(n^{8/9})$ amortized update time, later improved to $\tilde O(n^{2/3})$ by Flin and Halldórsson (ICALP 2025). We give a fully dynamic $(Δ+1)$-coloring algorithm with $\tilde O(\sqrt n)$ \textbf{worst-case} update time. Unlike previous algorithms, it avoids sparse-dense decompositions and relies only on elementary data structures, resulting in a short self-contained algorithm and analysis. Moreover, it avoids global recoloring, resulting in a worst-case recourse of $3$.

☆ Past as State, Present as Attention: Persistent-State Blockwise Flow Matching for Long-Horizon Co-Speech Motion Generation NeurIPS 2026
Yi Liu, Xiangyue Zhang, Jianfang Li, Jia Ma, Yisheng He, Jianqiang Ren
Co-speech motion generation remains challenging over long horizons due to the need for temporal coherence and consistent style. Existing methods typically generate long motions segment by segment, either conditioning on a short seed of recent frames, which gradually drifts, or retaining ever-longer explicit histories, which are costly to maintain and hard to learn from limited data. We trace these limitations to a key asymmetry between past and present: past motion carries slowly varying style that should be compactly summarized, whereas the current segment exhibits fast-changing local dynamics that require fine-grained modeling. This asymmetry motivates a simple Past-As-State, Present-as-Attention principle for long-horizon co-speech motion generation. Following this principle, we propose PASPA, which summarizes past history with an inter-block persistent state and models the current segment with intra-block bidirectional self-attention. We instantiate PASPA within a blockwise flow matching framework, with multi-block supervision for effective training over variable histories, prefill-decode state caching for efficient rollout, and hybrid classifier-free guidance for enhanced history and speech conditioning. Extensive public-benchmark experiments show that PASPA achieves state-of-the-art FGD performance and preserves motion style across long-sequence generation.

☆ RADIANT: Object-Centric Feed-Forward Global Illumination
Sarah Zhang, Zhiyang Dou, Minghao Guo, Benjamin Tod Jones, Wojciech Matusik
Feed-forward neural renderers synthesize global illumination across novel 3D scenes without per-scene optimization or runtime ray tracing. Existing transformer-based renderers tokenize individual triangles, so self-attention scales quadratically with mesh resolution. Yet macroscopic radiative transport is governed by the underlying surface geometry and object arrangements rather than a particular mesh discretization; finer tessellation can thus inflate token counts without changing the underlying transport physics, motivating a discretization-agnostic and object-centric representation. We introduce RADIANT (RADiance-based Inter-Actor Neural Transformer), an object-centric architecture for feed-forward global illumination from point inputs. By representing each object with a compact budget of 32 surface-patch tokens, RADIANT decouples attention sequence length from polygon counts. In a single network, an inter-object scene transformer models 3D radiative exchange and a view decoder renders images at target camera poses. To capture light transport without materializing O(N^2) bias matrices, invariant form-factor attention embeds geometric distances and surface orientations directly into query-key dot products as SE(3)-invariant inner products weighted by learned area and material gains. For primary visibility without mesh connectivity, camera rays cross-attend scene tokens guided by depth-tested point splats in a shared spherical frame. Across a 1.03M-scene benchmark under matched model capacity, RADIANT reaches 25 dB tone-mapped PSNR in 5x fewer training steps than RenderFormer, and achieves 32.2 dB at 600k steps (+3.3 dB over RenderFormer and +7.6 dB over RenderFormer-V2). We further demonstrate the scalability of RADIANT, which completes a 512x512 forward pass of a 500-object scene in 0.83 s, with computational cost scaling with the number of objects rather than polygons.
♻ ☆ Velocity-Space 3D Asset Editing NeurIPS 2026
Hao Liu, Yuxuan Lin, Jingfeng Guo, Ruihang Chu, Junjie Wang, Ruotong Li, Yujiu Yang
Local 3D asset editing requires modifying a target region while preserving the rest. Existing methods enforce locality through mechanisms external to the generator, such as manual 3D masks, post-hoc voxel merging, or 2D multi-view lifting. None intervene where the corruption originates: inside the ODE sampler. For faithful local editing, a rectified-flow generator's velocity field should be strong over the target region while vanishing on preserved content. Yet a single velocity field can hardly satisfy both requirements, leading to three problems: (i) identity leakage that keeps the edit signal non-zero on preserved regions; (ii) no dedicated edit-amplification channel, so strengthening the edit perturbs identity; and (iii) identity drag at the geometry and material stages, where a global condition pulls every token toward the target. We propose VS3D (Velocity-Space 3D Asset editing), an inversion-free, training-free, and mask-free framework that derives edit and preserve signals by comparing the frozen model's source- and target-conditioned responses and realizes this principle through three stage-specific modules. Reconstruction-Anchored Source Injection (RASI) absorbs identity leakage by turning the unconditional embedding into a per-step, asset-specific anchor calibrated through source reconstruction. Partial-Mean Guidance (PMG) amplifies the edit signal by contrasting high- and low-quality subsample estimates of the velocity difference, active only where a consistent edit exists. Twin-Agreement Residual Injection (TAR) lets the sampler decide token by token what to preserve at the geometry and material stages. Experiments on diverse 3D assets show that VS3D outperforms state-of-the-art native-3D and 2D-lifted editors, demonstrating the effectiveness of velocity-space editing for training-free 3D asset editing. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/LHaoooo/VS3D.
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript with additional experiments and supplementary material
♻ ☆ World from Motion: Generative Dynamic Gaussian Reconstruction from Monocular Video NeurIPS 2026
Liyuan Zhu, Shengyu Huang, Amrita Mazumdar, Tianye Li, Zan Gojcic, Gordon Wetzstein, Iro Armeni, Shalini De Mello, Alex Trevithick
We present World from Motion, a method for generating freely renderable dynamic 3D Gaussian representations from monocular videos. Our approach conditions a video model on dense, pixel-aligned renderings that encode appearance, geometry, and 3D scene motion along both input and target camera trajectories to correct rendering artifacts and fill in missing regions from an initial reconstruction. To train this model, we construct a dataset of aligned multiview video pairs and dynamic 3DGS representations, with simulated artifacts characteristic of monocular reconstruction. At test time, we distill the model's generations, including newly observed regions and motions, back into a single consistent, high-quality dynamic 3DGS, improving both novel-view synthesis and the underlying 3D motion. Our method sets a new state of the art in 4D reconstruction and seamlessly generalizes to in-the-wild videos with large viewpoint changes and dynamic motions.

♻ ☆ MeshCone: Second-Order Cone Programming for Geometrically-Constrained Mesh Enhancement
Alexander Valverde
Modern mesh generation pipelines whether learning-based or classical often produce outputs requiring post-processing to achieve production-quality geometry. This work introduces MeshCone, a convex optimization framework for guided mesh refinement that leverages reference geometry to correct deformed or degraded meshes. We formulate the problem as a second-order cone program where vertex positions are optimized to align with target geometry while enforcing smoothness through convex edge-length regularization. MeshCone performs geometry-aware optimization that preserves fine details while correcting structural defects. We demonstrate robust performance across 56 diverse object categories from ShapeNet and ThreeDScans, achieving superior refinement quality compared to Laplacian smoothing and unoptimized baselines while maintaining sub-second inference times. MeshCone is particularly suited for applications where reference geometry is available, such as mesh-from-template workflows, scan-to-CAD alignment, and quality assurance in asset production pipelines.
